Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                                                                  Aleph Alpha

                       Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier

                                                                      Aleph Alpha

                                                                                              Abstract

                   This report introduces Kolibri, an English–German Mixture-of-Experts transformer with 78.1B total parameters and 3.46B
                   active parameters per token, released as open weights under the Apache 2.0 licence. We built Kolibri for sovereign and
                   specialised deployment, with a particular focus on German and on regulated domains such as public administration, industry,
                   and aerospace. To support these settings, we control the full model-development process, including data, architecture,
                   training infrastructure, post-training, and evaluation.
                   We train Kolibri on 24T tokens across pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension, with German accounting for
                   more than 20 % of the data mix. To reach this share, we complement German datasets with more than 2T German tokens that
                   we curate from the web with our own pipeline and generate synthetically. Post-training combines supervised fine-tuning
                   on curated German and English data with reinforcement learning on more than 1.2M internally curated tasks, which cover
                   reasoning, tool use, instruction following, code, and retrieval. The model activates 4.4 % of its parameters per token, and
                   among the open models evaluated in this report, it lies on the Pareto frontier of quality and serving cost in English and German.
                   Kolibri builds on Kolibri Origin, an earlier iteration that validated our full pipeline at scale. Both models come from our Model
                   Factory, which connects data, experimentation, training, evaluation, and deployment through version-controlled workflows1.

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

Part 1

Introduction

Organisations in public administration, industry, and aerospace process sensitive data under regulation.
For these domains, a language model has to run on infrastructure that the organisation controls, rest on
training in line with European values, and serve German as well as English. In addition, the serving cost of
the model limits the scale at which an organisation can deploy it.

Kolibri, which we built for these requirements, is an English–German MoE transformer with 78.1B total
parameters, of which 3.46B, or 4.4 %, are active per token. The Apache 2.0 licence of the open weights
permits the deploying organisation to operate the model on its own infrastructure. Among the open
models we evaluate, Kolibri lies on the Pareto frontier of quality and serving cost in English and German,
both as a base and as a post-trained model (Figure 1). Figure 2 shows that Kolibri scores higher than three
open models on most benchmarks across Agentic, Industry, Math, Code and General Knowledge. The
model processes contexts of up to 1M tokens, and users set a reasoning effort per request to trade cost
and latency against answer quality.

We designed the development pipeline of Kolibri with applicable regulation, such as the EU AI Act, its
General-Purpose AI Code of Practice and the General Data Protection Regulation in mind from the ground
up. Our data pipeline filters against illegal, harmful, and pirated content and redacts personal data from
data sources. In addition, we align the answers of the model with the values of the democratic consensus
in Europe and train the model to abstain when the provided context does not support an answer.

Teams in Germany developed Kolibri end to end and train it on infrastructure in Germany and Finland.
Training spans 24T tokens across pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension, with a German
share of more than 20 % and sequences of up to 256k tokens in the final stage. To reach this share, we
complement German datasets with more than 2T German tokens that we curate from the web with our
own pipeline and generate synthetically. Post-training combines SFT on curated German and English data
with RL on more than 1.2M internally curated tasks, which cover reasoning, tool use, instruction following,
code, and retrieval. The predecessor of Kolibri, Kolibri Origin, is an internal model that we used to validate
the full development pipeline at scale. From Kolibri Origin to Kolibri, we scaled the data, the model size, the
context length, and post-training, and we refined the architecture and the training recipe (Table 1).

The entire pipeline, from data curation through pre- and post-training to evaluation, runs in our Model
Factory, which comprises the infrastructure, tooling, and development processes that connect data,
experimentation, training, evaluation, and deployment. The Model Factory expresses these workflows as

version-controlled code, which makes every experiment reproducible, composable, and traceable. A new
model generation, an additional language, or a further specialisation thus becomes a change to this code.

The remainder of this report follows the development of Kolibri. Part 2 describes the architecture, the
optimisation, the data, and the stages of pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension, together
with the experiments behind the design choices, and evaluates the base model. Post-training follows in
Part 3, which covers SFT and RL and evaluates the post-trained model. Part 4 describes the Model Factory,
and Part 5 summarises the results. The appendices in Part 6 give the details of the individual stages and
the quality-cost analysis.

Part 2

Pre-Training

This part covers the three stages that produce the Kolibri base model: pre-training, mid-training and
long-context extension.
After pre-training for 20T tokens at a 16k context length on data from nine domains (Section 2.3), mid-
training continues for 3.4T tokens at a context length of 64k tokens, on a mix drawn mostly from curated
datasets rather than from web text (Section 2.3.3). The long-context extension then adds 200B training
tokens at 256k token context length (Section 2.3.4). Figure 3 shows the training loss throughout pre-
training.
Every stage was scaled with respect to Kolibri Origin, which pre-trained on 7.5T tokens at a context of 4k
tokens, mid-trained on 122B tokens at 8k and extended the context on 96B tokens at 64k. Kolibri trains
on about three times as many tokens in total, at four times the maximum context. Mid-training grows the
most, by a factor of about 28.
We start by describing the model architecture and how we run the pre-training in Sections 2.1 and 2.1.6,
followed by our specific optimisation settings in Section 2.2. Section 2.3 describes the pre-training data: the
Common Crawl pipeline, the mixing of the data sources, PII redaction and decontamination. Sections 2.3.3

through an RMSNorm and write back through a second one. Orange marks what only the 40 SWA blocks have: RoPE on queries and keys,

benchmark scores of the resulting base model.

2.1 Model Architecture

Kolibri is a decoder-only causal Transformer (Vaswani et al. 2017) with hybrid attention, which interleaves
SWA and full-attention blocks, and sparse MoE layers of FFNs (Shazeer et al. 2017). It has 78.1B total
parameters and 3.46B active parameters per token. Therefore, each token uses 4.4 % of the parameters,
keeping the computation per token small at a large total capacity (Section 2.1.4). Figure 4 shows one block
and Table 2 lists its dimensions. Each block uses sandwich normalisation (M. Ding et al. 2021; V. Singh et al.

                                                            7 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                       Aleph Alpha

Property                                                    Kolibri                Kolibri Origin

Transformer blocks                                          50                     50
Leading dense layers                                        0                      2
Full attention / SWA blocks                                 10 / 40                50 / 0
SWA window size                                             512 preceding tokens   —
Model width                                                 2560                   2048
Vocabulary                                                  128 000                96 000
Attention heads / KV heads                                  48 / 4                 32 / 4
Attention head dimension                                    128                    128
Dense FFN hidden dimension                                  —                      6144
Routed experts per MoE layer                                384                    128
Experts per token                                           6 routed and 1 shared  8 routed and 1 shared
Routed-expert hidden dimension                              512                    768
Total parameters                                            78.1B                  30.6B
Active parameters per token                                 3.46B                  3.27B
Trained maximum context                                     256k tokens            64k tokens

Table 2. Architecture of Kolibri and, for comparison, Kolibri Origin. Active parameters exclude the embeddings.

2026), applying RMS normalisation (B. Zhang and Sennrich 2019) before and after both the attention and
MoE layers.

The tokeniser vocabulary of 128 000 tokens is trained with UniBPE (Section 2.1.2), which builds merges
bottom-up like BPE but selects each merge by Unigram loss. Compared with the GPT-5 tokeniser, it places
more token boundaries on morpheme boundaries, in German 65 % against 47 %, and needs 11.2 % fewer
tokens on German web text.

Attention uses GQA (Ainslie et al. 2023) with 48 query heads, 4 KV heads and head dimension 128. The
blocks combine SWA and full attention at a 4:1 ratio: every fifth block uses full attention, and the other
blocks use SWA (Beltagy, Peters, and Cohan 2020) over the 512 preceding tokens. Positional information
is injected only in the SWA blocks using RoPE (Jianlin Su et al. 2024) with θ = 10 000. The full-attention
blocks use no positional encoding. Queries and keys are normalised per head with RMS normalisation
(Henry et al. 2020) before RoPE. Section 2.1.3 compares the hybrid attention pattern with full attention,
selects the window size and gives the reasons for the positional encoding and QK normalisation.

Every MoE layer routes each token to 6 of 384 routed experts and also evaluates one shared expert (Dai
et al. 2024). The experts are SiLU-gated FFNs (Shazeer 2020). The total parameter count follows from the
sparsity sweep of Section 2.1.4. The routing scores are sigmoids of the router logits (DeepSeek-AI 2024b),
but are not renormalised after top-k selection. Expert loads are balanced with Exact Quantile Balancing
(EQB) and Load-Error Injection (LEI) (Neitemeier et al. 2026) (Section 2.1.5).

Kolibri Origin has a simpler architecture (Table 2), in which every block uses full attention with RoPE, the
first two blocks are dense, and each MoE layer routes a token to 8 of 128 experts. It is less sparse, with
10.7 % of its parameters active per token against 4.4 % for Kolibri. Its expert loads are balanced with the
combination of Soft-Clamped Momentum Expert Bias Updates (SMEBU) (V. Singh et al. 2026) expert-bias
updates and a GShard-style auxiliary loss (Lepikhin et al. 2021).

                                                            8 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                             Aleph Alpha

2.1.1 Ablation Methodology

We compare recipe choices through controlled proxy-scale ablations before validating at larger scale.
Each study specifies its resource-matching criteria, training budget and evaluation stage, with checkpoints
compared at a common training horizon.

Proxy Models We run ablations on two smaller proxy models: versions of Kolibri which share the main
architectural choices but fewer parameters (Table 3). Both proxy models differ in width and have fewer
routed experts per layer at a lower dimension compared to the target Kolibri. The proxy at scale M has
0.6B active out of 7.8B total parameters trained on 97.6B tokens. The proxy at scale L has 2.1B active out of
30.6B total parameters trained on 327.6B tokens.

Evaluation We evaluate every candidate change along three axes: modelling quality, stability in the
attention and MoE layers, and efficiency, both in training and decode during inference. To assess modelling
quality, we use validation loss and a suite of benchmarks, which are described in Section 2.4.1. Because
we broadened the evaluation suite as the model was developed, aggregates are not directly comparable
across studies. To ablate the impact on long-context performance, some studies applied a shortened
long-context phase directly after pre-training.

Learning-Rate Selection Architectural changes can shift the optimal learning rate, so using only the
scaling-law prediction can confound shape quality with a suboptimal learning rate. For model-shape

                                                                                                   √√
sweeps, we tune learning rates at the smallest ablation scale using η ∈ {η0/ 2, η0, 2η0}, where η0 is the
predicted learning rate.

2.1.2 Tokeniser

We designed the Kolibri tokeniser to capture the specificity of the German language, without compromising
on English efficiency and downstream model performance.

                                                            9 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                Aleph Alpha

The model uses a vocabulary of 128 000 tokens, up from 96 000 tokens used by Kolibri Origin. We train
both Kolibri tokenisers with UniBPE, a new hybrid tokeniser training method: it keeps the greedy bottom-up
approach of BPE (Gage 1994; Sennrich, Haddow, and Birch 2016) and scores merges with the Unigram
loss objective (Kudo 2018).

UniBPE Merge Score A tokeniser state assigns every token t a count ct over the training corpus, with
      ∑︁

N = t ct tokens in total. The Unigram model puts probability ct/N on t, and the negative log-likelihood
of the corpus under it is

                       L = − ct log = h(N ) − h(ct), h(x) = x log x, h(0) = 0. ∑︂ ct ∑︂ (1)
                                  tN t

This is the quantity Unigram tokenisers minimise top-down by pruning tokens. UniBPE minimises it bottom-

up: at each step it applies the merge that lowers L the most, and otherwise runs exactly like BPE, producing

an ordinary vocabulary and merge list.

Merging the pair (a, b) into a new token ab, where the pair occurs cab times, moves cab counts out of a
and out of b, creates a token with count cab, and shortens the corpus by cab tokens. Every other count is
untouched, so for a̸ = b the change of the loss

∆L = h(ca) − h(ca − cab) + h(cb) − h(cb − cab) − h(cab) + h(N − cab) − h(N )                              (2)
⏞  ⏟⏟           ⏞⏞                                          ⏟⏟           ⏞ ⏞ ⏟⏟ ⏞ ⏞    ⏟⏟              ⏞

   a loses cab                                              b loses cab  new ab token  shorter corpus

is closed-form. BPE ranks candidates by cab alone; UniBPE ranks them by ∆L, the most negative first. Both
only need the pair count and the two parent counts, so no new information is required over BPE.

Training We train the tokeniser on a shuffled 100 GB slice of the pre-training dataset (Section 2.3) without
Unicode normalisation. For pre-tokenisation we adapt the GPT4 pre-tokeniser (OpenAI 2023), changing
the regex to split every digit into its own token. The tokeniser has byte fallback, starting with 256 byte
tokens. The next 120 000 − 256 = 119 744 merges use the UniBPE algorithm, standard BPE performs the
next 7900 merges, and we then add 100 reserved and special tokens, for a total vocabulary of 128 000
tokens. The second stage of standard BPE has two purposes. First, some useful merges are difficult under
UniBPE, such as .. , preventing ... from being a single token. Second, Kolibri Origin used a pure UniBPE
algorithm, leading to degraded compression. This hybrid approach matches the compression of standard
BPE in our experiments.

Compression On German text, the Kolibri tokeniser reaches 4.90 bytes per token, the highest compres-
sion across twelve evaluated tokenisers in Figure 5, with the best of the nine other tokenisers being 4.35
for GPT-5, and is on par with BPE trained on the same pre-training corpus. On English, Kolibri reaches
4.58 bytes per token, against 4.67 for GPT-5, the best tokeniser on English. This lowers the cost of training
on and serving German text, and leads to shorter context lengths in long-context tasks while staying
competitive in English.

Morphology UniBPE consistently improves on morphological alignment compared to plain BPE. In
the first 60 merges each performs on the pre-training dataset, UniBPE creates the units of three or

2.1.3 Hybrid Sliding-Window Attention

One target of Kolibri is to improve long-context inference efficiency compared to Kolibri Origin which
uses full attention in every block: each token attends to all preceding tokens, so the compute grows
quadratically with the sequence length in all layers. During inference, the KV cache grows linearly with
the sequence length, leading to increased memory usage and computational cost. A mitigation is to
use a hybrid of full attention and SWA (Beltagy, Peters, and Cohan 2020) layers, which restrict attention
to a fixed-size window of preceding tokens, reducing both memory usage and computational cost. We
integrate SWA into Kolibri, increasing long-context inference efficiency and benchmark performance.

Hybrid Attention Kolibri uses full attention in every fifth block (10 of 50) and SWA in the other 40. First
experiments at proxy scale M (Table 3) showed improvements in long-context tasks without a loss on
pre-training benchmarks (Figure 7). At matched training compute, the hybrid model supports sequences
four times as long as the full-attention model. Moreover, Figure 9 (right) shows that the hybrid model is
generally more efficient during inference.

Positional Encoding The SWA blocks apply RoPE (Jianlin Su et al. 2024) with base θ = 10 000 to queries
and keys; the full-attention blocks use No Positional Encoding (NoPE). In early experiments, removing RoPE
from the full-attention blocks improved long-context performance, in line with B. Yang et al. (2025).

QK Normalisation Every block normalises queries and keys per head with RMS normalisation before
RoPE (Henry et al. 2020; B. Zhang and Sennrich 2019) which bounds the attention logits and keeps training
stable at higher learning rates. We refer to Appendix B.2 for an analysis of QK normalisation effects. During
RL, assuming sufficiently small gain parameters, the QK normalisation also bounds every query and key
element below the FP8 range, so the FP8 KV cache cannot overflow (Appendix I.4.1).

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                         Aleph Alpha

2.1.3.1 Window Size

We select the window size w∗ ∈ {128, 256, . . . , 4096} by running ablations at scale M and validating the
best two variants at scale L (Table 3).

After the pre-training stage, we add a long-context stage of 5B tokens on sequences of 256k tokens. We
evaluate the pre-training benchmarks at the last pre-training checkpoint and the long-context benchmarks
after the long-context stage. Across windows, we approximately match total FLOPs, KV-cache size and
parameter count (Table 4).

Ablations at Scale M We select the window for long-context score and inference efficiency, and also
compare the scores on the pre-training benchmarks.

Table 4 also reports the average accuracy and BPB of our pre-training benchmarks at the last pre-training
checkpoint. While the scores are generally similar across window sizes, the configuration with window size
256 achieves the highest scores.

Figure 8 shows the pooled long-context score for each window size configuration depending on the
context length. At scale M, w = 128 yields the highest pooled score and leads from 8k to 64k tokens, but
degrades at context length 128k. w = 512 follows closely and degrades the least with context length.

Validation at Scale L Based on the ablations at scale M, we validate w = 128 and w = 512 at scale L
(Table 3). Figure 8 also reports the pooled long-context score for these models. Their average accuracies
on the pre-training benchmarks are 48.0 % for w = 128 and 47.8 % for w = 512, while their pooled long-
context scores are 76.2 % and 77.1 %. Through 128k tokens, the two windows are generally close, with the
largest absolute difference being 3.1 percentage points. At context length 256k, however, w = 512 scores
6.7 percentage points higher than w = 128.

We therefore use a sliding window of w = 512 in Kolibri, at the cost of 0.2 pp of accuracy on the pre-training
benchmarks.

2.1.4 Scaling Sparsity

Sparse MoE architectures are able to increase model capacity at matched compute by routing each token
through a subset of experts. We measure their sparsity as the share of the parameters that is active for
a token. At fixed expert width and routing top-k, adding experts increases the total parameter count
while leaving the number of FLOPs per token approximately unchanged. A larger total parameter count
increases parameter storage, communication volume and optimiser states, and makes expert balance
harder to maintain. Downstream performance, however, improves substantially with total capacity (A. Clark
et al. 2022; Frantar et al. 2024; Ludziejewski et al. 2024; Abnar et al. 2025; Tian, K. Chen, et al. 2025; Kimi
Team 2025), a trend we confirm in our own ablations (Figure 9). We compare configurations of different
sparsity by their quality, inference efficiency and deployment cost.

Quality We vary sparsity by increasing the number of routed experts from 128 to 256 and 384 at 3.4B
active parameters, which gives 42.1B, 82.3B and 122.6B total parameters.

Moving from 42.1B to 82.3B improves loss and aggregate eval accuracy (Figure 9). The 122.6B configuration
improves loss and aggregate eval accuracy only slightly further. The load balancing of Section 2.1.5 keeps
the expert loads well balanced in all three configurations, including the 122.6B one. A preliminary sweep at
0.9B active parameters shows the same ordering in loss and accuracy. Appendix B.4 shows the loss and
accuracy of both sweeps and the deployment footprint of each configuration.

Inference Efficiency We focus on the inference regime that is most interesting for the RL phase, where
most of the runtime is spent decoding rollouts. We therefore compare the configurations by their decode
throughput: the tokens generated per second, summed over all concurrent requests. Since the decode
phase is mainly memory-bound, increasing total parameters can increase inference cost through greater
parameter storage and weight movement, as well as additional communication overhead. To capture these
effects, we measure this throughput for the three configurations with 3.4B active parameters on 8 H100

Figure 9. Accuracy mean during pre-training (left) and maximum pure-decode throughput by live context length (right) of three configurations
with 3.4B active parameters and different total parameters. Accuracy averages the 17 benchmarks of Appendix B.3. Throughput is measured
on eight H100 SXM5 GPUs in BF16, with the best tested parallel configuration for each model and context length. Kolibri Origin is shown for
reference over its supported context range.

SXM5 GPUs in BF16, using for each model and context length the best tensor-, data- and expert-parallel
configuration identified through a combinatorial search (Figure 9).

The main sources of memory traffic are the active model weights and the KV cache. Weight reads increase
with the total parameter count, although they can be amortised across tokens routed to the same experts
within a large batch. KV-cache reads, by contrast, remain specific to each request and grow with its
live context. At 4K context length, where weight reads account for a larger share of memory traffic, the
82.3B and 122.6B configurations are 13 % and 32 % slower than the 42.1B configuration. At 256k, the gaps
narrow to 4 % and 12 % as KV-cache reads account for a larger share of total memory traffic. Most of this
long-context traffic comes from the 10 full-attention layers, while the 40 sliding-window layers read a fixed
window and contribute negligibly.

Deployment Footprint In resource-constrained deployments, total parameter count directly reduces
the memory available for activations and KV cache, limiting request concurrency at long context lengths
(Table 31). For example, on two H100s, the 122.6B configuration fits only 3 concurrent 256k-token requests,
compared with 18 for 82.3B.

The 82.3B configuration improves all reported quality metrics over 42.1B, while increasing capacity to 122.6B
yields only a small further improvement, reduces decode throughput and fits far fewer long requests. We
therefore target 80B total parameters at 3.4B active parameters. With the total size fixed, further ablations
set the number and width of the experts and the other shape parameters. Appendix B.5.1 compares three
expert configurations at this size. Informed by a preliminary sweep at 0.9B active parameters, we choose
to widen the residual stream (Appendix B.5.2).

                                                            15 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                Aleph Alpha

2.1.5 MoE Load Balancing

An MoE layer combines a shared expert, evaluated for every token, with E routed experts, of which only
K are selected per token. For T tokens and E routed experts, each MoE layer computes router logits
Z ∈ RT ×E. Expert biases β ∈ RE are added to the router logits Z to produce the biased logits B used for
balanced expert selection:

B = Z + 1T β⊤, It = Top-K(Bt,:, K), St,e = sigmoid(Zt,e).                 (3)

Here Bt,: denotes the row of B for token t, and S denotes the routing scores that weight the outputs of the

selected experts; 1T is the all-ones column vector, and Top-K(x, K) returns the indices of the K largest

entries of x. For an input representation xt, the layer output is

                                                            ∑︂            (4)

                      yt = fshared(xt) + St,efe(xt),

                                                            e∈It

where fshared and fe are the shared and routed expert networks. The selected scores are not renormalised,
and the biases affect selection only; the mixture weights use the unbiased sigmoid scores.

Load balancing ensures sufficient learning signal for every expert and efficient expert-parallel execution.

The former requires balance across the global optimiser-step batch; the latter requires balance within

each local microbatch. For either batch B, we measure the maximum relative excess load by MaxVio

(DeepSeek-AI 2024b):                                        [︃        ]︃

                      MaxVio(B) = max ce(B) − 1 , (5)
                                                            e K|B|/E

where ce(B) counts the tokens assigned to expert e.

Balancing methods include auxiliary losses (Lepikhin et al. 2021) and expert-bias updates, either with a
fixed step (DeepSeek-AI 2024b) or based on quantiles (Kimi Team 2026b).

Kolibri combines Exact Quantile Balancing (EQB) for global balance with Load-Error Injection (LEI) for local
balance (Neitemeier et al. 2026). EQB maintains stable global load balance without a tunable balancing
hyperparameter. The LEI coefficient therefore primarily controls local balance and training efficiency,
giving the recipe fewer and less sensitive hyperparameter choices than the recipe of Kolibri Origin, which
combines SMEBU bias updates (V. Singh et al. 2026) with a GShard-style auxiliary loss (Lepikhin et al. 2021).
Over the Kolibri pre-training run, the global MaxVio averaged over layers stays at 0.31. Only the first two
layers, whose routing the bias overrides (Section 2.1.5.3), reach 2.39 on average.

2.1.5.1 Exact Quantile Balancing (EQB)

Quantile Balancing (QB) (Kimi Team 2026b) computes token biases α ∈ RT and expert biases β through
alternating quantile updates:

                           αt ← −QK(︁Zt,: + β⊤)︁, βe ← −Qr(︁Z:,e + α)︁, r = T K , (6)
                                                                                                         E

where Qm denotes the (m + 1)th largest entry. Subtracting this cutoff leaves m positive entries, so the
token bias update targets K assignments per token and the expert bias update r assignments per expert.

                                                                                       16 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier              Aleph Alpha

Training performs one token-bias update followed by one expert-bias update per optimiser step. The
resulting expert biases β are centred by subtracting their mean and we use the top-K of the biased logits
for routing. Using top-K per token approximates the token biases computed by the first step of the QB
iteration during training and inference.

The global batch is partitioned across data-parallel ranks and gradient-accumulation microbatches. Each
rank holds the router scores Zt,: for all experts for its local tokens, together with the shared expert biases β.
The token bias αt can therefore be computed locally from Zt,: + β⊤. Updating an expert bias βe, however,
requires a quantile of Z:,e + α across all tokens in the global batch. These values are distributed across
ranks and microbatches, so their statistics must be combined.

Marin (Dial 2026) averages quantiles computed separately on each rank, which generally differs from the
quantile of the combined batch. Kimi (Kimi Team 2026b) accumulates a 1000-bin histogram per expert
over local microbatches and sums the counts across ranks with an all-reduce. The resulting histogram
represents the global batch, but its quantile estimate is limited by the bin width.

EQB (Neitemeier et al. 2026) computes this global quantile exactly for bfloat16 adjusted logits using two-
pass radix selection (Yifei Li et al. 2024). Each rank encodes its local values as order-preserving 16 bit keys.
In the first pass, we build 256-bin histograms over the high byte and sum the counts across microbatches
to locate the bin containing the global quantile. In the second pass, we repeat this for the histogram of the
low byte of values in that bin to recover the exact quantile. Each layer requires two all-reduces of 256E
int32 counts per optimiser step, independent of T .

2.1.5.2 Load-Error Injection (LEI)

A shared global bias cannot balance all microbatches individually. A GShard-style auxiliary loss is a common
choice for local balancing, originally formulated for normalised softmax probabilities (Lepikhin et al. 2021).
With sigmoid scores, models such as DeepSeek-V3 and Arcee Trinity add a separate normalisation for
this loss (DeepSeek-AI 2024b; V. Singh et al. 2026). We introduce LEI to balance each microbatch, which
directly injects each expert’s deviation from its target into the score gradients. Neitemeier et al. (2026,
App. D) derive the LEI update as the straight-through estimate for unnormalised sigmoid scores, while the
GShard loss is the corresponding estimate for normalised softmax probabilities. For a local microbatch Bl,
with ce(Bl) assignments to expert e, the relative load and relative load error are

                                                fe = ce(Bl) , ρe = fe − 1. (7)
                                                       K |Bl |/E

We inject a rescaled version of the load-error vector into the sigmoid-score gradient of every token in the

microbatch:  Gt,e ← Gt,e + λ tanh ξ ρe, ξ = ∥ρ∥∞ , t ∈ Bl. (8)

                                    ξ                                τ

Here Gt,e is the incoming gradient with respect to St,e, λ > 0 controls the correction strength, and τ > 0
bounds the rescaled load error, which we found to help training stability. At ξ = 0, the scaling factor is 1 by
continuity. Kolibri uses λ = 1 × 10−5 and τ = 1.

2.1.5.3 Leading Dense Layers

Unlike Kolibri Origin, which has dense FFNs in its first two blocks, Kolibri uses MoE layers in all 50 blocks.
This is motivated by an ablation of the Kolibri architecture over the 327.6B-token budget of scale L that
found slightly lower loss and BPB and higher accuracy when replacing leading dense blocks with MoE
layers (Table 5). Diagnostics of the full Kolibri run, however, show that while the expert loads are balanced, its
first two MoE layers make almost no use of their routed experts. Appendix B.6 gives the routing diagnostics
of the full run and an analysis of the first two MoE layers.

2.1.6 Infrastructure

Pre-training ran on 768 NVIDIA B200 GPUs, across 96 nodes with 8 GPUs per node. The cluster has an
NVIDIA SuperPOD™ topology. Within each node, the GPUs share an NVLink™ Generation 5 interconnect
with 1.8 TB s−1 bidirectional bitrate. The nodes are connected via InfiniBand by 8 NVIDIA ConnectX-7®
network adapters per node, so each GPU can sustain a cross-node bidirectional bitrate of 100 GB s−1.

Our parallelism setup for pre-training is EP 8, FSDP 16, DP 6. With this, we make use of the hierarchical
interconnect in pre-training: Expert parallel across the 8 GPUs within each node and (fully-sharded) data
parallel across nodes. Thus, dense parameters are sharded across EP × FSDP = 128 GPUs (one full replica),
while sparse expert parameters are sharded across FSDP = 16 GPUs—the same local GPU on each node
in one full replica. Mid-training and long-context extension use FSDP 128, DP 6.

We leverage several existing libraries, namely PyTorch 2.14 (Ansel et al. 2024), DeepEP v2 (C. Zhao et al.
2025) for expert parallelism, and FlashAttention 4 (Zadouri et al. 2026) for both SWA and full attention
computations. Ablations without EP use SonicMoE (W. Guo et al. 2025) as an efficient MoE implementation.

For further details, see Appendix B.7, including Appendix B.7.1 on straggler detection.

m

2.2 Optimisation

Kolibri is trained with a Warmup-Stable-Merge (WSM) recipe (Tian, J. Wang, et al. 2025). The learning rate
warms up linearly over the first 100B tokens and then stays constant through pre-training, mid-training

                                                                                       18 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                        Aleph Alpha

and long-context training. In our experiments, a constant learning rate with checkpoint merging improves
post-trained performance over a learning-rate cooldown (Maskey et al. 2026). Each stage (except pre-
training) continues with the optimiser state after finishing the previous one and therefore needs no further
warmup. We then determine the base model weights as the average of the last 20 checkpoints of the
long-context stage (Yunshui Li et al. 2025), which are 10B tokens apart. Over the three stages the context
length grows from 16k to 256k tokens and the token batch from 75.5M to 201M tokens per step (Figure 3).
The architecture improvements, particularly the hybrid of sliding-window and full attention, enable longer
training sequences than in Kolibri Origin, which pre-trains at 4k tokens and reaches 64k tokens only in its
long-context stage (Sections 2.1 and 2.1.3.1). Sequences are assembled online from a Random Sequential
Document Buffer (RSDB) (V. Singh et al. 2026), which spreads the chunks of a long document over many
optimiser steps, by bin packing these chunks without padding tokens or unnecessary document cuts (H.
Ding et al. 2024) (Appendices C.1, C.1.1 and C.1.2).

We optimise attention and expert weights with Muon (Jordan et al. 2024), embedding weights and norm
scales with Adam (Kingma and Ba 2015), and LM head and router weights with AdamW (Loshchilov and
Hutter 2019) (Table 6). In contrast to the commonly used convention of matching Muon’s update RMS to
that of AdamW (Jingyuan Liu et al. 2025; Kimi Team 2025), we adopt the spectral convention (Bernstein
2025), which keeps the Muon learning rate independent of model width (Section 2.2.1). We use decoupled
weight decay for all optimiser groups except the embedding and norm scales. Adam and AdamW use
ϵ = 1 × 10−15, small enough that the updates of parameters with small gradients, such as the LM head
and the routers, stay independent of the gradient scale, as Muon updates are by construction.

Table 6 lists the optimiser, peak learning rate, weight decay and initialisation of every parameter group;
load balancing is described in Section 2.1.5. Our hyperparameter optimisation and transfer scheme is
described in Section 2.2.1.

AdamW           2.5 × 10−5          2−12                    Q, K, V; expert up and gate
                                                            Attention output; expert down         std 1/ din
Adam            2 × 10−3            none                                                          zero
                                                            Router gates

Table 6. Optimiser and initialisation of Koli√b︁ri by parameter group. Muon uses momentum µ = 0.95 with Nesterov and spectral scaling, which
multiplies the orthogonalised update by dout/din; Adam and AdamW use β1 = 0.9, β2 = 0.95 and ϵ = 1 × 10−15. Decoupled weight
decay multiplies the decayed weights by 1 − 2−12 in every step, independent of the learning rate. Normal initialisations are truncated at

three standard deviations and rescaled to the stated standard deviation; din is the fan-in of a layer, L = 50 the number of layers, and expert
biases start at zero.

2.2.1 Hyperparameter Transfer

The optimal hyperparameters depend on model size and token horizon (G. Yang, E. J. Hu, et al. 2021;
McCandlish et al. 2018; Bjorck et al. 2024; Porian et al. 2024; Bergsma et al. 2025), but a sweep at the

                                                                                       19 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

scale of the target run is infeasible. Proxy runs on smaller models and shorter horizons therefore guide the
choice of hyperparameters for the full-scale run. A common approach fits scaling laws that extrapolate
along both dimensions jointly (DeepSeek-AI 2024a; H. Li et al. 2025; Porian et al. 2024; Poolside 2026),
which requires sweeps at many points of a 2D grid over model size and horizon. Instead, we factorise
the transfer across model size and token horizon, and vary model size only through the width, at the 50
layers of the target model (Section 2.2.1.2). For the width transfer we use Maximal Update Parametrisation
(µP) (G. Yang, E. J. Hu, et al. 2021; G. Yang, Simon, and Bernstein 2024), which makes the optimal base
learning rate of every parameter group approximately width-independent, so that hyperparameters found
on narrower models carry over to the full-scale model (Dey et al. 2025; Meituan LongCat Team 2025).
The dependence on the token horizon can then be studied at a single narrower width. Instead of scaling
laws for the optimal learning rate and batch (DeepSeek-AI 2024a; H. Li et al. 2025; Bjorck et al. 2024;
Bergsma et al. 2025), we use loss extrapolation (Domhan, Springenberg, and Hutter 2015) to predict the
learning-rate landscape at the target horizon and pick the best cell of a grid of Muon learning rates and
batch sizes on a narrower proxy (Section 2.2.1.3).

The remainder of this section fixes the learning-rate ratios between the parameter groups (Section 2.2.1.1),
introduces the width transfer with µP (Section 2.2.1.2) and the horizon transfer by loss extrapolation (Sec-
tion 2.2.1.3) for learning rate, batch size and weight decay.

2.2.1.1 Learning-Rate Ratios

We find that different parameter groups require different learning-rate scales to achieve optimal perfor-
mance. To avoid an explosion of the grid in later sweeps we optimise the ratios between the learning rates
of the different embedding, norm and head parameter groups as a factor of the Muon learning rate in a
preliminary sweep. In internal sweeps, we find these ratios to be relatively stable across widths and token
horizons, and therefore assume that they can be held fixed when transferring hyperparameters. The router
learning rate is set equal to the head learning rate, based on internal ablations. The resulting production
learning rates are listed in Table 6.

2.2.1.2 Transfer Across Width

While there is work on joint width and depth transfer with µP (Bordelon et al. 2023; G. Yang, D. Yu, et al.
2023; Dey et al. 2025), it is less established and introduces additional complexity, and we therefore restrict
ourselves to width transfer only. We keep the number of experts and top-k fixed and scale the size of
every expert, so that the routing and load-balancing dynamics of the proxy match the production model.
Attention heads keep their dimension of 128 and we only scale the number of heads, as in µP (G. Yang,
E. J. Hu, et al. 2021). We assume the optimal batch size to be only dependent on token horizon (H. Li et al.
2025).

As described in Section 2.2, Muon runs in the spectral convention: after the Newton–Schulz orthogo-
                                            √︁

nalisation the update is scaled by dout/din, so that every layer receives an update of unit RMS operator
norm times the learning rate regardless of its shape (Bernstein 2025). This ensures that the optimisation
dynamics satisfy the spectral condition (G. Yang, Simon, and Bernstein 2024), and makes the Muon learn-

                                                                                       20 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                                                         Aleph Alpha

ing rate width-independent. The learning rates of embedding and norm scales are width-independent
as well, whereas those of the LM head and router scale with 1/d. µP also shrinks the initialisation of the
output layers with width. We initialise the LM head at the infinite-width limit of this scaling, zero, which
removes the width dependence of the initial outputs and brings the optimal hyperparameters of narrow
and wide models closer together (G. Yang, E. J. Hu, et al. 2021, App. D.2). The attention output and expert
down projections also start at zero, so that every block starts as the identity map (Hongyi Zhang, Dauphin,
and T. Ma 2019); in our ablations this converged faster and still transfers across width (Table 6). The router
is initialised with standard deviation 1/din (Vankadara et al. 2026).

To validate the transfer we swept the base learning rate on the proxies M and L (Table 3), where L is M

scaled to twice the width, both trained for 97.6B tokens, the horizon of M, with a batch of 576 sequences

of 16k tokens and with learning rates scaled according to the rules described above. They are not scaled

down from the production model, since the width sweep is an earlier ablation. Results are visualised in

Figure 10a, with the loss averaged over the trailing 3B tokens of each run. We see a shift of the optimal

2.2.1.3 Transfer Across Token Horizon

Transferring the optimal learning rate and batch size to a target token horizon is often done via scaling
laws (DeepSeek-AI 2024a; H. Li et al. 2025; Bjorck et al. 2024; Porian et al. 2024). These necessitate a large
grid of values to have enough points for a reliable power-law fit, making them expensive. Additionally, they

                                                                                       21 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                Aleph Alpha

complicate transferring other hyperparameters such as weight decay from short to long horizons, as this
would combinatorially explode the grid.

For Kolibri Origin, we fit power laws to the optimal learning rate and batch size at horizons from 100B to
430B tokens and extrapolate them to its pre-training budget of 7.5T tokens. Extrapolating those laws to the
Kolibri setting gives unrealistic hyperparameter predictions, motivating a method that uses the observed
loss trajectories of the candidate configurations.

We employ loss extrapolation (Domhan, Springenberg, and Hutter 2015) to predict the hyperparameters
with the lowest loss at longer token horizons based on the observed training dynamics at shorter horizons.
This allows us to use smaller grids for the search and employ coordinate descent to tune further hyperpa-
rameters iteratively. Since the sweep uses the batch size, warmup, learning-rate schedule and data of the
production run on the width-scaled proxy (Table 7), it doubles as a test of the production configuration,
and its runs are reused for the weight-decay sweep described below.

We use a power law with floor (Hoffmann et al. 2022) in the cumulative-learning-rate coordinate (Tissue,

V. Wang, and L. Wang 2025),

                             L(D) = E + A (D − W/2)−α,                                                    (9)

where D is the number of tokens, W the length of the linear warmup in tokens, E the irreducible loss, and
A and α the scale and exponent of the reducible part. After a linear warmup of W tokens at a constant
learning rate, the cumulative learning rate is proportional to D − W/2. We validated the method on internal
loss curves, not reported here, and find an average prediction error at the target token horizon below 1 %.
All tested methods are fairly sensitive to the fit start point. For the method used, we discard the first 20 %
of the post-warmup prefix before fitting. We find that the optimal grid point is robust to the specific form
of the power law used for extrapolation: we cross-checked with a shifted power law with floor (Domhan,
Springenberg, and Hutter 2015) and a one-break broken power law (Caballero et al. 2023) and obtained
the same ranking.

Learning Rate and Batch Size We sweep a grid of Muon learning rates from 5 × 10−4 to 8 × 10−3 and two
batch sizes on the width-scaled proxy model (Table 7), which halves the width of the production model
with the same rules as in Section 2.2.1.2, for 500B tokens. The observed loss is averaged over the trailing
2B tokens of each run and, at 500B tokens, is still lowest at the largest learning rate for both batches. The
prediction at 20T tokens is visualised in Figure 10b, with an optimal Muon learning rate of 1 × 10−3 and a
batch size of 4608 sequences of 16k tokens each. The selected batch is the smaller of the two, so while a
further reduction in batch size might be beneficial, we prioritise maintaining training efficiency.

Kolibri  Width               Heads / KV heads               Expert hidden      Experts, top-k    Layers
Proxy
         2560                               48 / 4                        512            384, 6       50
          1280                               24 / 2                      256             384, 6       50

Table 7. Production model and the width-scaled proxy used for the loss-extrapolation sweeps over learning rate, batch and weight decay.
Layers, experts, head dimension and the attention pattern are shared.

                                                            22 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

Weight Decay We use the same loss extrapolation to set the weight decay in a one-dimensional sweep.
Starting from the selected learning rate and batch size, we vary the weight decay and compare the
predicted loss at 20T tokens (triangles in Figure 10b). The baseline cell was trained with a weight decay of
2−13. Doubling the weight decay to 2−12 lowers both the observed loss and the forecasts at every horizon,
whereas halving it raises them. All estimators agree on this order, and we use 2−12 for Kolibri. Larger values
were not tested.

Limitations This optimisation is done purely with respect to the training loss under a warmup-stable
learning-rate schedule. While evidence shows that a WSM schedule can lead to better post-trained
performance (Maskey et al. 2026), merging and post-trained performance are not considered here.
Additionally, uncertainties are not modelled and some of the differences between grid points may be
within the noise level of the measurements, although all three forms of the extrapolation law select the
same cell (Section 2.2.1.3). We leave modelling merged-checkpoint and post-trained performance, as well
as uncertainty of the forecasts, to future iterations of the recipe.

                                                            23 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

2.3 Data and Training Stages

2.3.1 Overview

Kolibri Base is trained in three stages: pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension. Pre-training
consumes 20T tokens from a broad English-German data pool and establishes the model’s general
capabilities. Mid-training shifts the data distribution towards higher-quality reasoning, mathematics, code,
and instruction-following data, training on 3.4T tokens. Finally, long-context extension trains on 200B
tokens and extends the context length to 256k tokens.
Across these stages, data selection progressively becomes more selective and task-oriented.
Pre-training emphasises breadth and coverage, mid-training concentrates on capabilities that benefit
from higher-quality and more structured data, and long-context extension adapts these capabilities to
substantially longer sequences.
In addition to filtering illegal, harmful, toxic and pirated content, we redact PII from all training sources.
Each category is detected by a regular expression, checked by a validator that rejects false positives, and
replaced by a special token. A document whose redacted bytes exceed 15 % of its length is dropped. In one
illustrative run on a mid-training data sample, 5.3 % of scanned documents received at least one redaction,
with PDF data accounting for 92 % of all redactions. We report these numbers only as an example of the
redaction behaviour; the sample is not representative of the overall training corpus.
The following sections describe the data construction, mixing, and evaluation choices for each stage.

2.3.2 Stage 1: Pre-training Data

The pre-training dataset pool consists of 78 English- and German-language datasets with approximately
29.3T materialised tokens, from which the final training mix consumes 20T. The pool spans web text, code,
instruction data, scientific and technical text, and legal and public-sector data.
We group datasets into nine domains according to language, subject, and degree of structure. Datasets
spanning several domains are assigned to the domain containing the majority of their tokens. Table 8
summarises the resulting pool, and Figure 11 shows each domain’s share of its tokens and datasets.
Web data makes up the largest part of the pool. Its largest individual source comes from our internally
developed CC pipeline, which processes and filters Common Crawl snapshots to produce more than
308B quality-scored documents for downstream data mixing.
German accounts for 21.3 % of the final pre-training tokens and is treated separately in the data construction
process because its availability and filtering requirements differ from English.
Approximately 24 % (4.8T tokens) of our pre-training data is synthetic in origin (including rephrases and
translations). We produced synthetic data in English, using our deduplicated English CC data (see Sec-

tion 2.3.2.1) as seeds for rephrasing with Gemma-4-26B-A4B. We followed a Nemotron-CC-style recipe (D.
Su et al. 2025), using five prompt templates applied to each seed document: diverse QA pairs, distillation,
knowledge list, knowledge extraction, and encyclopedia-style rewriting. The outputs were post-processed
and filtered to remove LLM-generation artefacts and erroneous generations. In Section 2.3.2.2 we describe
our work on German synthetic data.

The following subsections describe key decisions around the Common Crawl (Common Crawl Foundation
2026) pipeline, the construction of the German data pool, quality annotation, and the final data-source
mixing procedure in more detail.

2.3.2.1 Common Crawl Pipeline

The CC pipeline is designed to process Common Crawl snapshots to curate high quality pre-training data
at scale. Text is extracted from the raw archives with Resiliparse (Bevendorff et al. 2018), yielding more than
308B documents.

Language-agnostic heuristic filters and language identification route each document to an English or a
German branch, which apply separate thresholds. Exact deduplication removes byte-identical documents
across all snapshots, which yields 21.16B distinct documents. Fuzzy deduplication, which compares
MinHash signatures of documents bucketed by locality-sensitive hashing, removes a further 25.9 % as
near-duplicates (Appendix D.1.3).

Substring deduplication cuts repeated blocks of at least 500 bytes, such as navigation menus and page
templates, out of the remaining documents, removing 21.3 % of text bytes (Appendix D.1.4). Appendix D.1
describes the stages in more detail.

Figure 12 shows how many documents each stage of the pipeline had as an input and removed, from the
extracted documents entering the heuristic filters to substring deduplication.

2.3.2.2 German Pre-Training Data

At our 20 T token budget, our ablations show strong German benchmark performance with around 20 %
German data in the training mix. This leaves us with a lower bound of 4 T German tokens needed for training.
Note that this refers to training tokens, not unique tokens. Since datasets are commonly upsampled during
training, the underlying data pool can be smaller than 4 T, though not arbitrarily so, because repeating
data can have diminishing returns after roughly four epochs (Muennighoff, Rush, et al. 2023).

To put the scale of this requirement into context, we first look at the amount of German pre-training data
available in existing open datasets. Table 9 summarises several major open German datasets. These
datasets are shown only as a reference point for the scale of publicly available German data and does
not describe our training set. For further details on training data, refer to the Public Summary of Training
Content.

The open datasets in the upper part of Table 9 add up to around 1.94T tokens. This token count does
not account for filtering, deduplication, or overlap between datasets, so the amount of usable unique
data would in practice be smaller. This comparison illustrates the challenge of sourcing German data at

                                                             26 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

the scale implied by our 4T-token training target. In the following, we describe the three approaches we
considered to construct our German data pool.

First, we process raw Common Crawl dumps with a pipeline that is parameterised by language, since
German and English web data cannot be filtered in exactly the same way. In particular, we retune several
filtering parameters for German. For example, web-data pipelines commonly remove documents with
unusually long words. However, German administrative prose often contains substantially longer words
than English, so applying English thresholds can disproportionately remove this register. We therefore
use FineWeb2’s German word-length settings, which allow a mean word length between 0 and 15 char-
acters, instead of the 3 to 10 character range used by Gopher for English. With these language-specific
adjustments, our pipeline produces 0.8T unique tokens of organic German web data.

Our second approach to augmenting our German corpus is to rephrase German documents. We use
Mistral-Nemo-Instruct-2407 to rewrite organic German documents as an encyclopedia entry, a Q&A
dialogue or an educational-style passage, preserving its semantic information. This teaches the model the
same facts in several surface forms and multiplies scarce organic data, and it is a different operation from
translation: the source is German, so the subject matter, the institutions and the register stay German.
Rephrasing gave us about 1.1T unique tokens, making it the single largest source of German in the model.
This data had already been produced for the Aleph-Alpha-GermanWeb project (T. F. Burns et al. 2026).

The third approach is translation. Translating English into German can expand the available data when
the model, prompts, and chunking are chosen carefully, but it does not provide the same kind of German
data as organically sourced text. Translated output can retain traces of the source language, such as
literal renderings of idioms; for example, Drive safe!” may become Fahre sicher!” rather than the more
natural “Komm gut an!”. More importantly, translation preserves much of the source corpus’s cultural
and institutional distribution. A corpus translated from English therefore remains, in many respects, an
English-language view of the world expressed in German. We experimented with translated data at scale
for Kolibri Origin, but for Kolibri we relied on organic and rephrased German data alongside a carefully
curated set of translated German data.

One side-effect of us building our own CC pipeline is that we can incorporate new crawls as they arrive so
that the resulting data pool remains up-to-date. Table 9 also lists the year from which the latest source
data of each listed dataset comes from. Using our own pipeline, we can ensure that the most recent data
in the pool dates from 2026.

German benchmarks are also limited in coverage and sensitivity. We therefore built a dedicated German
evaluation suite, combining hand-crafted and model-generated tasks, to better measure the impact of
our German data. See Section 2.4.

2.3.2.3 Quality Annotation

Method Prior works (J. Li, Fang, Smyrnis, et al. 2024; Ettinger et al. 2025) have shown that simple, single
fastText classifiers (Joulin et al. 2017) trained to distinguish, e.g., SFT-style data vs. random CC samples,
can be a successful method for curating CC data for quality, leading to improved model performance

                                                                                       27 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

on evaluation benchmarks. Other works have reported methods of ensembling classifier scores in ways
which appear to outperform using individual classifiers alone (D. Su et al. 2025; T. F. Burns et al. 2026). We
therefore experimented with ensembling approaches and compared this with a strong single-classifier
baseline. Simultaneously, we wanted to keep in mind scalability, e.g., it could be wasteful to apply an
LLM-as-a-judge to tens of billions of documents. For German, we built upon our public work introduced
in GermanWeb (T. F. Burns et al. 2026) by ensembling quality and grammar fastText classifiers using an
average of their scores. To score the English documents, we developed the following method.

We first scored a fixed 1M-document English sample of our curated CC data with a judge, Qwen3-32B
(Qwen Team 2025a), using more than 20 prompts. Prompts include educational value, chat-likeness,
encyclopedia-likeness, content versus format properties, binary ‘quality’ judgements, and benchmark
usefulness (Penedo, Kydlíček, Ben Allal, et al. 2024; J. Li, Fang, Smyrnis, et al. 2024; Sachdeva et al. 2026;
Wettig et al. 2024). For each prompt, the judge returns structured JSON, enforced via constrained decoding
against a schema. We noted that disagreement between judged axes was asymmetric. Low-scoring
documents did so on multiple (sometimes all) prompts, whereas high-scoring documents scored well on
some prompts but not others. This implies that the properties by which documents were judged as ‘good’
were more distinctive than the absence of those properties2. Document-level Spearman correlations
among the judge scores was also structured into groups of highly-correlated scores, e.g., content versus
format, conversational, maths, code, and evaluation- or task-usefulness.

To score the rest of the crawl, we distil each scalar judgement axis into a binary task (top quartile versus
bottom quartile, discarding the middle two) and train three families of classifiers: fastText (Joulin et al.
2017), a frozen Luxical-one embedding (DatologyAI et al. 2025) with an MLP head, and a fine-tuned BERT
(Devlin et al. 2019). We evaluate the classifiers on a held-out sample of 1M CC documents, sampled and
judged independently. Using this data, we were able to see how well classifiers reproduced the judge,
finding that while BERT models matched most closely over all (held-out F1 0.945, AUC 0.987), Luxical-based
classifiers performed nearly as well (F1 0.913, AUC 0.971), and much better than fastText (F1 0.865). Given
this, to balance inference speed over our corpus with quality, we opted to use the Luxical-based distilled
judge classifiers when constructing our Kolibri dataset. The distilled classifiers also showed the same
correlation structures as the judges. We combined the Luxical classifiers with a binary fastText classifier
trained to distinguish SFT-style data vs. random CC samples, which has been a successful method in
previous works (J. Li, Fang, Smyrnis, et al. 2024; Ettinger et al. 2025).

Mixing Annotations on the Simplex We combine five English per-document scores with weights on the
probability simplex. The scores are fastText P (high_quality) and four Luxical heads (educational anchor,
chat-like anchor, content-focussed score, and MMLU usefulness). We choose these four Luxical heads
on the bases that they are minimally redundant compared to other annotation axes, and because we
want to balance efficient inference time over our corpus (ruling out BERT classifiers) while having higher
agreement with the judges (ruling out fastText classifiers). Each classifier score lies in [0, 1]. To condition
the mix on fastText quality, we pair each score with either the identity or the indicator 1{fastText > τ } with
τ = 0.0216 (the corpus-median fastText score). This gives 2 × 5 = 10 basis columns, with weights Wa,b on

                                                                       ∑︁
the ten-dimensional probability simplex, Wa,b ≥ 0 and a,b Wa,b = 1. We search the simplex by sampling

      2By analogy, foods can be tasty in many distinct ways (a sweet apple, a savoury fish), but spoiled food is spoiled in much the same way.

                                                                                       28 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                 Aleph Alpha

21 interior vectors from Dirichlet(α = 1), as well as five one-hot vertices, namely the ungated single-score

baselines (all weight on one raw score, with the identity gate). We combine the scores like so:

                                                                (︁               )︁                        (10)
S(d) = combine Wa,b · rank gatea(d) · raterb(d) ,
a, b

where combine is a weighted mean, the gates a are the identity and 1{fastText > τ }, the raters b are the
four Luxical heads and fastText, and rank(·) is the per-column mid-rank empirical cumulative distribution
function. By selecting the top 25% of token mass based on these document scores, we optimise the ten
weights. We run ablations with a 0.4B dense DCLM model trained to 1× Chinchilla on 8.2B randomly
sampled tokens and record the mean BPB over all English benchmarks. We use these means in a Gaussian-
process surrogate over the samples which then selects the optimum weights to minimise the mean BPB
for the final mix.

English-Language Ablations We apply the optimised mix of annotations to our CC data, and compare the
performance of this mix with random CC, the fastText classifier alone, and the Kolibri Origin mix. Table 10
compares the two mixes on the 0.4B dense 1× Chinchilla DCLM model (J. Li, Fang, Smyrnis, et al. 2024;
Hoffmann et al. 2022) (8.2B tokens) and on a 7.8B-A0.6B MoE trained to 97.6B tokens (∼ 170 tokens per
active parameter). The table also reports random CC and fastText as a single CC quality classifier. At 0.4B,
English-overall BPB is 3.800 ± 0.513 (n = 3) against 3.988 ± 0.271 for the Kolibri Origin mix (n = 5). 3 English
QA is 3.901 ± 1.012 against 5.080 ± 0.814. At 7.8B-A0.6B MoE, overall BPB is 0.607 ± 0.012 (n = 3) against
0.642 ± 0.013 (n = 2), and English-overall BPB is 0.618 ± 0.015 against 0.652 ± 0.026. Maths is 0.493 ± 0.003
against 0.568 ± 0.023, and code is 0.550 ± 0.067 against 0.572 ± 0.027. The posterior probability that the
annotated CC mix is better than the Kolibri Origin mix on overall BPB at the MoE scale is 0.98, and 0.93 that
it is better than the fastText classifier alone.

Limitations In Kolibri, we use only a single distilled judge and the fastText classifier for annotations, and
apply this procedure for our English data only. We wish to extend this technique to other annotations and
languages. Another limitation is that the simplex mix is over five scores, whereas we expect notions of
‘quality’ or signals of what data is useful for language modelling are even higher dimensional.

2.3.2.4 Data Source Mixing

Having filtered and annotated the CC data and included the other data sources in our pre-training data
pool, we fit a mixing law on a swarm of proxy models to set each data source’s sampling weight, following
an approach inspired by OLMix (M. F. Chen et al. 2026). The mix ranges over all the 78 data sources of
the pre-training pool. These sources include our English and German CC ‘quality’-annotated buckets.
We form these buckets by ranking documents on the mixture-of-annotations score of Section 2.3.2.3, a
blend of five annotation scores for English and the average of two GermanWeb classifiers for German, and
splitting each language into five equal-token-mass tiers. We include only the higher tiers, dropping the
two lowest for English (i.e., including the top three, ∼ 60% of the token mass) and the lowest for German
(i.e., including the top four, ∼ 80% of the token mass). The objective is the equal-weighted mean BPB over
24 benchmarks, 18 English and 6 German 4.

Sampling and Ablations for Optimisation We train a swarm of 30M-parameter dense proxy models,
each on 3B tokens of a different mixture, and record its per-benchmark BPB. The swarm is 3354 proxy
trainings in total. Mixtures are drawn from a Dirichlet distribution centred on the natural token-count prior
q ∝ tokens, at 15 concentrations spaced log-uniformly in [0.1, 50], with 3(D+1) mixtures per concentration
for the D = 78 sources, plus the D one-hot corners.

Optimisation and Materialisation To predict each benchmark’s BPB from a given data mix, we fit a log-
linear law over the swarm of proxy models. We use log-linear regression models as proposed by M. F. Chen
et al. (2026), since they found that these models achieve the best downstream performance. For each
benchmark t, intercept at, coefficient vector bt, and data mix x, we fit

       BPBt(x) = exp(at) + exp(bt · x)

over the swarm by limited-memory Broyden–Fletcher–Goldfarb–Shanno (L-BFGS) optimisation with a

Huber loss and multi-start initialisation. We then pick the mix x by solving the convex program

   ∑︂                                                                ∑︁                          (11)

min wt exp(bt · x) + λ KL(x ∥ q), x ≥ 0, i xi = 1, xi ≤ κi,
x
   t

where wt are the objective weights, q ∝ tokens1/T is a temperature-scaled token prior, and the caps
κi = tokensi · r/R forbid a source from repeating more than r times at the target token budget R = 20T .
The KL pull trades quality against repetition: at λ = 0 the solver ignores source sizes and picks only the
best-fitting sources, while a large λ reproduces the size prior. We settle on λ = 0.05, T = 3, and r = 3,
which keeps every small source represented at least once while staying close to the prior. On the proxy
objective the chosen mix is marginally worse than the unconstrained optimum (predicted 3.670 ± 0.002
versus 3.667 ± 0.002 mean BPB), which we consider a reasonable trade-off for keeping the smaller sources.
The materialised mix draws 35.7% of its training tokens (7.15T) from our own pipeline and 64.3% (12.85T)
from external sources; Table 11 breaks down our own contribution by family.

     4Among the English benchmarks are MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag, HumanEval, MBPP, CommonsenseQA, Drop, NaturalQsOpen, PIQA,
SocialIQA, and Squad. The German benchmarks are Global-MMLU, ARC, HellaSwag, HumanEval, MBPP, and GSM8K.

out of a 20T-token budget. External sources supply the remaining 64.3%.

Limitations and Learnings We fit the data mix weights on models with 30M parameters trained on 3B
tokens, which makes both the fit and the swarm noisy. We find that the optimiser of the data mix weights is
sensitive to the number of swarm samples, and the prior and its two knobs (λ, T ) influence the predicted
optimum dramatically. It is not clear whether this is a limitation of the optimiser or a property of the data mix
problem. The natural prior is strongly skewed towards the largest sources, meaning that smaller sources
are typically less well-explored even at the lowest concentrations. Some of our German quality-classifier
buckets are down-weighted relative to their labels, so the mix optimisation does not appear to comport
with the German quality ordering. We wish to re-evaluate these annotations and their mixing in the future.
We also patched several token-count errors (byte-versus-token miscounts and a bucket mismatch) before
the final solve. Unfortunately, the sampling prior for the swarm ablations, computed from the old counts,
cannot be corrected after the fact. These errors are likely to have a small impact on the final mix, but we of
course wish to avoid them in the future.

                                                            31 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

2.3.3 Stage 2: Mid-Training

Mid-training continues training on higher-quality math, code, reasoning, and instruction data to strengthen
these capabilities. Following S. Hu et al. (2024), who reserve higher-quality data for the later phases of
training, we draw this data from a curated pool of datasets, and a mix (Section 2.3.3.3) sets the number
of tokens each dataset contributes. Mid-training keeps the constant learning rate and the checkpoint
merging of pre-training (Section 2.2). The stage ends in the base checkpoint from which long-context
extension (Section 2.3.4) continues training at the same learning rate.

2.3.3.1 Dataset Pool

The curated pool is the set of datasets from which the mid-training and long-context mixes draw. We collect
candidate datasets from public releases and from the pre-training datasets, drop every candidate whose
content largely overlaps another, and assign each dataset to one of the nine domains of Section 2.3.2.
The pool excludes the low-quality tiers of web data and, for German, the rephrased and translated web
text. The German exclusion rests on manual inspection, in which rephrased and translated text read less
naturally than organic German text.

Every dataset of the pool passes the cleanup pass of Appendix D.1.1, exact deduplication across the whole
pool (Appendix D.1.2), and PII redaction. The size of a dataset is its number of unique tokens, estimated
from its size in bytes as for the pre-training pool (Table 8). The repetition caps (Section 2.3.3.3) count the
passes of pre-training over a dataset against this size, and production mid-training draws each dataset,
over its tokens after treatment, for at most the passes that its cap leaves. In addition, the production mix
draws from the pool after its decontamination against the evaluation suite (Appendix D.2.5). Table 12 lists,
per domain, the number of datasets, the unique tokens, the tokens after treatment, and the most that
the repetition caps of Appendix D.2.1 allow mid-training to draw, and Figure 13 shows the share of each
domain in unique tokens and in datasets. Documents above 128k estimated tokens are reserved for the
long-context stage (Section 2.3.4), since they are scarce and only its sequence length of 256k tokens holds
them in full.

2.3.3.2 Ablation Setup

The ablations use a proxy model, a downscaled Kolibri with the same hybrid architecture, 11.9B total and
0.65B active parameters, pre-trained for 2.79T tokens. We mid-train that checkpoint on every mix for 84B
tokens, 3 % of the proxy’s pre-training tokens, at the production sequence length of 64k tokens. At this
budget, 18 runs cover three hand-set mixes and six optimised mixes (Section 2.3.3.3), the production mix,
six mixes that vary the in-domain composition of the two language domains (Appendix D.2.4), C1 entered
through a transition from the pre-training mix, and the Kolibri Origin mid-training mix (Table 40). Two
of the hand-set mixes, C2 and C3, and the production mix also run for 400B tokens, 14 % of the proxy’s
pre-training tokens and closer to the 17 % of production (3.44T after 19.99T), and a baseline run of the
same length continues pre-training on the pre-training mix at its sequence length of 16k tokens. Every
score is that of a merged checkpoint, the uniform average of the weights of the last k saved checkpoints

general-knowledge, math, and code means of each language and weights English and German 80/20,
and Appendix D.2.2 lists the benchmarks. For 16 of the 18 runs at 84B and for every run at 400B, we
also report the pooled score of the post-training benchmarks after an SFT, which measures the part of
a mid-training gain that persists after post-training. All SFTs share one recipe and one data mix, so their
differences come from the base checkpoints alone. An eval-noise model, fitted to replicate SFTs with the
standard errors of Miller (2024), gives each score a 95 % interval (Appendix D.2.2).

2.3.3.3 Mixing Method

We search for the mix with the highest aggregate score (Section 2.3.3.2) within the repetition limits of the
scarce high-quality datasets of the pool. Optimising the domain weights for this score alone sets four
domains to zero (Section 2.3.3.5).

Mixes and Sampling Rules A mid-training mix assigns every dataset of the curated pool a share of the
token budget along two axes. The domain weights give each of the nine domains its share of the budget,
and the in-domain composition splits the budget of a domain among its datasets. An optimiser in the
style of Olmix (M. F. Chen et al. 2026), fitted to the scores of MergeMix expert merges (J. Wang et al.
2026) as described below, proposes candidate domain weights, on which we then train proxy models
(Appendix D.2.3). The in-domain composition follows one of two sampling rules. Under the natural rule,
each dataset’s share of its domain is proportional to its unique tokens (Section 2.3.3.1). The pre-training-
delivered rule keeps the pre-training proportions of the datasets that pre-training drew from and gives
datasets new to mid-training their natural share. In addition, we hand-set three candidates, C1 through
C3 (Table 13), which serve as the baselines for the optimised mixes and as the reference mixes of the KL
penalty. C1 and C2 share their domain weights and differ only in the sampling rule, natural for C1 and

                                                                                       33 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

pre-training-delivered for C2. The optimised mixes and the production mix use the pre-training-delivered
rule.

Repetition Caps High-quality data is scarce, so a mid-training mix repeats its highest-quality datasets.
The repetition cap of a dataset limits its draws across pre-training and mid-training combined to four
epochs, the number of epochs up to which training on repeated data reaches approximately the loss of
training on as many unique tokens (Muennighoff, Rush, et al. 2023). Within each domain, a waterfill assigns
every dataset its share under the sampling rule, pins a dataset whose share exceeds its cap at the cap, and
redistributes the excess to the unpinned datasets of the domain in proportion to their shares.

Expert Merging and Optimisation In MergeMix, the proxy model is mid-trained on each of the nine
domains alone for 20B tokens, and a merge of these expert checkpoints with the weights of a candidate
mix approximates an 84B-token mid-training run on that mix. From a shared initialisation, merging the
experts and training on the mixed data agree to first order, and over 12 mixes, expert merges rank the
mixes as full mid-training runs do, with a Spearman correlation above 0.9 (J. Wang et al. 2026). Expert
merging thus allows us to evaluate 113 weightings, 100 sampled from Dirichlet distributions around our
initial hand-set mixes and 13 placed by hand to cover the range of the four smallest domains. Following
Olmix (M. F. Chen et al. 2026), we fit one log-linear law per benchmark to the expert-merge scores and
solve for the weights that maximise the aggregate score, subject to the repetition caps at a 5T budget
and a KL penalty towards a reference mix, which keeps the solution near a hand-set mix. The pre-training
evaluation suite (Section 2.4) has no benchmark that targets the instruction and reasoning domains or
Specialized domains directly. These domains thus enter the objective only through their effect on other
benchmarks, the instruction and reasoning domains through math and code, and Specialized domains
through none of them.

Optimised Mixes S1 to S6 Six solutions enter the proxy ablations (Table 13) next to the hand-set candidates
(Appendix D.2.3). They vary the reference mix (C2 or C3), the KL penalty (0, 0.01, or 0.05), and whether their
in-domain composition is projected from the 5T budget, at which the repetition caps pin datasets. At the
ablation budget every dataset stays below its cap, so S2 and S5 follow the sampling rule unchanged. For
the projected S1, S3, S4, and S6, the waterfill runs at 5T and its result is scaled down to the ablation budget,
so the datasets pinned at 5T lose share to the other datasets of their domain. S2 and S3 come from the
same solve, as do S5 and S6, and the domain weights of each pair differ by less than one point. In S3
and S6, the projection changes the in-domain composition of English instruction and reasoning, German
instruction and reasoning, and STEM (QA-style), and in S3 also that of Code. Appendix D.2.1 gives further
details on the caps, the optimisation, and the solutions.

German instruction and reasoning raised to its repetition cap (Appendix D.2.7). At 84B, the SFT scores of
the solutions with penalties 0, 0.01, and 0.05 lie within 0.9 points of each other, below the resolution of the
