eval-noise model, so the choice of the penalty rests on the domain weights. Without the penalty, the solve
sets four domains to zero, and at 0.05, the weight that M. F. Chen et al. (2026) recommend, the solution
stays closest to its hand-set prior. We thus take 0.01, the smallest tested penalty at which every domain
keeps a positive weight. In this mix, English instruction and reasoning, STEM (QA-style), and Code hold
73 % of the budget together, and the two web domains hold 3.4 % (Table 13). We mid-train Kolibri on 3.44T
tokens of this mix and interpolate linearly from the pre-training mix to it over the first H = 100B tokens
(Appendix D.2.6).

Decontamination Benchmark text in the late phase of training is memorised and, under a constant learn-
ing rate as in Kolibri, also inflates benchmark scores (Magar and Schwartz 2022). Benchmark text in the
early phase is forgotten once the subsequent clean data exceeds about five times the compute-optimal
token count (Bordt et al. 2025). Following Olmo 3 (Ettinger et al. 2025, Sec. 3.5.3), we therefore decontami-
nate the mid-training pool, from which long-context extension also draws, and remove every document
that contains text from the 226 tasks of our evaluation suite, with all their splits. The decon algorithm (Allen
Institute for AI 2025) flags a document by matching Inverse Document Frequency (IDF)-weighted n-grams
of the questions and answers, and a flagged document is dropped as a whole. Decontamination drops
1.5M of the 8.3B scanned documents, 0.06 % of the tokens (Appendix D.2.5). The pre-training pool remains
potentially contaminated, and the HumanEval scores reflect this contamination.

HumanEval Recitation A HumanEval completion counts as recited iff it matches the reference solution
shipped with the benchmark (Appendix D.2.8). During pre-training, 22 % to 95 % of the completions are
recited, and pass@1 follows the recited share, with a Pearson correlation of 0.90 over 96 evaluations. Within
the first 0.3T tokens of mid-training, the recited share drops below 10 % in both languages, and English
pass@1 decreases from 80.0 to 70.0. On the problems without a recited completion, English pass@1 stays

                                                            35 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                        Aleph Alpha

at 61 to 76 throughout mid-training (Figure 64). By the end of mid-training, the recited share rises again to
48 % in English and 47 % in German, so the HumanEval scores at that point remain inflated by recitation.
We thus also report the aggregate score without HumanEval (Appendix D.2.2) and plan to decontaminate
the pre-training pool for the next model.

Effect on the Aggregate Score From the M4 merge at the end of pre-training to the M4 merge at the
end of mid-training, the aggregate score rises by 2.8 points, or 2.9 points without HumanEval, with the gain
in math and code in both languages (Figure 14). At 400B, the production mix leads continued pre-training
by 4.9 points on the M4 merge and by 11.5 points after SFT, and it leads C3, the reference mix of its KL
penalty, by 3.8 points after SFT (Table 14). Both differences after SFT exceed the 95 % eval-noise intervals
(Appendix D.2.2), so the production mix, derived from S5, improves on the hand-set mix it is regularised
towards.

pre-training mix at its sequence length. The scores of the same mixes at 84B are in Table 39.

Knowledge Benchmarks The general-knowledge means fall by 1.0 points in English and 1.4 in German
from the pre-training M4 to the mid-training M4, with gains on MMLU-Pro and ARC and losses on TriviaQA
and both HellaSwag benchmarks. After long-context extension, the final base model still scores 5.2
points below the pre-training M4 on TriviaQA and 4.5 on HellaSwag (DE). HellaSwag (DE) rises with the
German-web share of a mix, and the cause of the TriviaQA loss remains open (Appendix D.2.8).

2.3.3.5 Lessons for Choosing a Mix

Regularisation towards a Hand-Set Mix Without the KL penalty, the solve sets English web and docu-
ments, German web and documents, Agentic code and tool use, and Specialized domains to 0 % and
STEM (documents) to 0.3 % (S1 in Table 13). We hypothesise that the weighting of the aggregate score
causes this outcome, since it weights math and code two to one over general knowledge. Across the
84B runs, the English knowledge mean correlates with the English math mean, the MBPP mean, and the
weight of English web and documents at a Pearson r of 0.25, 0.03, and −0.11, and the weight of English
web and documents correlates with the English math mean at −0.67 (Table 41). With the penalty, the two
web domains retain 22.6 % at 0.05 towards C2 (S4) and 3.5 % to 3.6 % at 0.01 (S2 and S3). The penalty
acts on the domain weights as a whole, so at 0.01 towards C3, German web and documents drops from
18 % in C3 to 2.1 % in the production mix. At 400B, the HellaSwag (DE) score of the production mix lies 5.5
points below that of C3, a cost of 0.09 points in the aggregate score (Table 14). Preserving a capability thus
requires its own term or constraint in the objective.

Figure 14. The general-knowledge, math, and code means of the pre-training suite per language along the production chain, at the M4 merge
at the end of pre-training, the M4 and M20 merges at the end of mid-training, and the M20 merge at the end of long-context extension,

Proxy Budget At 400B every adjacent pair of the four runs differs after SFT beyond the eval noise, by
4.3 points from continued pre-training to C2, 3.5 from C2 to C3, and 3.8 from C3 to the production mix
(Table 14). At 84B, C2 and C3 differ by 0.4 points after SFT and by 0.03 on the merge, and the SFT separates
only the lowest runs from the highest, in 14 of 120 pairs (Appendix D.2.2). Relative to the pre-training tokens,
the 400B budget is 83 % of the production ratio and the 84B budget 18 % of it, so the mixes separate after
SFT at the larger proxy budget only. With two budgets, the ratio at which this separation sets in remains
open.

Merge Score and SFT Score The M4 merge and the SFT order the four 400B runs alike, and the gaps
after SFT are larger, 4.3 points between C2 and continued pre-training against 0.5 on the merge (Table 14
and Figure 60). On these four runs, the merge score thus appears to understate the gain of a mix after SFT.

2.3.4 Stage 3: Long Context Extension

Our strategy is to extend the context of Kolibri to 256k while preserving the capabilities of the mid-trained
model, by interleaving naturally long documents with high-quality mid-training data rather than training
only on long-context data. We take a fixed size of the naturally long document contribution from our pre-
training pool and ablate how aggressively the mid-training pool is reweighted towards longer documents.
Overall, increasing the share of length-reweighted mid-training data improves long-context retrieval
performance, while checkpoint merging largely preserves general capability.

2.3.4.1 Dataset Pool and Candidates

The long-context stage draws from three data pools. The first is the original mid-training mix, processed as
before with documents longer than 128k tokens removed, and about 70 % of its tokens lie in documents
shorter than 8k tokens. The second pool contains the same mid-training datasets without the document-
length limit, which allows sampling to shift towards longer documents from the same data sources.
The third pool is a dataset of naturally long documents between 8k and 256k tokens, from which we
remove the synthetically generated samples to avoid artificially favourable results on synthetic long-context
benchmarks such as RULER.

Every candidate is a mix of 200B tokens that takes one third from the naturally long pool, a share s from
the mid-training reweighted pool, and the remaining 2/3 − s from the natural mid-training distribution.
We materialise and train three settings, s ∈ {0, 1/4, 1/3}.

Length Reweighting In the reweighted share, each document-length band is assigned a weight on a

logistic curve over the band index b, which runs from 0 for documents up to 8k tokens to 5 for 128k to 256k

tokens:                  1
         w(b) = 1 + e−(b−4) .

The weight is 0.5 for the 64k to 128k band and 0.73 for the longest band.

The natural and reweighted shares both draw from the same mid-training datasets, so their sampling rates
must be combined into a single per-band budget to avoid assigning the same documents independently
to both shares. The resulting sampling rate for length band b is

         r(b) = min(rnatural(b) + q w(b), rmax),

where rnatural(b) is the sampling rate required by the (2/3 − s) share drawn according to the original
mid-training distribution and is zero above 128k tokens. The scale q is shared across all bands and is solved
by bisection such that the reweighted component contributes its target budget of s times the total mix
size. For a 200B-token candidate, the three components therefore contribute 1/3 × 200B tokens from
naturally long documents, s × 200B from the length-reweighted mid-training pool, and (2/3 − s) × 200B
from the original mid-training distribution.

Figure 15. Length distribution of the long-context mix candidates, as the materialised token budget of each share per document-length band
in percent of the mix’s tokens. s is the share of the mix drawn from the mid-training mix with the length reweighting, and the naturally long
documents portion is identical across the three candidates. The last panel is the production mix, the s = 1/3 recipe rebuilt on the production
mid-training pool at proxy scale.

No repetition cap rmax binds in any candidate, so no document is repeated. One scale for the whole pool
means the reweighted share is dominated by whichever sources hold long documents, so its topic mix
drifts away from the mid-training mix, which Figure 16 quantifies.

Figure 15 illustrates the resulting length distribution of each candidate as materialised budgets. Varying
the reweighted share s from 0 to 1/3 increases the fraction of tokens of documents longer than 32k from
25 % to 45 %, and of documents longer than 128k from 4 % to 13 %, while the naturally long documents
allocation stays identical.

Domain Shift Without controlling for domains, unconstrained length reweighting risks shifting the
domain distribution of the resulting mix. Figure 16 shows this effect by comparing the token-weighted
domain distribution of the reweighted production mid-training pools against the original mid-training mix.

Because long documents are concentrated in web text and scanned material, the English Language
domain rises from 1.3 % to 24 %. In contrast, instruction-following, problem-solving, and agentic domains
are short by construction and fall to about half their share of the original mid-training mix.

This shift is a consequence of the length reweighting rather than an explicit design choice, and the
candidates were not constructed to control for it. We therefore cannot isolate the effect of the domain

with the original mid-training mix.

shift from the effect of document length in the corresponding evaluation results. Future experiments
should treat domain distribution and length distribution as separate ablation dimensions.

2.3.4.2 Evals and Ablations

Setup The ablation primarily sweeps the share of mid-training data that is reweighted towards longer
documents. We train three long-context candidates with increasing reweighted shares, together with a
control using 100 % of the original mid-training mix.

All four branches fork from the same mid-training checkpoint after about 330B mid-training tokens and
train for 2087 steps at a 256k sequence length, corresponding to about 70B tokens, with 200 warm-up
steps and a constant learning rate. The data mix is therefore the only difference between branches.

Each branch is evaluated at nine checkpoints on RULER (Hsieh et al. 2024) and HELMET (Yen et al. 2025) up
to 256k tokens, as well as on the general-capability suite of the main run. We also evaluate a merged check-
point obtained by uniformly averaging the branch endpoint with three pre-fork mid-training checkpoints
spaced roughly 70B tokens apart.

Results We evaluate the candidates using an aggregate over RULER and HELMET: each leg is divided
by its mean over the four branches after subtracting its chance level, so 1 is the cohort average, then
context lengths are averaged within each task, tasks within each suite, and the two suites weighted equally.
As detailed in Table 15, the candidates are close overall: the last four raw checkpoints lie between 0.97
and 1.02 on the aggregate, while the merged checkpoints improve to between 1.09 and 1.13. Among the
merged checkpoints, s = 1/3 achieves the highest aggregate score.

                                                            40 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                      Aleph Alpha

         Merged checkpoint                                           Last four raw checkpoints

Mix      RULER HELMET Aggregate                                      RULER HELMET Aggregate

s=0      1.122  1.063                                       1.093    0.951   0.994   0.973
s = 1/4  1.125  1.067                                       1.096    0.972   0.998   0.985
s = 1/3  1.146   1.115                                      1.130    1.019    1.018   1.019
control  1.124  1.059                                       1.091     1.017  0.979   0.998

Table 15. Aggregate long-context results used to rank the mix candidates.

At the task level, differences become more visible at longer context lengths, with the largest separation on
RULER at 256k. Increasing the reweighted share generally improves retrieval and extraction tasks such as
QA, NIAH, and Word Extraction (WE), while VT moves in the opposite direction. On HELMET, JsonKV is
close to saturation across candidates, whereas RagQA, Rerank, and LongQA InfBench QA show clearer
separation. The control remains competitive despite seeing no long-context data, with the clearest benefit
from long-context extension appearing on retrieval tasks at the longest context lengths. Detailed task-level
results are reported in the appendix in Table 47.

We also track performance on the standard non-long-context evaluation suite, covering mathematics,
coding, reasoning, and general knowledge. Long-context extension slightly reduces general capability in
the raw checkpoints: the three long-context branches trail the control throughout the stage in Figure 17.
Checkpoint merging largely recovers this difference, with the four merged checkpoints indistinguishable
within the standard error of the general-capability composite.

No mixture dominates every task, but s = 1/3, the largest reweighted share tested, gives the strongest
merged aggregate while maintaining comparable general capability after merging. We therefore use this
recipe for the production long-context stage. Since the optimum may lie beyond the tested range, future
experiments should evaluate larger reweighted shares while controlling for the accompanying domain
shift.

The ablation candidates draw from an earlier 1T-token candidate pool of the mid-training mix; the pro-
duction long-context mix rebuilds the s = 1/3 recipe on the mid-training mix that Kolibri trains on, scaled
down to 250B tokens to fit the proxy scale training horizon, and the last panel of Figure 15 shows its length
distribution.

2.3.4.3 Quality Beyond the Training Context

We evaluate Kolibri beyond its trained context length and find that it retains useful long-context capabilities
up to 1M tokens, with task-dependent degradation.

The Kolibri base checkpoint trains on sequences up to 256k tokens. Its SWA layers use RoPE within a fixed
window, while its full-attention layers use no positional encoding, so evaluation beyond the training length
needs no positional rescaling. We test this extrapolation on RULER (Hsieh et al. 2024) from 128k to 1M
tokens against Nemotron 3 Nano Base (Figure 18).

At 1M tokens, Kolibri Base scores above Nemotron 3 Nano Base on needle-in-a-haystack, variable tracking
and question answering, but below it on word extraction. Its needle-in-a-haystack score declines with

Table 16. Pre-training benchmarks by category, in English and German versions where one exists. “—” marks a benchmark we evaluate in one
language only. For HELMET we evaluate only the JSON KV, RAG QA, RULER MK3, and InfBench MC subsets.

Settings. We implement all the benchmarks with eval-framework (Aleph Alpha Research 2026) with
identical prompts, few-shot configurations, and task settings. For the multiple-choice benchmarks, the
answer choices are represented using the formulation in the OLMES library (Gu et al. 2024). For the
generative benchmarks, we sample 1 response for TriviaQA, 4 responses on the maths benchmarks, and
16 for the code benchmarks.

Performance Aggregates. For each category and language, we report the mean score of its benchmarks.
We report the mean over the categories of a language as “Overall” score of that language. We do not
aggregate across languages because the benchmark sets differ by language. These aggregates help us
easily track progress during model training without having to track individual benchmarks.

Abstention and Uncertainty We additionally evaluate Kolibri Base on 7 English multiple-choice tasks
that add an “I don’t know” (IDK) option to each question, which test whether a model moves probability
towards abstaining rather than towards a wrong answer. We score them with the distributional correctness
score (T. F. Burns 2026), alongside accuracy and a ternary score that rewards correct answers, penalises
wrong ones, and treats abstention as neutral. Appendix F describes the tasks and metrics. On these IDK
evaluations, Kolibri Base has the second-highest mean distributional correctness score after Nemotron 3

                                                            44 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                                                        Aleph Alpha

Super 120B-A12B Base. Its largest margin is on MMLU-Pro, where every other compared model except
Nemotron 3 Super scores far lower.

2.4.2 Baseline Models

We compare Kolibri Base to a range of open base models spanning dense models from 7B to 70B pa-
rameters and MoE models with 3B to 12B active parameters: Apertus 70B (Project Apertus et al. 2025),
OLMo 3 7B and 32B (Ettinger et al. 2025), Nemotron 3 Nano 30B-A3B (NVIDIA 2025a), Nemotron 3 Super
120B-A12B (NVIDIA 2026a), Qwen3.5 35B-A3B (Qwen Team 2026a), GLM-4.5 Air 106B-A12B (GLM-4.5
Team 2025), and Gemma 4 26B-A4B (Gemma Team 2026), next to Kolibri Origin Base. All the generative
tasks listed in Table 16 are sampled with a temperature of 0.6, top-p of 0.6 for a maximum of 1024 tokens,
except Gemma 4 26B-A4B Base, which is sampled at the temperature 1.0 and top-p 0.95, as specified in
its model card.

Further details of the model checkpoint and vLLM deployment details are presented in Table 48 in Ap-
pendix E.1. Our experimental settings might differ from those of the original publications of the baseline
models. Thus, our results are a comparison of eight baselines under one protocol, not a reproduction of
published numbers.

the same 12B active parameters as Nemotron 3 Super, scores below Kolibri Base in both languages and is
therefore not on the Pareto frontier of Figure 19.

YaRN at factor 4 at every length, while Nemotron 3 Nano 30B-A3B Base supports up to 1M tokens natively.

Gemma 4 26B-A4B Base; beyond its trained window it degrades the least and has the best score at 1M

Part 3

Post-Training

Post-training adds instruction following, reasoning and agentic tool use capabilities to the base model.
SFT and RL train Kolibri towards the same capabilities. The paragraphs below outline the design choices
that guide both stages in five areas: German, long context, agentic use, retrieval and hallucinations, and
reasoning effort. Figure 2 compares the released Kolibri checkpoint with three open models, and Table 25
with the SFT checkpoint that RL starts from. RL improves on the SFT checkpoint across all capability areas
shown, and Kolibri performs on par with comparable open models. The two stages are then described in
detail in Sections 3.1 and 3.2.

German The Kolibri base model is trained on German data in pre- and mid-training (Table 9 and Sec-
tion 2.3.3.1). Post-training carries this capability over to instruction following, reasoning and tool use, so that
German-speaking users get fluent and correct German answers and reasoning traces they can follow.

For SFT, we therefore remove English reasoning from exchanges in other languages (Table 20) and add
synthetic German datasets to the SFT data pool. For these, teacher models generate German chat,
math, science and tool-calling data. A short German opener at the start of every teacher trace keeps the
reasoning in German (Section 3.1.1.1). We measure how the German share of the mix affects the German
and English scores on select benchmarks (Appendix H.2).

We continue this training in RL, where we translate existing environments into German and include a
language consistency reward for both the reasoning trace and the answer (Table 24). We observed
language consistency scores go up for the German benchmarks (Figure 34).

Long Context Many applications benefit from long-context capabilities, such as analysing lengthy legal
documents, scientific papers, and multi-turn dialogues where context from earlier interactions can be
highly relevant. We train Kolibri on in-house curated long-context data to reason over inputs of up to
256k tokens. For SFT, we generate conversations over documents of 128k to 224k tokens, each with four
requests whose answers draw on sections spread over the document (Section 3.1.1.2). In RL, the retrieval
environments add multiple-choice questions over documents of 95 000 to 230 000 tokens (Table 55).

Agentic Use We train Kolibri to perform well in agentic settings, which require tool calls and terminal
interactions. To help the model recover from failed tool calls instead of repeating them, we backfill reasoning
between consecutive tool calls where open agentic datasets have none and mask the loss on wrong tool

                                                                                      49 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

calls and terminal actions (Section 3.1.1.3). Beyond the available data, we generate two software-engineering
SFT datasets: in one, a teacher model resolves GitHub issues with a patch, and in the other, it fixes bugs as
a Terminus 2 agent in a terminal. In RL, we then train on tool-calling, software-engineering and terminal
environments (Sections 3.2.2 and 3.2.3.5). The system prompt, the coding harness and the user request are
randomised per group, so that the policy does not overfit to a single harness or phrasing (Section 3.2.2.4).

Retrieval and Hallucinations Many application areas of Kolibri require it to answer questions based on the
documents or knowledge stores of an organisation. Here, the model searches a corpus over several steps,
answers from the retrieved text, and abstains when these sources do not contain the answer. Both retrieval
and hallucination reduction are trained mainly in RL and cold-started in SFT on high-reward rollouts of the
same environments (Section 3.1.1.4).

For the information retrieval use cases of our customers, we build knowledge bases and multi-hop question-
answering tasks covering both German and English corpora across general knowledge and specialised
domains (legal, electronics, hardware and aviation). During training, we randomise application-side choices,
such as the retrieval parameters, models and methods and the task language, so that the model does not
depend on one setup (Section 3.2.2.3 and Appendix I.1.4).

To reduce hallucinations, especially when answering questions based on retrieved context, we employ the
Merlin–Arthur protocol (Deiseroth et al. 2026), which we formulate as an environment. The protocol is a
game between the model (Arthur) and two players that hide sentences of the retrieved context: Merlin
hides the sentences whose removal raises the probability of the gold answer most, and Morgana those
whose removal lowers it most. Each question is posed three times, with the full context, with Merlin’s
context and with Morgana’s, without marking which one it is (Section 3.2.2.3). The model is rewarded for
the correct answer in the first two cases and for abstaining in the third, and Merlin and Morgana recompute
their masks from the current policy at every step.

Table 19. Sentence the chat template writes into the system prompt for each reasoning effort, and the request values that select it.

Reasoning Effort In addition to no-think mode, Kolibri features three distinct reasoning effort levels, low,
medium and high. The SFT stage instils these effort levels by training on reasoning traces labelled with an
effort level derived from their length relative to requests of the same dataset and difficulty (Section 3.1.2.2).
The RL stage then trains on all three effort levels, focusing on high effort, and further separates them via
the length-dependent completion_budget format signal of Section 3.2.2.2.

                                                            50 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

The chat template signals a chosen effort level to the model by adding a plain text section to the sys-
tem prompt. After the user’s system instructions and before any tool definitions, a section headed
# Reasoning effort holds one instruction per effort (Table 19). The user cannot change the wording
of this instruction through the API. If a request sets both reasoning_effort and enable_thinking,
reasoning_effort takes precedence.

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                        Aleph Alpha

3.1 Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT)

                       GPQA Diamond         AIME 2026             IFBench          LongBench Pro

                                      Median completion tokens per sample

Figure 20. Score of the Kolibri SFT checkpoint on a selection of benchmarks against the median number of tokens it generates per sample,
with one point per reasoning effort from none to high. The marker size grows with the reasoning effort. The German counterparts of the
English benchmarks are depicted by a dashed line and square markers.

The SFT stage introduces new capabilities, such as instruction following, reasoning at a requested effort
level, and tool calling. This stage serves as a cold start for RL: it establishes desired behaviours that the
model must produce at a sufficient rate for RL to become effective in improving them (DeepSeek-AI 2025).
SFT of Kolibri proceeds after the long-context training stage (Section 2.3.4) with cross-entropy loss on the
assistant turns (Section 3.1.4). The released Kolibri SFT checkpoint, the weight average of two runs trained
on different mixes (Section 3.1.4.3), is the input checkpoint to the RL training phase. Its performance on
German and English benchmarks across different reasoning efforts is depicted in Figure 20.

Terminology SFT establishes these capabilities by training the model on data that demonstrates them.
A data pool is the set of all curated datasets (Section 3.1.2.3). A generated dataset starts from seeds,
prompts or documents, from which a teacher model writes the completion. We use a user simulator to
write the follow-up user turns (Sections 3.1.1 and 3.1.1.1). During generation we, at times, utilise backfilling
to add a generated reasoning trace to an assistant turn that has none (Section 3.1.1.3), and reasoning
prefills, short openers that start the teacher model’s reasoning trace. A training run trains on a mix, a set
of datasets and their sampling weights. To define training mixes, we use MergeMix (Section 3.1.3) during
which datasets are grouped into clusters and a specialist is fine-tuned on each cluster (Section 3.1.3.1). A
weighting assigns a coefficient to each cluster specialist and determines how they are averaged. The set
of all valid weightings forms the simplex from which we begin sampling at a reference mix, a hand-set
mixture of datasets. We train one run per candidate mix and combine resulting checkpoints by souping,
which averages the weights of runs trained on different mixes into one model (Section 3.1.4.3).

                                                                                       52 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

3.1.1 Datasets

We legally assess, compile, and process an extensive pool of fine-tuning data that combines openly
available datasets, open data in the following, with synthetic data we generate. For some open datasets, we
regenerate the completions, because their answers fail our audits (Section 3.1.1). Additionally, we generate
datasets in-house for domains that are not sufficiently covered by open data, e.g., German (Section 3.1.1.1),
long context (Section 3.1.1.2), agentic tasks (Section 3.1.1.3), retrieval (Section 3.1.1.4), and safety, compliance,
and alignment (Section 3.1.1.5).

The main models we use to generate this data, and to regenerate parts of the open datasets, are GLM-
5.2 (Z.ai 2026b), GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c) and Qwen3.8-27B (Qwen Team 2026c). We employ different
models for different regeneration and judging tasks as they have different strengths. For example, smaller
models are generally faster for long-context tasks, but larger models are more suitable for generating
strong reasoning traces. They also have different values and biases, which we consciously select or avoid
(Section 3.1.1.5).

All samples undergo the same processing pipeline, which removes overlap with our evaluation benchmarks
(Section 3.1.2.1) and labels each user-assistant exchange with a reasoning effort. Section 3.1.2.3 describes
the resulting dataset pool. The final SFT training utilises a subset of the dataset pool found by applying a
procedure called MergeMix (J. Wang et al. 2026), which proposes candidate training mixes by weighting
clusters of datasets per domain (Section 3.1.3).

Regenerated Completions Training on wrong or low-quality completions directly teaches the model
undesirable behaviour, so we check candidate datasets before they enter the pool. Sample audits of
several candidate datasets found many completions of mixed quality, including wrong answers to science
problems, which led to regressed benchmark performance if included in training. For these reasons,
we completely regenerate completions for three subsets. We use seeds from Nemotron-Instruction-
Following-Chat-v1 (NVIDIA 2025c) and Nemotron-SFT-Instruction-Following-Chat-v2 (NVIDIA 2026c) and
generate new completions using both GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) and GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c). For the reasoning-
off split of Nemotron-SFT-Instruction-Following-Chat-v2, we generate completions without reasoning, so
that the pool also holds answers for the none effort level (Section 3.1.2.2). Each conversation is extended
for up to 50 turns. We employ a user simulator to write follow-up turns in the style and intent of the
original user, and have a teacher model answer them. Open data holds few long conversations, so we
generate them. The extension trains the model to keep following instructions and earlier context over
many turns. Nemotron-SFT-Science-v2 (NVIDIA 2026d) consists of synthetic multiple-choice questions,
whose completions we regenerate with Qwen3.8-27B (Qwen Team 2026c).

3.1.1.1 German

Large open post-training corpora such as Nemotron translate prompts and answers into other languages,
German among them (NVIDIA 2025d), while keeping the reasoning traces in English. However, a user who
does not read English cannot follow a chain of thought in English, which degrades the user experience

Figure 21. Artefacts that machine translation carries from an English reasoning trace into a German one. Left, the English trace of a reasoning
model on a German problem; right, its machine translation into German. Highlights mark four kinds of artefact, numbered at their first
occurrence in each trace. 1 Meta-language: the trace states that the problem is in German and that the answer has to be German.
2 Repetition: glosses of the German prompt for an English reader become tautologies such as “Brötchen sind Brötchen”. 3 Decimal-comma
aside: the trace explains the German decimal comma, which a German reader does not need. 4 English notation: the arithmetic keeps 0.45
and 16.2, while the prompt and the answer write 0,45 and 16,20.

of non-English native speakers. Translating English reasoning traces into German leaks artifacts of the
English trace, such as passages it quotes from the German prompt and English notation like a decimal
point in place of the decimal comma (Figure 21). We instead generate samples in which prompt, reasoning,
and answer are German; tool schemas, calls, and results stay unchanged. In ablations at target scale
(Appendix H.2), a model trained without this data reasons in English on German AIME’26, and a small
share of it switches the reasoning to German at the cost of more loops without an answer. Upsampling the
German data 16× yields more answers that are correct and German and a higher English AIME’26 score,
with a German AIME’26 score that does not differ from the model without German data.

Data Generation Pipeline We generate German data for chat, math, science, and tool use. The German
alignment and safety data are described with their English counterparts (Section 3.1.1.5). For each domain,
we employ a teacher to write a German prompt based on a seed and generate a response. Seeds may be
English samples, documents, or a collection of task attributes. Where a seed carries a reference answer,
the response is verified against it.

Without intervention, reasoning models tend to reason in English even on German prompts. We therefore
prefill the teacher model’s reasoning trace with a short German opener, such as “Gegeben ist:” (“We
are given:”) or “Der Nutzer möchte” (“The user wants”), and let the teacher continue from it. We call this
technique reasoning-prefill distillation. Each opener is randomly picked per sample from a curated list
per task domain, so that the model does not learn one fixed opening phrase. An instruction in the system
prompt alone keeps 67 % of the teacher’s traces German throughout, and the prefilled opener raises this
share to 97 % (Figure 22). A similar technique has been used in on-policy self-distillation to keep student
and teacher from drifting into English during RL (Y. Liu et al. 2026). We apply it offline to a separate teacher
to generate SFT data. Finally, a heuristic gate checks that both reasoning and answer are German, and
an LLM language healer fixes the errors it finds in the assistant turns. A sample whose trace remains
malformed or switches language keeps its answer without reasoning, as in the shared post-processing

Figure 22. Steering the language of the teacher’s reasoning on German prompts, shown on a math problem. Both variants send the same
system and user turns. Left, the system prompt instructs the model to reason in German, and the model opens the think block itself; its trace

(Table 20). Its answer can still be a good one, and as a sample without reasoning it trains the none effort
(Section 3.1.2.2).

Chat General chat consists of multi-turn conversations seeded from user turns of open chat datasets and
from generated everyday questions. These conversations are continued by a user simulator that deepens,
constrains, contradicts, or changes the topic, which trains the model to follow instructions that change
over a conversation. GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c) writes the replies with reasoning, and Qwen3.8-27B (Qwen
Team 2026c) those without. User turns stay casual, as in deployment, with lower case, typos and missing
punctuation. Chat replies have no reference answer, so filtering them by quality would require an LLM
judge. We skip this filter, as it adds compute without a known benefit. Guha et al. (2025) find that none of
the answer-filtering methods they test on reasoning data, LLM verification included, improves on training
with all answers.

Tool Calling A user simulator asks German questions about entities drawn from the category trees of
German Wikipedia, and GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) answers them by searching and reading Wikipedia articles
through tool calls. Both the entities and the tool calls query the live German Wikipedia at generation time
rather than a fixed dump. The model thus learns to search and reason in German over German sources.
Several such conversations are concatenated into long contexts that end with a request for an essay tying
their topics together, which requires facts spread over the whole context and adds German long-context
data. The same trajectories also enter the data pool as knowledge answers without tool calls.

English tool-calling conversations of Toucan (Xu et al. 2025) and Nemotron-SFT-Agentic-v2 (NVIDIA 2026b)
are translated to German, with tool schemas, calls and results left unchanged. GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c)
replaces their English reasoning with a new German trace for every assistant turn. Each reasoning trace is
verified against the tool call or answer that follows it. Every tool-calling sample enters the data pool once
with and once without its reasoning traces.

                                                                                       55 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

Math and Science The seeds for the German math and science data are hard problems from several
open math and science datasets. All of these problems come with a reference answer. A problem counts
as hard if the reasoning trace of its source is at least 20 000 characters long for math and 10 000 for
science. We apply GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c) to rewrite each problem in natural German without seeing the
reference, so that the German problem does not leak the answer. We then reapply GLM-5.3 to solve the
problem, and the answer is kept only if it matches the reference. Multiple-choice science questions keep
their option letters, so the answer is compared by letter. Math conversations are optionally extended to
multiple turns with generated user follow-ups, which trains the model to handle follow-up questions on
the same problem.

3.1.1.2 Long Context

The long-context extension of Kolibri (Section 2.3.4) trains the base model on documents of up to 256k
tokens. We add long-context samples to SFT to preserve the model’s long-context retrieval ability and
to extend it to reasoning over long inputs, such as large documents, code bases, and tool outputs in
agentic applications. Such tasks go beyond retrieving facts from a long input and require summarisation,
multi-hop reasoning and the use of distant information in reasoning traces.

As open datasets rarely provide long-context samples, we generate our own long-context dataset (Fig-
ure 23). We use the same pre-processing as pre-training (Appendix D.1.1) and select from a corpus of
curated documents with token lengths between 128k and 224k. This corpus also includes a collection of
German legislative documents. Papers and German legislative documents are expected long-context
inputs in Kolibri’s target applications.

We split a sampled document into about 40 sections at coarse boundaries such as page breaks, blank
lines, line breaks or, failing those, windows of 400 characters. Qwen3.8-27B (Qwen Team 2026c) receives
the document, an index of its sections and a prompt, and writes four different simulated user requests
about the document, each tied to the sections it needs, or to none for a request about the whole document
(Figure 23). A request tied to distant sections must combine distant passages rather than retrieve a single
fact. Requests about the opening of a document alone would not train the model to use the rest of a
long input, so the prompt asks for requests spread over the whole document. We measure this spread by
dividing each document into five parts of equal section count. In 77.4 % of the conversations, the requests
together draw on sections from at least three of the five parts. Because model performance degrades with
input length (K. Hong, Troynikov, and Huber 2025), each simulated request is answered in a separate call
that receives only this request and the sections it is tied to, or the whole document for a request about the
whole document. The resulting requests, reasoning traces and answers are appended to a conversation
beginning with the seed document, so that later turns rely on the document and on the earlier exchanges,
as in multi-turn use. This conversation forms a single training sample: the teacher model answers most
requests from a few sections, while the model trained on the sample sees the full document.

The dataset holds around 78k conversations over as many documents from various open corpora. In this
collection, around 11 % of the samples are German. In a proxy-scale ablation, adding this data improves
AA-LCR and LongBench Pro, with the largest gains at the longest contexts (Appendix H.1).

Figure 23. Generation of one long-context conversation from a seed document. A seed document with a length between 128k and 224k

3.1.1.3 Tool Calling and Agentic Coding

The agentic data of the mix consist of open agentic datasets and two software-engineering datasets that
we generate. The following paragraphs describe how we format the trajectories of the open datasets and
how we generate our own.

Trajectory Formatting and Reasoning Backfilling Tool-calling models tend to repeat a failed call instead
of recovering from it (Junhao Su et al. 2026), and reasoning between actions lets an agent track its plan
and handle exceptions (S. Yao et al. 2023). Thus, we add reasoning to tool calls where it is missing, in a
step we call backfilling. A teacher model writes reasoning that leads to the recorded tool call, which stays
unchanged. The reasoning is kept only if a second LLM call derives the same tool call from it alone. These
reasoning-backfilled conversations enter the data pool along with their non-reasoning counterparts. This
ensures that the requested reasoning effort decides if the model reasons, and not the source dataset. We
empty the content of every assistant turn with tool_calls. Every step then holds only the reasoning,
the tool calls and their results, which unifies the pattern that leads to a tool call across dataset sources.

Finally, we mask the loss on clearly wrong tool calls: calls whose tool response reports an unknown tool or
invalid arguments, and exact repeats of the previous call that never receive a different response. These
turns stay in the context, so the model learns the correction that follows without being trained to produce
the error, as in Fu et al. (2025) and Slinko et al. (2026).

Agentic Software-Engineering Open datasets provide trajectories that resolve issues in real repositories
and are verified by their tests. To increase the amount of such trajectories in our pool, we generate two

                                                            57 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

agentic datasets on the repositories of SWE-rebench V2 (Badertdinov et al. 2026), which also give the
software-engineering and terminal environments of RL a cold start (Section 3.2.2). SWE-rebench V2
provides the issues, the repository snapshots and the test environments; we generate the trajectories
and, for the terminal tasks, the bugs. For GitHub issues, GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) explores a snapshot of the
repository with read-only tools and answers with a patch, without ever seeing the reference patch, so that
the trajectory shows how the fix is found.

For terminal tasks, GLM-5.3 (Z.ai 2026c) injects a small, realistic bug into a Python repository and writes a
bug report from the user’s point of view. Injected bugs turn any repository with tests into a verifiable task,
beyond its existing issues. A bug is kept only if at least one test fails and at least half of the tests still pass,
so that it is detectable but local. Another GLM-5.3 instance then fixes the bug in the Terminus 2 harness
of Terminal-Bench (Merrill et al. 2026), and we keep the trajectories that pass the tests. Repositories of
SWE-bench Verified (Jimenez et al. 2024) are excluded from both datasets, as we evaluate on them.

3.1.1.4 Retrieval and Hallucinations

The retrieval and the hallucination datasets train the model to answer on the basis of document collections
typical at organisations and companies and to abstain when these do not contain the answer. Both are
rollouts of RL environments and provide a cold start for RL. The environments and their rewards are
described with RL (Section 3.2.2.3 and Appendix I.1.4).

Retrieval and Search We roll out GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) in the retrieval environments on their English and
German training tasks, with the randomisation of the environments applied per episode. We keep a rollout
when an LLM judge, Gemma-4-31B (Gemma Team 2026), accepts its answer, the gold answer appears in
the retrieved text and the transcript ends on an answer. In addition, every tool call must name a served tool,
and the episode must earn the full format reward. The search must also be efficient: no duplicate calls, at
most 30 % near-duplicate queries, at most three searches per required hop, and at most two searches
after the evidence was found. These limits train the model to stop searching once it has the evidence,
which keeps latency and cost low. We also check that the reasoning of every kept trace is in the language
of the user prompt, so that German users can follow it.

Reducing Hallucinations To train the model to abstain when the context does not support an answer, we
hold out a small set of the samples of the Merlin–Arthur RL environment as seeds (Section 3.2.2.3). An
8B proxy model that we trained on the Merlin–Arthur RL environments computes Merlin’s and Morgana’s
contexts for each seed. The same model generates completions on the full context and on both masked
contexts (Deiseroth et al. 2026). We keep a seed when its completions answer correctly on the first
two contexts, abstain on Morgana’s and pass a Command A+ (Cohere 2026) judge. Each of its three
completions enters the data pool as its own sample, so that the same question appears with and without
its evidence and abstention depends on the context, not on the question.

                                                            58 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

3.1.1.5 Safety, Compliance, and Alignment

Post-training largely determines how a model answers harmful, value-laden, and law-related requests
(Ouyang et al. 2022; Bai et al. 2022). A model trained only to follow instructions also follows harmful ones,
and a small share of safety data in the mix reduces this (Bianchi et al. 2024). We therefore add both English
and German open safety datasets to the SFT mix. The mix also holds examples in which hypothetical
users ask the model in various ways to produce copyrighted material and teach the model to reject such
requests. For legal questions that name no jurisdiction, models built in the US or China often assume
US law for English prompts and Chinese law for Chinese prompts (Zhizhi Wang and Suresh 2026). We
acknowledge that several of our teacher models were built in China, so they carry known political biases
on sensitive topics, while our model should adhere to the values of the democratic consensus in Europe.

Value Alignment Many requests call for a judgement that depends on values. For these, the model
should judge by the values of the broad democratic consensus in Europe. We curate a set of documents
whose content mostly lends itself to a discussion of values and their consequences, such as the impact of
a technology or the consequences of a political decision. An LLM proposes up to five questions about
each of these documents. The questions are phrased as everyday requests without words such as “ethical”
or “moral”, as users rarely state that a request touches on values. The model thus learns to apply these
values without such a cue. Documents that support no such questions are discarded. Open-source
models answer the questions under a system prompt that centres human dignity, human rights, liberal
democracy, the rule of law and pluralism. The prompt instructs the model to present positions across
the democratic spectrum fairly and to keep facts, uncertainties and value judgements apart. In-house
question-answer pairs on politically sensitive topics add to this data and counter the known political biases
of teacher models on these topics (Section 3.1.2.1).

3.1.2 Data Pool Curation

The datasets above differ in format, origin and quality, so we curate them into one training pool in three
steps. A shared processing pipeline brings every sample into one canonical format, drops conversations
that claim another model’s identity or follow Chinese Communist Party (CCP) narratives, and removes
rows that reuse benchmark text (Section 3.1.2.1). Each reasoning trace then receives a reasoning effort
label from its length relative to traces of the same dataset and prompt difficulty (Section 3.1.2.2). The result
is the final data pool, which we break down by domain, source and language (Section 3.1.2.3).

3.1.2.1 Shared Processing

Every dataset of the pool, open or generated, is processed using the same steps (Table 20). Each step
either drops a whole conversation or rewrites it in place. The open datasets arrive in different formats:
some carry tool calls, some place the reasoning inline in the answer, and many have no reasoning at all.
The rewrites bring every sample into a common schema so that all datasets combine into one training set.

                                                            59 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

Most open datasets are generated by other models, and responses often carry their identity, such as “I
am DeepSeek” or “As Qwen, I”. A pattern filter drops every conversation in which the assistant claims to
be another model or to come from another lab, unless it also names our own model, and ignores denials
such as “I am not Llama”. Answers of teacher and source models can also follow known political biases
on sensitive topics, such as narratives of the CCP. For datasets with open-ended answers, we judge the
whole conversation, including reasoning traces, with gpt-oss-120b (OpenAI 2025) to classify each as either
carrying a CCP narrative, a refusal on general safety grounds, or being balanced. Conversations of the
first kind are dropped.

Table 20. Post-processing steps every dataset of the SFT data pool passes, in the order they run. The list is not exhaustive: some datasets
pass additional filters or transformations, and some steps change nothing in datasets whose source already satisfies them.

Decontamination Benchmark items in the training data inflate a model’s scores beyond its performance
on new inputs in real-world use (Magar and Schwartz 2022; S. Yang et al. 2023). To avoid this, we scan the
SFT data pool against our benchmarks and drop the rows that reuse benchmark text.

We build on the decon engine of Olmo 3 (Allen Institute for AI 2025; Ettinger et al. 2025) and match
word n-grams with n = 5. Published procedures use n from 7 to 13 (A. K. Singh et al. 2024), which suits
pre-training text, where a long match marks a copied passage. In post-training data, a request can copy a
benchmark item in a few words, and a long match is often boilerplate (Abdin et al. 2024; A. K. Singh et al.
2024).

Short n-grams also match benchmark instructions that repeat across its items, such as “Answer with a
single letter”, which hold no benchmark question (Brown et al. 2020; Abdin et al. 2024; A. K. Singh et al.
2024). Olmo’s decon weights each n-gram by its IDF (Spärck Jones 1972) over all benchmarks together.
We compute the IDF within each benchmark instead, so wording common to a benchmark counts for
little. Of 90 such instructions, our filter raises 0 false positives at the same recall, against 57 with the IDF
over all benchmarks.

Because a long row shares more n-grams with a benchmark question by chance alone, we score the row
in windows and keep the highest window matching score. Each window is 8 times as long as the question,
so its size scales with the length of the question.

To set the threshold, we plant lightly altered copies of benchmark items (case, spacing, light paraphrasing)
into a sample of the SFT data pool, weighted by the share of each dataset. We take the highest threshold
that still finds them at a recall of 0.99. At this threshold, the filter drops 1.0 clean rows per 10 000, against
3.5 to 171 for n from 7 to 13 (Figure 25). Paraphrasing can still get past this filter (S. Yang et al. 2023).

Before MergeMix (Section 3.1.3), the filter removes 1097 of 22 000 436 rows of the 57 pool datasets
(0.0050 %, Figure 24). Training the same SFT recipe with and without these rows shows no measurable
difference on the benchmarks they match.

Figure 26. Assignment of reasoning effort labels to conversation turns of each sample from the SFT data pool. We first grade the difficulty of

each conversation by its prompt with a Large Language Model judge. We then count the reasoning length L of each exchange, the number

of reasoning tokens from one user turn to the next. For each reference group g (a combination of a dataset and a difficulty), we compute 30th
and 70th percentiles ℓg30 and ℓg70 of L. To post-hoc label a given turn of conversation, the effort level is assigned based on the relative position
of L to the 30th and 70th percentiles ℓg30 and ℓg70. Rather than a hard cut-off, we use smooth blending (Equation (12)) around the bucket
edges 0.3 and 0.7. This effort label is then rendered as part of the system prompt; an exchange without reasoning is labelled none.

Longer reasoning at test time raises accuracy on hard problems (Wei, Xuezhi Wang, et al. 2022; Kojima et al.
2022; OpenAI 2024; Snell et al. 2025; DeepSeek-AI 2025). However, reasoning models also spend many

                                                                                   61 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

tokens on easy problems (X. Chen et al. 2024), which adds latency and cost without improving the answer.
Prior work trains a model to decide whether to reason at all (J. Zhang et al. 2025), or controls the reasoning
length through a token budget in the prompt (T. Han et al. 2024), budget forcing at inference (Muennighoff,
Zitong Yang, et al. 2025) or a length target rewarded during RL (Aggarwal and Welleck 2025). Similarly,
we aim for a reasoning length that adapts to the prompt by itself, while the user keeps control over the
trade-off between answer quality and latency.

Kolibri is a single hybrid model that either answers directly or reasons at one of three effort levels, similarly
to Qwen3 (Qwen Team 2025a), Llama-Nemotron (Bercovich et al. 2025) and gpt-oss (OpenAI 2025). The
chat template renders the requested level as one of four fixed sentences in the system prompt, for none,
low, medium and high (Table 19). Unlike dedicated effort tokens, whose meaning has to be learned from
scratch, the sentences describe each effort in words that the model can follow before any effort training.
The SFT traces carry no effort label, so we assign one post hoc. As summarised in Figure 26, the assigned
label depends on the length of a reasoning trace relative to the other traces of the same dataset and
prompt difficulty. Post-hoc labels can still act as a control, because the mix contains several completions
of the same prompt: 171 021 of its 1 794 428 distinct opening prompts (9.5 %) appear with two completions
that both contain reasoning. In 53 723 of these pairs the two reasoning lengths differ by a factor of two or
more, and under the thresholds and the soft draw described below about 37.3 % of the pairs receive two
different labels. Within such a pair, the label is the only part of the input that differs, so training ties the
difference in reasoning length to the label rather than to the prompt.

Domain- and Difficulty-based Effort Labelling Each effort level should work in every domain, so the
training data must contain every effort level in every domain. We thus investigate the reasoning length L,
the cumulative number of reasoning tokens generated in an exchange, the span between two successive
user turns. We map turns with higher reasoning length to higher reasoning effort. For the datasets that
contain reasoning (76 datasets in an earlier, larger version of our SFT data pool), the median reasoning
length ranges from 27 to 90 223 tokens. Thus, a global threshold under our assumed rule would label
almost every short chat-like prompt as low reasoning effort, and almost every mathematical proof as
high. Therefore, we adopt dataset-specific reference groups.

Notably, reasoning length also depends on the difficulty of the prompt. Empirically, we find that the
reasoning length for a fixed percentile of the hard prompts is up to 31.3 times that of the easy ones. We
therefore fit the thresholds per reference group g = (dataset, difficulty). The dataset stands in for domain
and task format, because each dataset of the mix comes from one source with one task shape. Grouping
by dataset thus separates proofs from tool calls without a domain classifier, and the difficulty grade refines
the group within the dataset.

We grade the difficulty of each conversation with GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) as the judge model, following the
LLM-as-a-judge approach (Zheng, Chiang, Sheng, et al. 2023). The judge grades a conversation as easy,
medium, or hard, based only on the knowledge and reasoning its prompt requires to be solved correctly.
Grading the whole pool with GLM-5.2 is too expensive, so GLM-5.2 grades 100 000 conversations, and
we distil these verdicts into a cheaper student model called Poacher-2B, as W. Liu et al. (2024) did for
their complexity scorer. Poacher-2B then grades every conversation of the pool four times, and we round

efforts cover overlapping but ordered ranges of length, and the same effort covers different lengths in
different domains. Because the thresholds are set per difficulty, a user does not have to estimate the
difficulty of their prompt when choosing a reasoning level.

none, the model emits an empty reasoning block. The median reasoning length orders low < medium
< high on 23 of the 24 benchmarks with a measurable length. Figure 28 splits this by the difficulty that
Poacher-2B assigns to the evaluation prompts of 11 graded benchmarks. With the effort fixed, hard prompts
receive about 32.5 times the reasoning of easy ones; with the difficulty fixed, high receives between 1.4
and 3.7 times the reasoning of low. The effort thus moves the length within the range the prompt calls for,
and does not override it. We average the score over the 14 benchmarks whose generations fit the length
limit. Reasoning at low raises this mean from 38.4 under none to 64.9, and high reaches 67.8, with a
paired standard error of 0.8 points against low. The step from medium to high adds 0.7 points.

The reasoning effort also works the same way in German and English. On 11 German benchmarks the
median reasoning length orders low < medium < high on 10, and the benchmark median rises from 326
to 1788 tokens, a factor of 5.5; the 13 English benchmarks rise from 1160 to 2993 tokens, a factor of 2.6.
German prompts thus receive shorter reasoning at every effort, which matches the data pool: the median
reasoning length of its German exchanges is 368 tokens against 2078 for English. Figure 20 plots the
score against the median completion tokens per effort for a selection of benchmarks, with the German
counterparts of GPQA Diamond and AIME 2026 as dashed lines.

multi-turn splitting (Section 3.1.4.2) and rendering with our Kolibri chat template. The 142.4B loss tokens,
42.4 % of the total tokens, are the unmasked tokens that provide the gradient signal. Most samples are in
English (92.6 %) or German (5.0 %); the remaining 2.4 % are in other languages, including 0.8 % that mix

3.1.3 Data Mixing with MergeMix

The final SFT model trains on a targeted mix drawn from the data pool, not on the whole pool. To find a
strong mix, we group the datasets into 20 clusters by the capability they train (Section 3.1.3.1) and search
for the share of each cluster. Fitting these shares from trained candidate mixes needs at least 3(m + 1)
training runs for m clusters (M. F. Chen et al. 2026). In our case, these are 63 SFT runs of 16.78B tokens
each. MergeMix (J. Wang et al. 2026), which we also use in mid-training (Appendix D.2.1), needs only one
shorter training run per cluster, of 4.50B tokens. We use MergeMix to train on 90B instead of 1057B tokens,
i.e., 91 % less training compute, fine-tuning one specialist model per cluster. To score a candidate mix, we
average the specialists’ parameters, each weighted by its cluster’s share in the candidate mix, and evaluate
the merged model. After the specialists are trained, each candidate costs one evaluation instead of one
training run. We find that averaging works here because models fine-tuned from one checkpoint stay
close in parameter space (Wortsman, Ilharco, et al. 2022). Berasi et al. (2026) select SFT mixes of two to
four domains in the same way.

We first check that merged models rank weightings as trained models do in SFT. For this, we merge a
math, a code, and a chat specialist with 12 weightings and train a model on the same mix for each of the 10
weightings that are not a single specialist (Appendix H.3). On AIME 2026 and LiveCodeBench, merging
and training rank the weightings alike; on MATH-500, the scores barely change with the weighting. We
conclude that the merges are a usable proxy and apply the method to the 20 clusters of the full SFT mix
(Section 3.1.3.2). With 20 clusters, the merges find good candidates but do not rank them: most weightings
the search finds train above the hand-set baseline, but their merged scores do not predict which of them
trains best. We therefore train the most promising weightings and select the final mixes by their trained
scores (Section 3.1.3.3).

3.1.3.1 Clusters and Specialist Models

We run the sweep on an earlier version of the SFT data pool, with 63 datasets and 232B tokens. Unless
stated otherwise, this subsection counts the tokens of data in the same way as Section 3.1.2.3. We group
the datasets by their capabilities into 20 clusters: math, math proofs, code, software engineering, science,
general chat, instruction following, safety, tool calling, interactive agents, terminal use, long context and
German, where the larger capabilities span several clusters. We choose 20 as the finest grouping along
capabilities in which every cluster still holds a few billion tokens of data. The smallest cluster holds 4.55B
tokens. Each specialist is a model fine-tuned on 4.50B packed tokens of a single cluster, fewer than the
smallest cluster holds, so no specialist sees its data more than once. The specialists train from the last raw
checkpoint of the Kolibri long-context training (Section 2.3.4), with the SFT ablation recipe (Appendix H.4),
shortened to the specialist budget and without cooldown.

3.1.3.2 Searching over Cluster Weightings

We search for the weighting of the 20 clusters whose merge scores best, where a weighting is a set of 20
non-negative shares that sum to one. The set of weightings is too large for a grid, so, as in mid-training

(Appendix D.2.1), we draw weightings from Dirichlet distributions whose concentration κ sets how close
the draws stay to the centre. The first round centres on the baseline weighting, which gives each cluster
the share of packed tokens its datasets receive in the reference mix, a hand-set SFT mix that fixes the
epochs of each of the 63 datasets. It draws 43 weightings, at κ = 50 close to the baseline and at κ = 15
further away. The second round draws 25 weightings around the best merge of the first, at κ = 25. With
the baseline, we score 69 merges.

All merges and the 20 individual specialists are scored on 14 benchmarks, most of them from the post-
training suite (Section 3.3.1), grouped into seven capabilities. Among them are math (AIME 2025, AIME
2026 and AIME 2026 IID), code (LiveCodeBench), agentic work (SWE-bench Verified, a Terminal-Bench
subset (Merrill et al. 2026) and multi-turn BFCL), instruction following (IFBench), knowledge (GPQA Dia-
mond and MMLU-Pro), long context (LongBench Pro and AA-LCR) and German (the German translation
of AIME 2026 and KulturBank, Appendix H.5). The score of a cluster weighting is the mean across all seven
capability averages, weighting each capability equally regardless of its benchmark count.

3.1.3.3 From Merges to Trained Mixes

Among the 69 merges, the baseline weighting scores 50.2 % and ranks 49, while the best merges of the
two rounds reach 59.2 % and 61.4 %. We then train 8 candidate weightings as data mixes (Table 53). The
candidates are: the baseline as reference, draws from both rounds including the best merge of each, and
two weightings proposed by surrogate models fitted to the merge scores (Table 22). Like the specialists,
each model trains from the Kolibri base model with the ablation recipe (Appendix H.4).

Training confirms that the search finds better mixes overall: 6 of the 7 other weightings train above the
baseline, and the best reaches 56.7 % against 51.5 % for the baseline. However, it does not confirm the
ranking of the merges. The best merge trains worst, at 50.0 %. The best trained weighting is a first-round
draw from the middle of the merge ranking. Overall, merged and trained scores do not correlate (Spearman
ρ = − 0.09). In the validation, the merges rank the weightings as training does, but in the sweep they do
not. The sweep merges 20 specialists instead of two or three, and each specialist trains on 4.50B instead
of 20.0B packed tokens. Our experiments do not show which of these two differences breaks the ranking.

                                                            67 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

3.1.4 Kolibri SFT Training

The SFT stage starts from the Kolibri base model and trains for 4000 steps, around 268B tokens, on
packed sequences of 256k tokens (Section 3.1.4.2). At this length, the cost of a sequence depends on how
its tokens split into conversations, since attention cost grows with the square of each conversation’s length.
With packing alone, every rank waits for the rank with the most expensive sequence. Ordering the packed
sequences by attention cost on every rank, without communication between ranks, raises the throughput
by 84 % (Section 3.1.4.1). MergeMix leaves four candidate mixes within a few points of each other, so we
train each for longer, continue the best two to full length and soup them; the soup scores above both of its
runs (Section 3.1.4.3).

3.1.4.1 Data Loading

We follow common practice and pack several conversations into one sequence of fixed length with
document-masked attention (Krell et al. 2021; Dao 2023), so no token attends across a conversation
boundary. Conversations are assigned online with Next-k-Fit (Johnson 1974): each goes into the first of k
open bins that still has room, and if none has, we emit the fullest bin and open a new one. Larger k trades
memory and start-up time for less padding. In ablations at a sequence length of 32k tokens, raising k from
1 to 1024 lowers the share of padding tokens in the inputs from 14.8 % to 0.6 %. Less padding means more
tokens per optimiser step, so the learning rate is tuned for the chosen k (Goyal et al. 2017). Kolibri trains
with k = 256 bins at a sequence length of 256k tokens with a share of padding tokens of 0.001 %.

With bin packing, every sequence is equally long, but not equally expensive. Attention cost grows with

the square of each document’s length, so a sequence holding one long conversation costs far more than

one holding many short ones. Each data-parallel rank draws its sequences independently. Since all ranks

synchronise after every microbatch, all ranks have to wait for the rank with the most expensive sequence,

resulting in wait times and idle compute. Similar to workload balancing for variable-length training (Zheng

Wang et al. 2025; Ge et al. 2025), but without communication between ranks, every rank buffers b packed

sequences and orders them by their attention cost

                      ∑︂
                      C(s) = |d|2, xn = s(π(n)), n = 1, . . . , b,   (13)

                      d∈s

where |d| is the length of a document d in sequence s, s(i) the i-th sequence of the buffer of size b sorted
by descending C, π a permutation of 1, . . . , b drawn from a seed shared by all ranks, and xn the n-th
microbatch a rank draws from the buffer. Because all ranks sort buffers of the same size drawn from the
same distribution, the i-th sequence of each buffer has a similar cost on every rank, so all ranks process
similarly expensive microbatches at the same time. Each rank fills a second buffer while it draws from the
first and switches to it once the first is exhausted, so sorting does not stall the training.

On 64 B300 GPUs with the Kolibri SFT configuration, a buffer of b = 256 raises the median throughput per
GPU from 8610 token s−1 without the ordering buffer to 15 850 token s−1, by 84 %.

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                          Aleph Alpha

Setting                 Value

Steps                   4000

                        the last 800 steps to a tenth of the peak

Table 23. Training settings of the SFT stage. Sequences are packed to the full context length, so a step covers 67.1M tokens. The optimiser
groups, their momentum and their weight decay are those of pre-training (Table 6).

We train with cross-entropy loss only on the tokens the model produces at inference: the reasoning, answer
and tool calls of each assistant turn. User turns, tool responses and the special tokens of the chat format
are masked out. The loss is normalised over the whole batch, so every supervised token has the same
weight and neither packing (Section 3.1.4.1) nor multi-turn splitting re-weights the mix. The chat template
drops the reasoning of earlier assistant turns, so a conversation with reasoning cannot be supervised on all
its turns at once. We therefore split it into one sample per user turn, with loss on the last turn only; earlier
turns keep their tool calls and answers but lose their reasoning. On average, a conversation yields 2.27
samples. A shuffle buffer keeps the samples of one conversation from dominating a batch.

3.1.4.3 Candidate Runs and Souping

This stage selects the SFT model we pass on to RL. The MergeMix sweep leaves four candidate mixes
within a few points of each other at the ablation budget (Section 3.1.3.3), so we compare them at the length
of a full SFT run and soup the best of them. Alongside, we train a baseline mix, B0, that samples the datasets
of the final pool about naturally, with the German data upweighted. It is a sanity check that reweighting
the domains improves on natural sampling. Starting from the Kolibri base model (Section 3.1.4), we first
fine-tune one model per mix for 2500 steps, 168B packed tokens. Two of the four candidates give clearly
the best of these models, which we call C1 and C2. Over the benchmarks of Figure 30, they reach means
of 59.9 ± 1.8 % and 55.7 ± 2.1 %, against at most 52.6 ± 1.9 % for the other three models. At full length, the
candidates thus spread further apart than at the ablation budget. We continue training C1, C2 and B0 to
4000 steps, 268B packed tokens.

Souping The three mixes weight the domains differently, and their models lead on different bench-
marks: C1 on AIME 2026 and LiveCodeBench, C2 on AA-LCR (Figure 30). To combine these strengths,
we soup (Wortsman, Ilharco, et al. 2022) every pair of the three models: each parameter of a soup is the
mean of its values in the two models. Averaging works because both models are fine-tuned from the
same base checkpoint and stay close to each other in weight space (Wortsman, Ilharco, et al. 2022). It
is the operation of the MergeMix sweep, applied to two full runs instead of specialists. Maiti et al. (2025)

show that a soup of models strong in different benchmark categories can score above each of them on
multilingual, tool-calling and math benchmarks. We soup only pairs, with equal weights.

Results Every soup scores above both of its models on the mean (Figure 30). The soup of C1 and C2
reaches the highest mean, 63.4 ± 1.8 %, against 58.9 ± 1.8 % for C1, the best single model, and 30.0 ± 2.0 %
for the base model. B0 scores lowest of the single models, so the candidate mixes pass this sanity check. On
AIME 2026, the soup scores below C1, within noise. Selection and these results use the same benchmarks,
so the scores of the selected soup are optimistic.

Math Data Guha et al. (2025) find that math scores, AIME among them, rise with the amount of math
reasoning traces in SFT. Yet C1 scores highest on AIME 2026, although math makes up only 4.9 % of its
tokens, against 18.3 % for B0. Compared with B0, C1 drops or downsamples in particular the math datasets
with on average easier problems and longer reasoning traces. B0, which keeps these datasets, reaches
69.4 ± 9.3 % on AIME 2026, within noise of the base model before SFT at 74.6 ± 10.7 %. On AIME 2026 IID,
the base model scores exactly the same, 74.6 %, so its AIME 2026 score does not stem from memorised
problems. One explanation is that the long reasoning traces lengthen the generations of the model until
they no longer finish. In line with it, B0 ends more AIME 2026 generations in a repetition loop or at the
context limit, 7.3 %, against 2.1 % for the base model and 0.0 % for C1. Other causes are possible, as C1 and
B0 also differ in other domains.

As the best model on the mean, the soup of C1 and C2 after 4000 steps becomes the released SFT
checkpoint and the starting point of RL.

                                                            70 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                   Aleph Alpha

3.2 Reinforcement Learning (RL)

While SFT adapts the base model towards user interaction and tool use, RL further expands its capabilities
by training it on its own rollouts against verifiable rewards. In this section, we describe the RL training
stage that starts from the SFT checkpoint. It consists of a single training run on 38 environments that
span math, coding, reasoning, instruction following, tool use and agentic tasks in English and German
(Section 3.2.2). We train asynchronously on a token-level objective that combines a policy-gradient term, a
squared log-ratio regulariser (Kimi Team 2026a; Team et al. 2025) and targeted On-Policy Self-Distillation
(OPSD) for low-reward groups (S. Zhao et al. 2026), as outlined in Section 3.2.1. For the policy-gradient term,
we use group-relative advantages (Shao et al. 2024; Z. Liu et al. 2025), a Binary Total Variation (B-TV) trust
region (P. Qi, X. Zhou, et al. 2026) and a per-token bound on the gradient norm of the logits. The trainer
further replicates the FP8 quantisation and expert routing of the inference engine to keep both policies
numerically aligned. We push FP8 weights to all inference replicas via Remote Direct Memory Access
(RDMA) after every optimiser step without discarding in-flight requests, and serve tens of thousands of
concurrent rollouts with isolated code sandboxes, as described in Section 3.2.3. Section 3.2.4 presents
the Kolibri RL run, its results and an analysis of its training dynamics. Finally, Section 3.2.5 summarises the
lessons we learned over several iterations of our RL setup, from early experiments to Kolibri Origin and
Kolibri.

Terminology For each prompt, we sample a group of sequences, where each sequence consists of the
complete single- or multi-turn rollout for this prompt. A rollout step comprises multiple model output
tokens, and the environment response that follows it, e.g., a tool response. A turn comprises all rollout
steps between two user messages, such that one user message may be followed by several rollout steps
with tool calls before the model responds to the user. A sequence may consist of several segments, which
are several rollout steps that extend the same tokenised context. We split sequences into segments due
to, e.g., chat-template changes that adapt previously rendered contexts (Section 3.2.3.2). These splits can,
but need not be along turn boundaries. We perform optimiser steps on batches of sequences, regardless
of their number of segments. We only train on the tokens that are newly generated by the model in each
segment, masking out re-rendered context as well as system, user and tool-response tokens.

3.2.1 Training Recipe
