Group-Relative Advantages Given a group G of sequences yi, we compute the group-relative advantage

of each segment as

                      Ai,e = Ri,e − R¯, with R¯ =                    1 ∑︂ 1 ∑E︂j     Rj,e.                             (15)

                                                                     |G| j∈G Ej e=1

Each generated token t inherits the advantage of its segment, i.e., At = Ai,e for tokens t of segment yi,e.
The mean reward R¯ is computed on a sequence level, over the Ej segments of each sequence yj, to
prevent segment splits from overweighting individual sequences. Sequences that fail due to infrastructure
errors, e.g., in the environment or the inference engine, are removed from their group before computing
the baseline. Similar to Dr. GRPO (Z. Liu et al. 2025), we skip the advantage normalisation originally used by
GRPO (Shao et al. 2024). Following Decoupled Clip and Dynamic Sampling Policy Optimization (DAPO)’s
dynamic sampling (Q. Yu et al. 2025), we generally drop groups in which all sequences obtain the same
environment reward. However, we keep groups with a pass rate of 1 to apply format penalties to passing
sequences, and only drop the group if its sequences all incur the same format penalty. We re-sample
dropped groups two additional times from the same environment to keep environments with high drop
rates from being under-represented in training.

Policy-Gradient Term To correct for the off-policy shift of asynchronous training (Section 3.2.1.5) and the

training–inference mismatch between the inference engine and the trainer (Section 3.2.1.3), the policy-

gradient term weights each token’s advantage by the policy ratio. For a sampled token with probability

pθ,t under the training policy and pµ,t under the inference policy used to generate it, this ratio is rt =

pθ,t/pµ,t. Following Divergence Proximal Policy Optimization (DPPO) (P. Qi, X. Zhou, et al. 2026), we replace

standard PPO-style clipping (Schulman et al. 2017) with a B-TV mask. Given thresholds ϵ+ and ϵ−, this

mask suppresses token updates when the sampled-token probability moves too far from the inference

policy in the direction favoured by the advantage, i.e.,

The left panel of Figure 31 shows Gt, i.e., the norm at unit advantage. For on-policy sequences, rt = 1

and thus Gt ≤ 1. However, when training and inference policy diverge, the norm grows with the policy

ratio and becomes unbounded for pµ,t → 0, such that a single token with a low inference probability and

moderate training probability can dominate the update. The B-TV mask does not remove such tokens,

since it only acts on absolute probability changes in the direction favoured by the advantage, independent

of the actual policy ratio. We therefore directly bound the norm by scaling our objective as

                                                                                           (︃ (︃                 )︃)︃
                                                                      ct = stopgrad min 1, Gmax ,
                            ℓtPG                  =  ct  ℓ˜PG,                                           Gt

                                                          t

where Gmax bounds the logit gradient of the policy-gradient term at Gmax|At|, leaving tokens below the
bound unchanged. Computing ct comes at effectively no additional cost, as it only requires the two
sampled-token probabilities that the trainer already computes. Equivalently, the bound caps the effective
policy ratio ctrt at Gmax/(1 − pθ,t), which compensates the softmax factor 1 − pθ,t of Equation (17). To first
order, this process also bounds the change of the sampled token’s probability, as Appendix I.3.3 shows.

                                                                             73 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

In contrast, ratio-based bounds such as the importance-weight cap of Clipped IS-Weight Policy Optimiza-
tion (CISPO) (MiniMax 2025) limit rt regardless of the training probability, and thus ignore the softmax
factor. They down-weight tokens with a high training probability even though their logit gradient may be
small, while tokens with a low training probability can exceed the norm bound before they reach the cap.
As the second panel of Figure 31 shows, a CISPO cap of 2, which matches the norm bound at low inference
probabilities, is far more restrictive at high ones. The common cap of 5 in turn allows much larger gradients
than the norm bound near the on-policy diagonal at low inference probabilities, as the right panel shows.

The B-TV mask (P. Qi, X. Zhou, et al. 2026) of Equation (16) zeros out only one side of the diagonal band
−ϵ− ≤ pθ,t − pµ,t ≤ ϵ+ in Figure 31. In particular, it still keeps a token whenever the advantage is negative
and the training probability is higher than the inference probability, in which case the norm bound scales
down the token’s gradient. In addition to this per-token bound, we clip the global gradient norm.

3.2.1.3 Training–Inference Consistency

While training and inference policies share their weights, they compute token probabilities with different
engines, kernels and numerical precisions. The resulting training–inference mismatch between inference
probabilities pµ,t and training probabilities pθ,t causes recently generated sequences to be instantly off-
policy, which in turn can destabilise training (F. Yao et al. 2025; P. Qi, Z. Liu, et al. 2025). We therefore align
both engines as much as possible, and additionally rely on several regularisation mechanisms outlined in
Section 3.2.1.1. That being said, the engines use different attention, MoE and matrix-multiplication kernels,
and none of the kernels are batch-invariant (H. He and Thinking Machines Lab 2025), which causes small
mismatches. Rollouts sample from the full distribution at temperature 1. We do not use top-p or top-k
truncation, so the trainer directly computes pθ,t from its unmodified logits.

FP8 Quantisation-Aware Training Serving the policy with FP8 weights and an FP8 KV cache raises the
throughput of rollout generation, which can dominate the cost of RL, by a factor of two to three in our
runs, while also improving per-request decode speed. If the trainer uses unrounded BF16 weights and
activations, however, the rollouts come from a different policy than the one being optimised. This training–
inference mismatch biases the policy gradient, and we observed RL training diverge in this configuration.
The trainer therefore uses FP8 quantisation-aware training (Jacob et al. 2017), using the same quantisation
as the inference engine. Appendix I.4.1 provides details.

Routed-Expert Replay The inference engine records the experts that its MoE routers select for every
generated token, allowing the trainer to replay these choices (W. Ma et al. 2025). Tokens without recorded
routing, such as padding, use the routers of the trainer. Full activation checkpointing (T. Chen et al. 2016)
recomputes each layer with the same experts in the backward pass.

                                                            74 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

3.2.1.4 Auxiliary Objectives

Squared Log-Ratio Regulariser  We add a squared log-ratio term (Kimi Team 2026a) to softly penalise
policy drift as                           ℓtreg = τKL(︁log rt)︁2,

where τKL controls the strength of the regulariser. This term acts on every trainable token, including those
with an active B-TV mask, thus following the DPPO loss of PRIME-RL (Prime Intellect 2025). We do not
employ a KL term to a frozen reference model, and do not add an explicit entropy bonus to our objective.

Targeted On-Policy Self-Distillation We additionally employ targeted OPSD (S. Zhao et al. 2026) for
selected multi-turn environments to provide a dense learning signal for groups with low reward variance
and low environment rewards, which would otherwise have been dropped by the group-relative objective.
Of these groups, we randomly pick a rollout which has the most turns with a tool response error. Each
error class (listed in Table 60) is associated with a targeted remedial hint, which we add to the sequence
context as an additional message exactly at the turn where the error occurred (similar to Cursor 2026).
Then we recompute the full-vocabulary distribution for every token of the failed completion under the
current training policy. The resulting hinted distributions act as the teacher, and we minimise their forward
KL divergence to the distribution of the training policy under the original, unhinted context as

                                   ℓtOPSD = DKL(︁stopgrad(︁πθ( · | st, ht))︁ ⃦⃦ πθ( · | st))︁ ,

where ht denotes the remedial hint and stopgrad stops the gradient towards the teacher. For these rollouts,
we disable the RL loss. A single group can thus only contribute to either the group-relative RL or OPSD part
of Equation (14). Different from S. Zhao et al. (2026), the teacher is the live training policy and its privileged
context provides dense teacher distributions without requiring a ground-truth solution. We specialise
OPSD to tool-use and terminal environments. Appendix I.6 gives the hyperparameters, and Table 61 lists
these environments. Table 60 lists all failure classes with their remedial hints.

3.2.1.5 Asynchronous Training

Rollouts and optimisation run continuously and asynchronously. Rollout threads drop groups without
learning signal, such as groups whose sequences all receive the same reward (Section 3.2.1.1), and every
other group enters the replay buffer as soon as it finishes. The staleness of a group is the number of
optimiser steps since its oldest sequence started. The trainer samples groups from the buffer at random
and without replacement, so each group is used in at most one optimiser step. After every optimiser step,
the weights of all inference replicas are updated in flight (Section 3.2.3.4): active requests keep their KV
cache and continue under the new weights, so different policy versions can generate parts of the same
sequence. The prefix cache can likewise survive a weight update, so a new request may reuse KV entries
computed by an older policy. Training is therefore only approximately on-policy. We bound the off-policy
shift by evicting groups from the buffer once they exceed a maximum staleness (Table 59), and the policy
ratio of Section 3.2.1.1 corrects for the remaining shift.

                                                            75 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                                           Aleph Alpha

Balancing the Loop The training loop runs at the speed of its slowest part. If groups are produced faster
than the trainer consumes them, the trainer is the bottleneck. If the trainer consumes groups faster than
they are produced, e.g., when either inference or the environments are saturated, it idles. Environments
with saturated resources leave both the inference engine and the trainer underused. Ideally, environment
capacity is large enough that environments are never the bottleneck, and the GPUs are split between
trainer and inference so that the loop is balanced, producing groups as fast as they are consumed. We
therefore allocate nodes such that the trainer is barely the bottleneck during the later stages of training.
Appendix I.4.2 explains how adding GPUs to a balanced loop shortens the optimiser steps but not the
rollouts, which increases staleness. As a remedy, we scale the batch size with the GPU count.

3.2.2.1 Reward Structure by Environment

Figure 33 shows the verifier and the reward shape of every environment. The frozen judge model (Sec-
tion 3.2.3) is used only where the graded object is natural language, a conversation, or the model’s intent: it
grades math proofs, simulates the user and grades natural-language assertions for the customer-service
environment, grades safety refusal, and decides on answers and abstentions that miss the exact match.
Verifiers that execute untrusted or unbounded code on the rollout workers (Section 3.2.3) run in a killable
process pool with a timeout, so a wedged check scores zero without stalling training. Appendix I.1.1 details
the reward of each environment.

3.2.2.2 Environment-Specific Format Signals

Beyond solving the task defined in each environment, we expect the model to produce well-formatted out-
puts by, for example, making well-formed tool calls and keeping its language consistent across thinking and
content blocks. We compile a list of output format requirements in Table 24. Each of these requirements
defines a format signal that influences training reward through the aggregation described next.

Combining Multiple Rewards Format signals act multiplicatively on environment reward. They are only
applied to positive rewards to prevent training on groups of episodes that do not solve the environment

                                                                 77 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                       Aleph Alpha

task but vary in the quality of their output format. Since SFT already trains the model to produce well-
formatted output, format signals are sparsely active in practice. As a result, they need to be aggregated
in a way that allows a single format signal to have a significant impact on the final reward, while ensuring
that environment reward remains the primary training signal, even if multiple format defects are found
simultaneously.

Let sk ∈ [0, 1], k ∈ {1, . . . , K} denote one of K format signals, where sk = 1 signals good format and
smaller values signal a format defect. We fix constants fk ∈ (0, 1] that configure the strength of format
signals relative to each other and rescale the signal to gk = fk + (1 − fk)sk ∈ (fk, 1].

The final reward Ri of environment i is computed by scaling the environment reward Renv i > 0 with a

p-norm aggregation of format signals

                      ⎡                                     ⎛ (︄                        )︄1/p⎞⎤

                      Ri = Rienv ⎣mmin + (1 − mmin) exp⎝−            ∑︂  [− log gi,k]p  ⎠⎦ .                                (18)

                                                                     k

The global multiplier mmin sets the minimum fraction of the environment reward that is retained irrespective
of output format, and the aggregation norm p controls how quickly simultaneous defects compound. For
Renv i ≤ 0 no format reward signals are applied.

                      and no generated token matches the banned-script pattern.

3.2.2.3 Environment Summaries

German Environments To prevent the model from regressing on German reasoning, we translated our
English environment data into German, and include a language consistency format signal (Section 3.2.2.2).

Figure 34 shows that, while the SFT checkpoint reasons in German on 89 % of German AIME 2026
(Mathematical Association of America 2026) prompts, the RL training results in 99 % German reasoning.
At the same time, accuracy on German AIME 2026 rises from 75 % to 91 %, while accuracy on the English
prompts moves from 92 % to 96 %, closing most of the gap between the two.

100 %               Language consistency                                                                  Accuracy

                                                                      70 %
         SFT                                                          60 %

Figure 34. What the German environments buy: held-out benchmark scores of the SFT checkpoint and of ten RL checkpoints trained with the
German environments in the mix. Left: share of responses whose reasoning and answer are in the language of the prompt. Right: accuracy
on the same benchmarks. AIME 2026 is 30 problems sampled 16 times each; both axes average over all samples. German AIME 2026 moves
from 89 % to 99 % in language consistency and 75 % to 91 % in accuracy by step 1000, while English AIME 2026 accuracy moves from 92 %
to 96 % and its language consistency stays at 100 %.

Long-Context Question Answering To retain long context capabilities, we added a long-context envi-
ronment which poses select-all-that-apply questions over the seed documents of the SFT long-context
conversations (Section 3.1.1.2), 95 000 to 230 000 tokens each, as one further turn appended to the
conversation. Appendix I.1.2 describes data generation and filtering.

Retrieval-Augmented Question Answering Our retrieval environments are designed to reflect the prac-
tical needs of retrieval-augmented question answering in real world use-cases. To this end, we pair a mix
of general corpora and specialised corpora (in English and German), with randomisations over a range
of configurations of the provided tools, as well as alternative prompt formulations, and multi-hop task
synthesis using GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b). This setup familiarises the model with the kind of data and questions
agentic RAG applications involve, and teaches it to solve multi-hop questions with search tools. We provide
details in Appendix I.1.4.

Hallucination Reduction Environments Our Merlin–Arthur (Deiseroth et al. 2026) environments aim
to reduce hallucinations by teaching the model (Arthur) to abstain from answering questions that are
unanswerable due to a lack of information in the provided context. During training, the environment
generates unanswerable samples from answerable ones by masking a sample’s context using a helpful
agent (Merlin) and an adversarial agent (Morgana). The environment rewards the model for answering

                                                            79 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                              Aleph Alpha

truthfully on the former, and abstaining on the latter sample. It also rewards a correct answer on the
unmodified sample, so we present each sample in three variations. Merlin and Morgana mask the least
and most important sentences, respectively. We approximate the importance of a sentence by measuring
the difference in the probability the current model assigns to the ground truth answer when the sentence
is masked, relative to the probability of the ground truth answer under no masking. We give more details in
Appendix I.1.3.

Political Balance, Copyright and Safety We built an environment that rewards balanced answers on
topics where source models show known political bias (Section 3.1.2.1). The environment presents the
model with a politically sensitive prompt and uses an LLM judge to assign reward based on whether the
assistant answer is balanced. Some political prompts in the dataset also present plausible grounds for
safety refusal. The model scores full reward for answering helpfully and in a balanced way, or if it refuses
to answer a prompt that presents grounds for refusal. This environment is narrowly focused on Chinese
political content and does not address other political biases. Copyright refusal environments prompt the
model in German or English to produce copyrighted material, either directly or by asking for inferences
from it. The environments then reward the model for refusing to produce copyright-protected material,
with refusal detected by an LLM judge. We also include a safety alignment environment in training that
presents the model with unsafe prompts and scores refusal with an LLM judge.

To make the model robust to various deployment scenarios, we randomise environments along three
axes: the system prompt, the coding harness and typos in user prompts (Figure 35). The user request has
about 5 % of its lowercase words misspelled, sparing the words the verifier depends on. Environments
with system prompts in both English and German draw the language of the system prompt per group,
independently of the language of the user request, so the user request alone sets the expected language.
The instruction-following environment applies no language-consistency signal, since its requests can ask
for an answer in a language other than their own; its verifier checks that language instead.

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                Aleph Alpha

Every draw comes from the randomisation seed of the group, so the sequences of a group still face an
identical task, which the group-relative advantage of Equation (15) requires.

3.2.2.5 Training Curriculum

Without a curriculum, the rollout threads would draw an environment by its weight (Figure 32) and then
uniformly sample a prompt within it. However, prompts that are too easy or too hard carry no learning
signal, since a group is dropped when all of its sequences carry the same reward (Section 3.2.1.1). Such
prompts become more common as the policy solves more of them. The curriculum therefore focuses on
prompts whose groups carry learning signal, and across environments, it scales down the weight of an
environment that runs out of such prompts. The training batch follows this adapted distribution, up to the
effects described at the end of this section. Table 59 in Appendix I.5 lists the curriculum hyperparameters.

Difficulty Buckets and Pass Rates The curriculum sorts the prompts of each environment into three
buckets by their estimated pass rate, namely hard prompts with an estimate of 0, solved prompts with an
estimate of 1, and trainable prompts in between. A per-bucket target weight cb sets the share of prompts
drawn from bucket b, and is set highest for the trainable bucket. A prompt starts with its offline pass rate,
the mean reward of a reference model’s samples (Appendix I.1), or with a default value where it has none.
Groups sampled at high reasoning effort replace the estimate with the group’s mean environment reward,
whether the group is kept or dropped, so a group in which every sequence fails moves the prompt to the
hard bucket. Groups at low or medium effort, and groups in which every sequence was removed due to
an infrastructure error, leave the estimate unchanged.

Drawing a Prompt Within an environment, the curriculum draws a bucket with probability proportional

to its weight and takes the prompt at its head, the one evaluated longest ago. The weight of bucket b is its

target weight cb, scaled down when the bucket cannot supply it,  (︂ nb )︂

(︁ )︁
wb = cb min σb, γb , σb = min 1, B .

The supply term σb lowers the weight of a bucket that holds fewer prompts nb than the B groups of a

batch. The recency term γb keeps the curriculum from redrawing hard and solved prompts that were just

evaluated: it grows linearly with the staleness (Section 3.2.1.5) of the last evaluation of the bucket’s head

and saturates at 1, and it is 1 for the trainable bucket and for a head never evaluated.

Drawing an Environment An environment is drawn with probability proportional to ωeVe, i.e., to its sam-
                                                ∑︁

pling weight ωe times the sum Ve = b wb of its bucket weights. While every bucket supplies its target
weight, Ve is 1. Thus, an environment without trainable prompts is drawn rarely and returns to its sampling
weight once re-evaluated prompts become trainable again. Figure 41 shows the buckets over the Kolibri
run.

Preserving the Environment Mix Rollout threads draw an environment per group rather than per unit of
time, so the training batch follows the curriculum’s draws even though rollout durations differ widely: a

slow environment holds its thread for longer, but it is not drawn more often. Three effects still skew the
batch. All threads start at once, so fast environments finish first, which biases early training steps towards
shorter environments and sequences. A thread that drops a group retries the same environment with a
new prompt only twice (Section 3.2.1.1), which under-represents environments with many drops. The replay
buffer evicts groups that exceed the staleness limit, which is more likely to trigger for slow environments.

3.2.3 Infrastructure

3.2.3.1 Cluster Layout

Our RL training runs on 256 NVIDIA B300 GPUs, split into 32 nodes with eight GPUs each. All nodes are
scheduled together and placed within a single InfiniBand topology domain. Figure 36 shows how we split
the GPUs between training, inference and judging on a node level. A centralised controller places the
trainer, the inference replicas and the CPU-side services on their nodes, and coordinates them via Monarch
(Meta PyTorch 2025a). The controller node hosts the main training loop execution, the global router for the
inference replicas and sandbox executors, the curriculum and the replay buffer processes. The CPU-side
services run on the same nodes as the GPUs. Every node except the controller’s hosts a rollout worker
that steps through the environments, as well as a sandbox service that executes model-generated code
via Apptainer (Kurtzer, Sochat, and Bauer 2017). The global router on the controller node only selects the
replica or sandbox executor for each request. The rollout workers then send their inference, judge and
sandbox requests to it directly, so no request or response data passes through the controller. Additional
environment services for the retrieval environments run via Apptainer containers on host 1. Table 58 in
Appendix I.5 lists the hardware, precision and optimisation settings for our experiments.

                                                             82 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

3.2.3.2 Rollout Generation

We allocate 144 GPUs on 18 nodes to inference. Each GPU hosts an independent vLLM (Kwon et al. 2023)
replica of the full model in FP8 precision, and is used to gather both training and validation rollouts. To
generate the rollouts, we run 45 concurrent rollout threads per inference replica (Table 58). We split them
evenly across the 31 rollout workers, rounding up to 210 threads per worker. Each thread draws a prompt
according to the curriculum of Section 3.2.2.5 and subsequently steps its group through the environment.
For each group, we sample a random reasoning effort level of Table 19 and additionally decide at random
whether the model’s reasoning traces are preserved across turns. If they are not preserved, removing
them re-renders the previous turns, which starts a new segment. The routing decision is made on a group
level by the global router on the controller node. It ensures that all rollouts of a group of sequences and the
subsequent steps of each rollout land on the same inference replica. A sequence is marked as truncated
when a generation reaches its completion-token budget or length limit or if a later step overflows the
context window. Truncated sequences get 0 reward. Most environments define their own step budget
and grade a rollout as usual once the budget is exhausted. For those that do not, we define a global step
limit that also acts as truncation.

Segments Before each step, the rollout worker renders and tokenises the full conversation with the
model’s chat template. If the resulting tokens no longer extend the current segment, e.g., because reason-
ing traces were removed or generated text re-tokenises differently, we end the segment and start a new
one. The new segment re-renders the context, such that future tokens see a consistent context between
training and inference. It then masks this context for the training steps to ensure that each generated
token is only trained on once. In particular, we are not using the typical Token-In, Token-Out (TITO) principle
(Gallouédec and Rasul 2026) where generated text is never encoded twice and new tokens are always
only appended. This choice is motivated by downstream usage during deployment, where the text might
be re-encoded between turns. However, splitting the sequences increases memory usage and optimiser
step times, since it significantly increases the number of segments and thus cumulative tokens for some
groups. Splits caused by malformed text or stray special tokens in a generation are penalised via the Stable
rendering format signal of Section 3.2.2.2.

Judge We allocate two nodes to host frozen replicas of a judge model to use for automatic grading
and user simulation in some environments, as detailed in Section 3.2.2.1. We choose Qwen3.5-122B-A10B
(Qwen Team 2026a) in FP8 as the judge. For efficiency reasons, judge requests use greedy decoding
without reasoning. If a judge reply fails for a given sequence, the sequence is removed from its group.

3.2.3.3 Trainer

The trainer builds on our fork of TorchTitan (Liang et al. 2024), which pre-training also uses. It shards
the model across 96 GPUs on 12 full nodes via FSDP (Y. Zhao et al. 2023). In contrast to the pre-training
setup in Section 2.1.6, it uses no expert or other parallelism besides FSDP. It uses the optimiser groups
of pre-training (Table 6), i.e., Muon (Jordan et al. 2024) for the attention and expert weights and Adam

                                                                                       83 /189

Figure 37. Weight push from the trainer to the inference replicas. At startup, the receive buffers are mapped to the trainer parameters and a
trainer rank is assigned for each parameter, balancing the bytes per rank. After every optimiser step, the trainer ranks push the new weights via
RDMA into the single receiving replica of each node, from where they are broadcast to the node’s other replicas over NVLink. The conversion
on the trainer side is batched and pipelined, and the intra-node NVLink distribution reduces load on the inference node’s NICs.

(Kingma and Ba 2015) for the embedding, norm scales, LM head and router. Each group maintains its own
learning rate, and we do not use weight decay. Table 58 provides details. The trainer uses full activation
checkpointing.

Segment Packing Before each optimiser step, the collator splits the segments of the batch into fixed-size

bins. The bin size exceeds the model’s context window, such that each segment fits into a single bin.

We determine the number of bins with a greedy longest-first heuristic for bin packing, rounding up to a

multiple of the number of ranks. We then sort the segments and, starting with the longest, assign them to
                                         ∑︁ (︁              2 )︁
the bin P with the lowest estimated cost j∈P Lj + Lj /κ that still has capacity. Here, Lj is the number

of tokens of segment j, and κ is the ratio of the active parameters to the query dimension summed over

the full-attention layers, such that L2j /κ is the FLOP cost of full attention relative to the linear layers. Each

rank processes its bins as consecutive microbatches and accumulates their gradients. Thus, the number

of microbatches grows with the number of tokens in the batch.

Checkpointing The trainer asynchronously saves a checkpoint consisting of model weights, optimiser
states and learning-rate schedules at regular intervals. The checkpoints additionally track the curriculum
state, including the pass rate estimate of every prompt. The replay buffer is not saved, such that a resumed
run starts with an empty buffer that is refilled with rollouts of the restored policy. The Kolibri RL run saves a
checkpoint every 25 optimiser steps, although only the last one is used for benchmark evaluation. The run
did not experience interruptions, so no resumes were required.

3.2.3.4 Data Transfer

Batch Transfer We use one-sided RDMA for the transfer of training batches from the replay buffer to
the trainer ranks. Once a group is finished, the rollout workers send it over Monarch RPC to one of the

                                                                                      84 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

replay buffer’s shards. The replay buffer stores identical prefixes between two sequences only once to
reduce storage overhead. To draw a batch, the collator splits the batch size across the shards with a single
multivariate hypergeometric draw over their sizes, such that the batch follows the same distribution as a
draw from a single buffer. The shards then write their sampled groups via RDMA into one registered host
buffer of the collator. After packing the bins, the collator writes the microbatches of each trainer rank to
that rank via RDMA. It stages them in a double buffer, filling one half while the other is being sent. Once all
writes complete, the collator sends each rank the layout of its microbatches, i.e., the offset, shape and data
type of each tensor. The rank then reads its tensor in-place.

Weight Transfer Our weight transfer follows the RDMA weight transfer of Perplexity (Perplexity 2025;
L. Chen 2025).

At startup, each vLLM worker registers its parameters for RDMA transfers once and sends the addresses
to the controller. From these addresses, the controller computes a routing table. It maps each trainer
parameter to its vLLM counterpart, taking fused projections into account. It then assigns each parameter
to one trainer rank, balancing the bytes per rank. The weight transfer supports FSDP and EP on the trainer
side and Tensor Parallel and EP on the inference side. Additionally, only one inference replica per node
receives the weights via RDMA.

After every optimiser step, the trainer pushes updated weights to the inference replicas, as shown in
Figure 37. The ranks all-gather each parameter in batches from its FSDP shards, and some projection
weights need to be fused for vLLM’s single tensor layout. Each parameter is quantised to FP8 on the
assigned rank and our single-GPU replica deployment skips the splitting of weights into a per-replica
layout. Each rank subsequently writes its pieces via RDMA to the receiving replica of every node, while
all ranks already gather the next parameters within a fixed memory budget. This process overlaps the
writes with the preparation of the next parameters and the gather of further parameters within the batch.
The writes are tagged with the policy version, so that once all writes have arrived, each receiving replica
converts the weights in place into its kernel format and broadcasts them over NVLink to the other replicas
of its node. This broadcasting setup alleviates the bottleneck of the node’s NICs, as the entire bandwidth
can be used to receive the weights of only one replica instead of being spread across eight replicas.

3.2.3.5 Sandboxes

Most tasks in the software-engineering environments have a dedicated container image, so a run may use
tens of thousands of images. Since pulling and unpacking every image on every node would overload the
shared scratch filesystem, we convert each image on first use to a single-file Apptainer image (Kurtzer,
Sochat, and Bauer 2017) and cache it on shared scratch. Each non-controller node runs one sandbox
executor on the CPUs that the run’s nodes keep idle, so runs never wait on a shared sandbox pool. Figure 38
shows the resulting stack on one node. All sandboxes of an image on a node share its SquashFS root,
which the executor mounts read-only via squashfuse. Harness tools that a task image lacks, such as
tmux, come from a second read-only layer (Appendix I.2). On top of these shared layers, each sandbox
adds its own size-capped tmpfs writable layer through OverlayFS, so no image is copied or extracted.

Figure 38. Sandbox stack of the RL run on one worker node. Task images are converted once to single-file Apptainer images on shared
scratch. Sandboxes of the same image share its read-only SquashFS layer on the node, and each adds its own writable tmpfs layer. The right
panel shows one sandbox: the OverlayFS root, the long-running Apptainer instance, and the egress proxy for the allowlisted hosts.

The sandbox itself is a long-running Apptainer instance that lives for the whole rollout. Every command of
the rollout runs inside it via apptainer exec, so files and processes persist between steps. To isolate it
from the node and from other sandboxes, the instance runs in an unprivileged user namespace with its
own mount, process and network namespaces. For environments that install dependencies, an egress
proxy in the network namespace allows only package registries, GitHub and Hugging Face.

3.2.4 Kolibri RL Training

3.2.4.1 Setup

We start from the SFT checkpoint of Section 3.1 and train for 1000 optimiser steps using Muon and Adam
at a base learning rate of 1 × 10−5 with a linear warm-up over 50 steps. Each batch contains 256 groups of
size 8, i.e., 2048 sequences. A sequence may consist of up to 100 rollout steps. The model is trained on
contexts of length up to 256k. Tables 58 and 59 in Appendix I.5 and Appendix I.6 provide a detailed list
of training hyperparameters. We track progress with held-out validation rollouts for every environment,
using uniform sampling of environments and reasoning efforts. Analysis and benchmark comparisons are
performed with high reasoning effort unless specified otherwise.

3.2.4.2 Kolibri RL Training Run

Figure 39 shows the validation reward of the Kolibri RL run by environment group and aggregated over
groups. The mean validation environment reward rises from 0.62 over the first 50 steps to 0.83 over the
last 50. The largest gains occur in instruction following and structured output, from 0.65 to 0.94, followed
by the software-engineering and tool-use environments.
Table 25 compares the released Kolibri checkpoint with the SFT checkpoint used to train it on a selection
of the benchmarks of Tables 28 and 29. The Kolibri checkpoint and the open models are evaluated with the
protocol of Section 3.3, which also covers BrowseComp (Wei, Sun, et al. 2025) and BFCL v4 (Patil et al. 2025).

The RL run greatly increases the SFT checkpoint’s instruction-following capabilities on IFBench (Pyatkin
et al. 2025), and leads to substantial improvements across agentic, math and coding abilities. Kolibri
performs on par with or slightly better than the open-source baselines on the presented benchmarks.
Among the three open models of Figure 2, it scores highest in Tables 28 and 29 on both AIME benchmarks
(Mathematical Association of America 2026), GPQA Diamond (Rein et al. 2023), τ 3-bench banking (Shi et al.
2026), LiveCodeBench (Jain et al. 2024) and IFBench (Pyatkin et al. 2025), and on BrowseComp above the
two models the table scores on it. It trails behind Qwen3.6 35B-A3B and Mistral Small 4 119B-A6B on the
AA-Omniscience Index (Jackson et al. 2025). Figure 47 in Section 3.3 compares the released checkpoint
with the same open-source baselines on agentic retrieval and customer-proxy benchmarks, finding that
our model outperforms them on agentic retrieval and competitive on the customer proxies.

Analysis Figure 40 shows the training dynamics of the Kolibri run. Its left panel shows that, without an
entropy bonus, the normalised entropy of the training policy rises from 0.040 over the first 20 steps to
about 0.06 between steps 350 and 600. It then stabilises and falls to 0.033 over the last 20 steps, while
the share of solved prompts keeps growing (Figure 41). Its middle panel shows that the B-TV mask and the
gradient-norm bound act on roughly 0.01 % of tokens per step. Finally, we see on the right that the training–
inference mismatch of Section 3.2.1.3 hovers at around |log rt| = 0.03, with a median forward KL divergence
of 2.4 × 10−3 per token. Both measures include the policy staleness induced by our asynchronous setup.

Figure 41. Difficulty buckets of the RL curriculum over training, summed over all environments. Left: share of all prompts in the hard, trainable
and solved buckets. Right: share of sampled prompts per bucket, divided by its target bucket weight cb. During initial training steps, the
curriculum compensates for missing and inaccurate offline pass rates, which populates the hard and solved buckets across envs. The model
then improves over the course of training, causing more samples to become solved. In particular, several environments become entirely
solved or trainable, draining their share of hard samples.

Curriculum Dynamics Training causes groups to saturate, leading to more and more solved prompts.
Figure 41 shows this trend and how our curriculum adjusts for this. As more hard prompts of an environment
become trainable or solved, its hard bucket shrinks, in turn reducing the supply term σb of Section 3.2.2.5
and thus the share of sampled hard prompts below its target.

Self-Distillation Dynamics OPSD aims to bootstrap the model’s capabilities via targeted hints for failures
with respect to terminal code execution and tool calls. It trains on low-reward groups that the group-
relative objective would otherwise discard. The left panel of Figure 42 shows that such behaviours are
frequent early during training in the terminal environments, but then rapidly decrease with distillation,
before stabilising. The tool-use environments follow the same pattern (right panel of Figure 69). During
later training stages, errors relating to outputs and time limits dominate, correlating with sequences taking
more turns in later training. The corresponding OPSD hints encourage the model to read files in slices and
filter command output.

Figure 42. Targeted OPSD over the Kolibri RL run. Left: Detected failure cases across the 5 terminal environments, counted at the optimiser
step whose weights produced the sequence. Right: Moving average over 25 steps of the share of distilled tokens for which the teacher’s most
likely token is the sampled token.

The right panel of Figure 42 shows how often the teacher agrees with the student’s token, which is a proxy
for the strength of the distillation signal. Agreement starts high and drops as training progresses, likely
because the teacher policy can make better use of the provided hints. Eventually, the teacher’s agreement
with the sampled tokens increases again, which indicates that the student starts to train out of the hinted
errors. Appendix I.6 expands on these findings, showing how distilled tokens have higher average entropy
throughout training and which share of groups the different OPSD hints act on. It also provides qualitative
examples of how a hint reshapes the teacher on a flagged turn for two different error classes, in both cases
focusing on the tokens that caused the error.

3.2.5 Lessons Learned and Hills Climbed

Releasing Kolibri is a story of incremental improvements. While this section has focused so far on the final
Kolibri training setup, we now elaborate on its evolution over time. To this end, we split the work on Kolibri’s
RL post-training into a preliminary exploration phase, a hill-climbing stage for each of two base model
generations, and the final release checkpoint of Kolibri. The following paragraphs sketch lessons learned
and decisions made across these generations. Figure 43 shows representative benchmark scores of our
RL checkpoints of Kolibri Origin and Kolibri over time, culminating in the final release checkpoint of Kolibri.
Table 26 compares the scale of our setup during early runs of each generation with that of the final run.

3.2.5.1 Phase 1: Initial Experiments

Starting Small For our first experiments, we forked torchforge (Meta PyTorch 2025b) and used our fork
to start experiments on a single small math environment. Our fork has since substantially evolved, with
existing features refactored to allow for larger scale and several new features introduced, such as in-flight
weight updates via RDMA (Section 3.2.3.4) and FP8 quantisation-aware training with routed-expert replay
(Section 3.2.1.3). From these first experiments, we incrementally expanded the scope to more sophisticated
math and later coding problems. For our first code environments, we used remote sandboxes for code
execution, which kept the load off our cluster, but made the reward computation reliant on an external
sandbox service. This proved challenging, as sandbox creation was throttled and unreliable, and failed

Figure 43. Benchmark scores of RL for Kolibri Origin and Kolibri. Grey lines indicate median evaluation scores for open-source baseline
models. Each colored point corresponds to a finished evaluation run that improved at least one of the four depicted benchmarks. Vertical
lines mark the first evaluation of an RL checkpoint of Kolibri. The lines track the best score of their model generation, evaluated with their
respective setup at the time they were ran. These intermediate points do not indicate representative benchmark performance, but are meant
as a visual representation of Kolibri’s progress over time A star marks the released Kolibri checkpoint, sampled at top-p 0.97 and top-k 128 as
in production. SWE-bench Verified is scored with the mini-swe-agent harness and shown for Kolibri only, as we did not optimise Kolibri Origin
towards agentic capabilities. The release (star) is scored with the OpenCode harness, without web access.

connections scored correct code as wrong. Towards a more sovereign infrastructure, we therefore moved
code execution to local sandboxes running on the CPUs of the training nodes.

Efficient Weight Updates The trainer and the vLLM replicas (Kwon et al. 2023) initially ran as plain Monarch
actors (Meta PyTorch 2025a), with synchronous weight updates through torchstore (Meta PyTorch 2025c).
A weight update stopped new requests, waited a median of 221 s for running generations, and took another
217 s to load. To improve performance, we stopped using torchstore, included RDMA writes from every
trainer rank, and added in-flight weight updates and an NVLink broadcast per node (Sections 3.2.1.5
and 3.2.3). These changes cut the weight update time down to about 17 s on comparable runs, which
enabled weight updates after every training step, in turn bringing our training more on-policy.

Choosing a Curriculum With more effective training, the model became able to saturate on environments,
which presented us with the new challenge of incrementally scaling task difficulty in lockstep with model
capability to keep the number of dropped groups consistently low. We initially considered complex training
curricula, such as sorting samples along a difficulty scale according to a reference, placing a sampling

                                                                                      90 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

distribution on this scale, and then moving the distribution’s mean towards more difficult samples over the
course of training. While successful, we eventually decided to move to the simpler three-bucket approach
(Section 3.2.2.5) to reduce the number of required hyperparameters.

Format Reward After those changes, our reward was climbing nicely, but benchmark scores were not.
We found that our RL training overfit on output formats at the cost of environment rewards. Most sequence
groups only had reward variance due to format differences and, as a result, did not provide environment
reward as a training signal. This issue motivated us to split both reward types and only apply the format
signals to sequences with positive environment reward. After these changes, 500 RL steps improved
AIME 2025 scores from 60.4 for the SFT-trained model to 70.6.

Interacting with SFT Our initial assumption was that effective post-training requires tight coupling
between SFT and RL. Although behaviours that RL should reinforce need to be introduced in SFT, we
found that more concrete training hyperparameters, such as learning rates and the number of training
steps, can be set independently. In particular, we have not seen checkpoints collapse into a hard-to-train
state through SFT.

Multi-Environment Training We anticipated difficulties in integrating many environments simultane-
ously into a single RL run, because slow environments are trained further off-policy, putting them at a
disadvantage in the mix. To compensate, we initially implemented a strict sampling regime that enforced
approximately fixed environment ratios in every batch. This sampling restriction turned out to harm com-
pute efficiency and did not improve empirical performance, so we dropped it. Without it, the mix still
approximately followed the sampling ratios.

3.2.5.2 Phase 2: Moving to Kolibri Origin

User Simulators and Judges Starting experiments on Kolibri Origin, we quickly found that our check-
points scored poorly on tool calling. We thus started training on tool-calling environments, chiefly a
customer-service environment. This environment was the first to require simulated user turns. We ex-
plored using external inference providers to host the user simulator, but moved to self-hosting to reduce
cost and improve reliability. We implemented this user simulator as a Monarch actor, which we later
expanded to also function as an LLM judge for environment reward.

Testing New Environments without Training To speed up the integration of new environments in our
training mix, we aim to estimate an environment’s climb-ability with a cheaper proxy, i.e., we sample multiple
completions for each prompt from the initial SFT model and compute a pass@k plot (M. Chen et al. 2021).
Ideally, an environment has trainable prompts in the sense of Section 3.2.2.5, which show as a gap between
pass@k and pass@1, and harder prompts that become trainable as RL progresses. We also run the same
check on recent RL checkpoints, which shows whether the policy trained on the current mix already solves
the new environment.

                                                                                       91 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier           Aleph Alpha

Sequence Monitoring With increasing scale, we started recording all training and validation sequences
on disk, gathering multiple terabytes per run. We built infrastructure around these sequence dumps,
including coding agent capabilities for semi-automatic evaluation, and dedicated web interfaces that allow
easy access to individual environments and samples. This infrastructure allowed us to efficiently monitor
environment quality and model behaviour, which led to several improved filters and reward functions.

Internal and Customer Preview Releases No benchmark or sequence interface fully covers the range of
possible user interactions. During development, we therefore internally released several RL checkpoints
of Kolibri Origin within a chat interface, encouraging the entire company to explore our model. Colleagues
reported failures the benchmarks did not show, and each round of feedback went into the environments
and the training data of the next release. For example, an early checkpoint avoided batched tool calls and
sometimes did not respond after a reasoning trace, which never surfaced in evals. The chat interface also
ran turn-level blind A/B tests against similar models, allowing us to gauge model capabilities during more
realistic use and adjust training accordingly. In addition, with each release we worked with Aleph Alpha’s
forward deployed teams to improve and verify the contextualised performance of the model in customer
proxy settings. Figure 44 shows this performance on the five Industry RAG benchmarks over the training
runs of September 2026.

3.2.5.3 Phase 3: Like a Kolibri from the Ashes

A Stronger Base Model Kolibri is a larger and significantly more capable model compared to Kolibri
Origin due to a longer context window, more experts and a more extensive pre-training recipe (Sections 2.1
and 2.3). To adjust to the new model, we decreased the learning rate from 1 × 10−4 to 1 × 10−5, and added a
per-token gradient-norm bound (Section 3.2.1.2) after noticing that gradients for individual tokens reached
into the thousands. The longer context also surfaced new infrastructure bottlenecks. For example, the
rollout threads ran as asyncio tasks on the controller, whose event loop lagged by a few seconds for Kolibri
Origin, but immediately jumped to several minutes for 256k-token contexts. We thus moved the threads
into rollout workers on every node (Section 3.2.3).

Agentic Environments Hill-Climb Real World Use-Cases Figure 44 tracks the test splits of MuSiQue
and Honeypot and the five in-house Industry RAG benchmarks over the training runs of September 2026.
Scores rise with each improvement to the environment parametrisation and to the core skills of the model.
The Industry RAG benchmarks rise as well throughout the month. This result indicates that by simulating
key aspects of real-world use-cases, such as harnesses, data distributions, and types of questions in our
environments, we are able to successfully optimise performance on those use-cases, without ever having
seen these concrete use-cases during training. This conclusion is further supported by our own internal
ablations.

Exploits The longer context window enabled training on more complex agentic environments. To support
these environments, we extended our sandbox setup, which was focused on code, to also handle agentic
use cases. We additionally integrated several different harnesses. Both additions surfaced exploits that
we caught via our sequence monitoring. For the first exploit, evaluating SWE-bench Verified (Jimenez et al.
2024) with the OpenCode harness (OpenCode Team 2026) gained about 7 percentage points compared
to other harnesses. Our investigation found that the model used OpenCode’s web-fetch and web-search
tools to find ground-truth solutions for prompts on GitHub in about 7 % of trials, causing it to pass almost
all of these trials (Figure 45, left). The left side of Figure 45 shows an example sequence.

                                                            93 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                         Aleph Alpha

Figure 45. Example sequences that led to exploits, with ellipses marking omitted text. Left: The OpenCode harness exposes a web-fetch tool,
and the policy resolves an SWE-bench instance to its upstream pull request, downloads the patch and applies it. Right: The sandbox does not
let apt switch to its unprivileged download user. The reasoning concludes that the process runs as root and can therefore write to kernel
settings, and the next tool call disables unprivileged user namespaces on the host kernel.

The other exploit was related to some sandboxes that were not fully isolated from their hosts. In particular,
one sandbox command disabled the unprivileged user namespaces our sandboxes run in (Section 3.2.3.5).
The right side of Figure 45 shows an example sequence. The next day, a run scheduled on that node could
not start sandboxes. Thus, sandbox escapes were, at least in our setup, as much a function of sandbox
security as of model capability. We subsequently run sandboxes as unprivileged users, removed web
fetching from the evaluation harnesses, and in training, we harden our egress allowlist and system prompts
to avoid model exploits. We will further look into best practices.

Inside a sandbox, commands still run as root of its own user namespace, so package managers can install
into the sandbox’s writable layer, but this root maps to an unprivileged range of host users and cannot
change the host kernel.

Scaling Environments The last changes before the final run added over a dozen new environments. Most
of the newly added environments worked well and immediately had a positive impact on downstream
performance. An exception was the tool calling environments, which needed an additional judge for
free-form responses, as well as a filter of potentially wrong or misleading reference answers.

Final Adjustments The final run of Section 3.2.4 provided several additions targeted at remaining issues
with earlier Kolibri experiments. We found that many environments, particularly agentic ones, were difficult
to solve from the SFT checkpoint. As a remedy, we implemented OPSD (Section 3.2.1.4) using error-
dependent hints to provide a dense learning signal to the model. Simultaneously, we switched from additive
format signals to the multiplicative p-norm version of Equation (18). This change allowed for individual
signals to provide meaningful feedback to positive sequences while keeping a minimum reward for success.
The final run also added the Merlin–Arthur environments of Section 3.2.2.3 to reduce hallucinations,
extended OPSD to further terminal environments, and graded retrieval answers by token F1 (Rajpurkar
et al. 2016) (Appendix I.1.4) instead of the judge. With these adjustments and a general increase in scale
from Table 26, Kolibri was ready for its big run, which we detail in Section 3.2.4.

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                                  Aleph Alpha

Trained-on context length                                                         2                 9                   38

Table 26. Scale comparisons across RL setups during early development, for Kolibri Origin runs, and for our final Kolibri run. In addition to
algorithmic improvements (Section 3.2.1), careful reward design and data curation (Section 3.2.2), scaling up the infrastructure along various
axes is crucial for Kolibri’s RL training. The early experiments and Kolibri Origin’s setup count code sandboxes, while Kolibri also includes
agentic sandboxes that were added later. GPU hours are weighted by the GPU’s peak dense FP8 tensor throughput relative to a B300.

Future Work There are several directions that our newly hatched Kolibri has yet to explore. Prominent
ones include environments that require reasoning over long inputs, Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation
(MOPD) (LLM-Core Xiaomi 2026), multi-stage training (NVIDIA 2025b), and switching to an environment-
driven rollout framework, which would allow for more flexible rollout logic and the integration of black-
box harnesses. Black-box harnesses also open up non-linear message histories because of context
compaction, and multi-agent setups, where several agents interleave their messages. Both break the
assumption that a rollout is one policy appending turns to a history it owns, which is what makes them
hard to support in the current trainer-driven interface (Section 3.2.3.2). Another important addition is a
dedicated environment to draw an SVG of a pelican riding on a bicycle (Willison 2024).

Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                            Aleph Alpha

3.3 Released Model Evaluation

We compare the released Kolibri checkpoint with Kolibri Origin and with open models, including models
of similar and larger active size (Tables 28 and 29).

3.3.1 Evaluation Suite

Our post-training evaluation suite covers knowledge, math, agentic tool use, code, instruction following,
grounding and hallucinations, agentic retrieval, Industry RAG and long context, in English and in German
where a German version exists. Table 27 lists the benchmarks by category with their settings and German

Table 27. Post-training benchmarks by category, with their settings and German versions. Every German version marked as a translation
comes from ellamind (2025), and an en dash marks a benchmark without a German version. The score avg@N is the mean over N runs of
every sample.

Settings. All models are evaluated either with eval-framework (Aleph Alpha Research 2026) or, on the
agentic benchmarks, Harbor (Harbor Framework Team 2026), with Kolibri at reasoning effort high. A
model reports no score on a benchmark whose prompts or agent trajectories exceed its context window,
and a model that cannot call tools reports none on the tool-calling benchmarks: BFCL, BrowseComp,
Tau2- and Tau3-Bench, the agentic retrieval benchmarks and Industry RAG. The long-context benchmarks
handle this case differently. Their prompts exceed the 64k-token context window used to serve Kolibri
Origin, but the corresponding rows record 0 rather than no score. Two of these benchmarks need a caveat
on how their scores may be read. On BFCL v4, web search uses a different search backend from the
leaderboard and every model samples at its own parameters, so the scores are not comparable with the
leaderboard, and an entry that outgrows a model’s window counts as wrong. The Industry RAG scores are
a relative signal between models rather than the accuracy of the deployed assistant, and Industrial Drive
Technology (11 items) and Automotive Supplier (26) are too small to show a trend.

Performance Aggregates. Each category’s Average row is the unweighted mean over the benchmarks
of that language every compared model is evaluated on, without the AA-Omniscience Index, whose range
is not a percentage, and without Honeypot, which draws its questions in both languages, and the Overall
rows are the unweighted mean of the category averages. A benchmark the table does not mark (DE)
counts towards the English average of its category, whether or not it has a German counterpart, so a

                                                                                       97 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

benchmark that exists in English alone, such as TerminalBench or SWE-Bench Verified, still enters the
English side. Only four of the eight categories the Overall rows cover have German benchmarks, so the
German Overall rests on the Knowledge, Math, Agentic Retrieval and Industry RAG averages, and the
English one on all eight. The five BFCL v4 splits stay out of the agentic average as well, since the overall
score above them already aggregates them: it is bfcl-eval’s leaderboard score, a weighted mean over
those five splits and an irrelevance-detection split the table does not print. Averaging the splits beside the
score that aggregates them would weigh the same measurements twice and give BFCL six of the agentic
slots against one for each Tau2-Bench domain, so the table indents them under that score and sets them
in grey. Long Context, the axis every other category holds fixed, would stay outside them too, but neither
of its benchmarks is scored for every compared model, so it carries no average at all.

3.3.2 Baseline Models

We compare Kolibri to twelve open post-trained models spanning dense models from 27B to 70B parame-
ters and MoE models with 3B to 12B active parameters: Qwen3.5 35B-A3B (Qwen Team 2026a), Qwen3.6
35B-A3B (Qwen Team 2026b), Qwen3-Next 80B-A3B Thinking (Qwen Team 2025b), Qwen3.8 27B (Qwen
Team 2026c), Nemotron 3 Nano 30B-A3B (NVIDIA 2025a), Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B (NVIDIA 2026a),
GLM-4.5 Air 106B-A12B (GLM-4.5 Team 2025), GLM-4.7 Flash 30B-A3B (Z.ai 2026a), Gemma 4 26B-A4B
IT (Gemma Team 2026), Mistral Small 4 119B-A6B (Mistral AI 2026), GPT-OSS 120B (OpenAI 2025) and
Apertus 70B Instruct (Project Apertus et al. 2025), next to Kolibri Origin. Each model is served at the context
window and sampling parameters its model card documents. Table 48 in Appendix E.1 lists each model’s
checkpoint, reference and deployment. Apertus 70B Instruct scores near zero on every benchmark whose
answer is parsed out of the completion while scoring in range on the multiple-choice ones, so its column
is set in grey, takes no best-score mark and enters no average. GPT-OSS 120B and GLM-4.5 Air 106B-A12B
are evaluated on neither long-context benchmark, since the 128k-token window their deployments serve
leaves too little room for the prompts of those benchmarks and the reasoning they need.

3.3.3 Results and Discussion

Figure 46 shows the Overall aggregates against the active parameters per token. Qwen3.5 35B-A3B,
Kolibri and Qwen3.8 27B span the convex hull of the Pareto frontier in both languages. Kolibri scores 0.8
points above Qwen3.5 35B-A3B in English and 1.0 in German at 3.46B active parameters against 3B.

Qwen3.8 27B has the highest overall aggregates, but as a dense model it activates nearly 8 times as many
parameters per token as Kolibri. At 3.46B active parameters, Kolibri scores above all 3B-active baselines in
both languages. Across all compared MoE models, Kolibri scores best on both Overall rows. In particular,
Kolibri excels on maths benchmarks. On AIME 2025 and 2026 in English, it scores above every MoE model
of the comparison, including those with several times its active parameters, and only the dense Qwen3.8
27B scores higher. This lead over the other models with 3B active parameters also holds on the German
versions of both benchmarks. Kolibri also performs best on the SQuAD (M/A Grounding Score), on SealQA
above 24k without distractors and on the automotive supplier proxy. In 15 further rows, it only lags behind

the substantially larger Qwen3.8 27B, among them the English math average, Tau3-Bench, Honeypot,

Figure 47 compares the released Kolibri checkpoint with the three open models of Figure 2 on the Agentic
Retrieval and Industry RAG benchmarks of Table 27. MuSiQue (Trivedi et al. 2022), Honeypot and German
Agentic Wiki QA test the multi-hop search that the retrieval environments train (Section 3.2.2.3). The five
customer proxies run over unseen corpora and questions (Appendix I.1.4 and Table 57). Among the plotted
models, Kolibri scores second highest on Honeypot and Agentic Wiki QA (DE) (following Qwen-3.8 27B).
Across the five customer-proxy Industry RAG evals, Kolibri averages 89.7 on the English tasks and 67.5 on
the German ones. On English it beats every MoE baseline, including those with 12B active parameters.
On German it beats every MoE baseline except Qwen3.5 35B-A3B (70.0). Only the dense Qwen3.8 27B
scores higher on both (93.2 / 80.2).

3.3.3.1 Real-World and Customer Use-Cases

Most public benchmarks fail to capture specialised sector needs. For example, popular information retrieval
benchmarks, require a specific harness and search ability over the full internet, and avoid the delicacy of
harness choices, specialised corpora and data ingestion in their evaluation. We spent significant effort (1)
measuring contextualised performance in customer-specific use cases, such as automotive, aerospace,
German public sector, semiconductors and industrial drive technology (2) in building environments which
address these specifics directly, and thus reflect real-world use-cases, in particular for agentic RAG
applications.

Our results show very strong performance within-distribution, in terms of the datasets we clean and
prepare on the basis of publicly available corpora and synthetic multi-hop RAG performance. Together
with feed-back from evaluations on the real-world ”customer-proxies”, our retrieval environments contribute
to an iterative engine that allows us to sharpen the model’s agentic RAG capabilities, domain-specific
logic and capabilities, and to improve on contextualised evaluations without ever training on customer
data. Our final scores on the customer-proxy evaluations provide evidence that the retrieval-environments
reflect the specifics of production setups (harness, data ingestion) as well as reflecting real-world data
distributions.

3.3.3.2 Hallucination Reduction

Kolibri hallucinates far less than Kolibri Origin: it abstains instead of answering wrong on 44 % of AA-
Omniscience items (Origin: 15 %), and on RGB it holds back more often (86 % vs 74 %). On our own M/A
grounding score, which certifies rather than estimates how much of an answer came from the document,
Kolibri reaches 23.4 % where Kolibri Origin and several other models score 0. See Figure 48 for more
details.
