F “I Don’t Know” Evaluations

This appendix complements Section 2.4 with evaluations of how models distribute probability when they
may abstain.

Motivation. Kalai et al. (2026) argue that language models hallucinate partly because most benchmarks
use binary grading, under which guessing always scores at least as well as abstaining. They propose “open
rubric” evaluations that state the scoring in the prompt, with wrong answers penalised by L = t/(1 − t)
points for a correctness threshold t. Our ternary score (+1 correct, 0 abstain, −1 wrong) is the t = 0.5, L = 1
case of that scheme, although we do not state the rubric to the model. The distributional correctness
score (DCS) (T. F. Burns 2026) goes further and scores the model’s whole probability distribution over the
answer options plus an “I don’t know” (IDK) option: DCS := (pc − pw)(1 − pIDK), where pc, pw, and pIDK
are the probability masses placed on correct, wrong, and IDK options, respectively. DCS lies in [−1, 1]
and abstaining scores 0. T. F. Burns (2026) reports that on half of 12 tested benchmarks, every one of
the six models tested had negative DCS, so the negative averages below are expected. Confidence-
weighted accuracy (C-Accuracy) is pc when the argmax option is correct and 0 otherwise. It assumes
that base-model probabilities are informative, and Kadavath et al. (2022) found that this largely holds
for well-formatted multiple-choice questions. Kumaran et al. (2026) give causal evidence that language
models use internal confidence to decide whether to abstain and steered internal confidence signals up
or down to change how often models abstained.

Setup. We evaluate Kolibri Base, the checkpoints after its pre-training and mid-training stages, and
the following baseline models of Section 2.4: Kolibri Origin Base, OLMo 3 7B, OLMo 3 32B, Apertus
70B, Nemotron 3 Nano 30B-A3B, and Qwen3.5 35B-A3B, together with GLM-4.5 Air 106B-A12B and
Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B. We use 7 English IDK variants. Each task appends an IDK option to an
existing multiple-choice task and is scored by log-likelihood. As in the main evaluations, all runs and
metrics use eval-framework (Aleph Alpha Research 2026). The source tasks are:

 • Grade-school science. ARC.
 • Commonsense. GoldenSwag (a filtered HellaSwag subset), PIQA, and Winogrande.
 • Knowledge and expert questions. MMLU, MMLU-Pro, and GPQA.

Results. Table 49 gives the mean over the 7 tasks. For readability, all scores are the raw metric times
100. Higher scores are better. The largest model, Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B, is first on all metrics.
Kolibri Base is second on C-Accuracy and DCS. A paired Bayesian bootstrap of the per-question scores
gives a posterior probability above 0.9999 that Kolibri Base has the higher average DCS than every model
besides Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B.

MMLU-Pro gives the clearest single-task result. Kolibri Base scores −20.23, ahead of every comparison
model except Nemotron 3 Super 120B-A12B (−11.02). The other models score between −36.33 (GLM-4.5
Air 106B-A12B) and −58.31 (OLMo 3 7B), with Apertus 70B and Kolibri Origin Base also at the bottom (−57.56

G Reasoning Mode at Long Context

We evaluate the post-trained Kolibri checkpoint on the RULER tasks of Section 2.3.4.3 with reasoning on
and off (Figure 65). Reasoning off raises needle-in-a-haystack recall, while reasoning on scores higher
on question answering at the longest context lengths. The Qwen3 technical report also finds lower
RULER scores in thinking mode and suggests that extra reasoning can interfere with retrieval (Qwen Team
2025a). The reasoning-on run allows up to 16k generated tokens and the reasoning-off run up to 1k, so this
comparison does not isolate the effect of reasoning alone. Nemotron 3 Nano is evaluated with thinking off,
as recommended for this task. At 1M tokens, Kolibri with reasoning off scores above Nemotron 3 Nano on
variable tracking and question answering, but below it on needle-in-a-haystack and word extraction.

H.3 SFT MergeMix Sweep

Following J. Wang et al. (2026, Section 4.2), we compare merged and trained mixes on math and code

before the full sweep. We train a math, a code and a chat specialist from the ablation base model of

Appendix D.2.2, each for 20.0B packed tokens. They train on all math, all code and all general chat datasets

of the SFT mix, each dataset sampled in proportion to its unique tokens (natural sampling, Section 2.3.3.3).

A weighting gives each specialist a share, and the shares sum to one. For every weighting, we evaluate

its merge, the specialists averaged with these shares, and a model trained for the same budget on data

sampled with the same shares. We use two series of weightings. In the first, the math share α runs from 0

to  1      in  steps  of  0.2,  and  code     takes  the  rest.  In  the   second,     chat  has  a  share    of  1  ,  and  math    and   code  split  the
                                                                                                                  3

remaining      2      in  the   ratio  α  to  1  −  α.  The  single  specialists       at  the  ends  of  the  first    series    are  the  same  model
               3

either way, so the first series compares 4 weightings and the second 6. For each benchmark10 we compute

the Spearman correlation between merged and trained scores in each series (Table 52 and Figure 66).

Merging and training order the weightings alike on AIME 2026 and LiveCodeBench. They disagree on

MATH-500 (0.32 and 0.26), where every weighting scores between 84 and 91 %. On this benchmark, the

scores barely move with the weighting, so neither ordering carries information.

H.5 KulturBank

KulturBank is an in-house benchmark of 200 German questions on general knowledge about Germany.
The questions cover eight topics with 25 questions each: food and drink, geography, history, culture,
nature, sport, technology and economy. The model answers from its own knowledge, without tools or
retrieved context. It reasons briefly in German and then gives the answer as a short term in \boxed{}. Of
the questions, 16 rest on a false premise and have no correct answer; for these, the model explains the
error and answers \boxed{Falsche Prämisse}. If the model does not know an answer, the prompt
asks it to answer \boxed{Unbekannt} instead of guessing. A response counts as correct if the boxed
term matches one of the accepted answers of the question.

I Reinforcement Learning

I.1 Environments

I.1.1 Reward Structure by Environment

Code Generation The extracted program runs in a sandbox against every test case of the task with a
20 s timeout per test. Its output is compared with the expected output, with a tolerance for floating-point
numbers and case-insensitive yes/no answers, so a solution is not failed for whitespace or formatting
alone. The reward is the fraction of test cases passed.

                                                            172 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier            Aleph Alpha

Software-Engineering Agents The policy works in a per-task container through a coding harness with a
shell tool, and its changes are extracted as a candidate patch. The patch is applied in a fresh container
and the task’s tests are run: the reward is the fraction of passing fail-to-pass tests floored by pass-to-pass
tests regression. Sequences are capped at 100 steps.

Software-Engineering Repair The search-and-replace edits are compared with the golden patch by the
judge model, without executing tests; the reward is the judge’s score, and a non-empty solution block is
the Solution block signal.

Terminal Tasks Terminal tasks are graded by the corpus’s own test script, which runs ‘pytest‘ over the final
machine state and writes a test report; the reward is the fraction of tests that pass.

Customer-Service Environment The reward compares the final database state with the expected one,
checks the required actions and judges natural-language assertions about the conversation.

Retrieval-Augmented Question Answering The policy searches a document corpus with the tools of
Table 56 and gives its final answer in the format that the prompt sets out. The reward is the best token F1
of that answer against the gold answers, and a sequence that never calls a search tool scores zero. The
Boxed answer, Answer format and Citations signals of Table 24 score the form of the answer. Appendix I.1.4
gives the step caps and further details of the environment.

All environments have a per-environment token and step-budgets with token exhaustion scoring zero for
tool use environments and triggering grading for SWE environments.

I.1.2 Long Context Environment

For every document, GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) writes two questions with six options, one to four of them correct.
A seeded plan per document fixes which part of the document each question draws on (beginning, middle
or several distant passages) and how many options are correct, so the distribution of question shapes is
not left to the generator. A question has to combine facts from several passages, may not be answerable
from the conversation turns alone, and uses true statements from elsewhere in the document as wrong
options.

Each question is answered eight times by a Kolibri checkpoint at low reasoning effort and eight times by
GLM-5.2, and enters the training set only if GLM-5.2 solved it at least once and Kolibri at most six times;
where Kolibri’s attempts agree on a different answer or solve it at most once, Kimi K2.6 (Kimi Team 2026c)
rules on the disputed options without seeing the key. The reward is 1 only when the letters on the final
Answer: line are exactly the correct set, and an empty response scores 0 instead of being dropped, so
reasoning past the token budget on a long document is penalised.

                                                            173 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                        Aleph Alpha

I.1.3 Hallucination Reduction Environment

The hallucination reduction environments implement a Merlin–Arthur protocol (Deiseroth et al. 2026) on

various RAG training datasets. Arthur is the model that we train, i.e., the policy πθ, and Merlin and Morgana

are masking algorithms. For a seed with context x, question q, and gold answer a, they choose masks

m ∈ {0, 1}C over the C maskable units (sentences) of x. The masks are ensured to hide similar amounts of

context with some tolerance. The masks optimise the following objective, where x ⊙ m denotes a context

x in which parts have been masked out by m:

                                                                      (︁    )︁    (25)
mˆ Mer, mˆ Mor = arg max log πθ(a | x ⊙ mMer, q) + log 1 − πθ(a | x ⊙ mMor, q) ,
mMer ,mMor

such that |kMer − kMor| ≤ δ, where k = |m| is the number of units a mask hides. Moreover, each mask hides
at least one unit and leaves at least one visible, and the two masked contexts must differ. We approximate
both masks by first ranking the sentences by how much masking each one individually changes the
log-probability of a, teacher-forced on the same inference engine that produces the rollouts, and then
removing the top k. During training, we recompute the masks from the policy at every step, so Morgana
follows the features that make Arthur prone to hallucinations; no second model or human annotation is
involved in generating these abstention examples. Since Merlin and Morgana hide the same number of
sentences within an adjustable tolerance, Arthur cannot tell them apart by how much is missing.

Arthur’s training relies on rewards obtained from completions on the question q paired with Merlin, Morgana,

and full (unmasked) contexts. A completion y receives a reward R(y) that rewards Arthur for doing what its

role asks and penalises a wrong answer on the full context and Merlin’s:

          ⎧     if y answers a on the full context or Merlin’s,
          ⎪⎪⎪1  if y gives another answer on the full context or Merlin’s,
          ⎪⎪⎪   if y abstains on Morgana’s context,
          ⎨−1   otherwise.
R(y) ≈
          ⎪⎪⎪1
          ⎪⎪⎪
          ⎩0

We additionally influence these values by small terms for token overlap with a and for abstaining on the
full context or Merlin’s. An answer counts as correct on an exact match with the gold answer or one of its
aliases; otherwise, an LLM judge model decides whether it matches and whether it abstains.

Each of the three versions of a seed is rolled out 8 times, and each role is centred on its own mean; the
advantage of rollout j is computed as

                                                      Aj ∝ R(yj ) − R¯role(j),

where R¯role(j) is the mean training reward of the rollouts of its role scaled by a fixed weight per role and
by the quality of the masks (for Merlin and Morgana only). Arthur cannot gain advantage over correct
answers by abstaining, since answers (and not abstentions) are expected on the full context or on Merlin’s.
A separate environment poses unanswerable questions with their full context and rewards only abstention.

The Merlin–Arthur protocol also gives us a hallucination measurement. It measures how often the model
answers correctly on Merlin’s context is its completeness, and how often it refuses on Morgana’s is its
soundness. Together, they yield our M/A grounding score, a lower bound on how much of the answer

                                                            174 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier            Aleph Alpha

Tool                   Description

Search tools           Dense embedding search; returns the top-k chunks with score, document id, title and text.
vector_search          BM25 keyword search over the same chunks.
lexical_search         Merges the dense and lexical results by reciprocal-rank fusion.
hybrid_search          Regular-expression search like grep -n over the documents BM25 retrieves for the pattern.
grep
                       Lists the values of a filter column with their document counts, most common first.
Filter tools           Finds documents by exact filter values without a query; returns their ids, titles and filter values.
list_filter_values
find_documents         Reads a document by id, in pieces with an offset or whole up to 8000 or 16 000 characters.
                       Reads up to four fixed-size pages (2000 or 4000 characters) of a document by filename.
Document access tools  Reads 2000 or 4000 characters centred on a character position from a search hit.
open_document          Reads numbered lines from a line given by a search hit, 50 by default and 200 at most.
open_pages
open_window
read_lines

Table 56. Tools of the retrieval environments, grouped by role. The filter tools are served only when the corpus declares filter columns, and
hybrid_search and grep need a BM25 index over the document chunks.

provably came from the document. The lower bound makes the grounding score conservative: zero
means that it cannot be certified that the document was used, not that it went unused. More details on
the grounding score and the Merlin–Arthur protocol can be found in our paper (Deiseroth et al. 2026).

I.1.4 Retrieval-Augmented Question Answering

Many customer applications answer questions from documents within the domain of their organisa-
tion, so the model has to search a corpus over several steps with the specific tools the application of-
fers and answer from the retrieved text. Two environments train this behaviour, and both are part of
the RL mix (Section 3.2.2.3) and provide the rollouts that cold-start SFT (Section 3.1.1.4). retrieval
poses multi-hop questions over English data, retrieval-de German questions over German data and
retrieval-honeypot, referred to as Honeypot, poses synthetic multi-hop questions over specialised
corpora. This section describes the task, the tools they use, the question sets, the randomisation applied
to each episode, the infrastructure that runs the environments during training, and the evaluation and
results.

Task and Tools In each episode, the policy receives a question and a set of tools, searches the corpus,
reads the documents it finds and gives a final answer in the format that the prompt specifies. The answer
is graded by F1 score based on word-overlap with the gold answer and its aliases, together with the format
signals of Appendix I.1.1. Table 56 lists the tools, which fall into three groups: search tools that return
passages, filter tools that restrict a search to documents with given metadata, and document access
tools that read a document once a search has found it. The retrieval tools take a query, a result count k
(unless the session fixes it) and an optional filters argument that restricts them to documents matching
given filter-column values; grep takes a regular expression and a match limit. Sequences are capped at a
maximum of 100 steps in retrieval and retrieval-de, and 50 steps in retrieval-honeypot.

                                                            175 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

Factual Questions The questions of retrieval are taken from the training split of an multi-hop question-
answering dataset. They are answered over an English pool, served through one of several index bundles
that differ in their Extract, Transform, Load (ETL) parameters: the embedding model and chunk size for
vector search, and the BM25 parameters for lexical search. Before training, we apply Kimi-K2.7 (Kimi Team
2026d) to roll out every question in the environment, and these rollouts drive a filtering and repair pass.
A question is dropped when one of its supporting articles is missing from the corpus, when Gemma-4-
31B (Gemma Team 2026) answers it correctly without retrieval, or when an LLM judge labels it malformed,
ambiguous or unsupported on the basis of its rollout. Where the judge considers a question repairable, we
apply GLM-5.2 (Z.ai 2026b) to rewrite an outdated gold answer or an underspecified question, and again
use GLM-5.2 to check the rewritten question and answer. The training split is restricted to an allow-list of
questions that are not in the SFT pipeline, and the test split is excluded from training in both SFT and RL;
Section 3.1.1.4 describes the rollouts used in SFT. retrieval-de questions are translated and filtered
versions of the English questions, processed by the same pipeline and answered over a German pool.

Honeypot Questions Honeypot covers nine specialised corpora (industrial product documentation,
aerospace certification, financial regulation, EU and German law, scientific reports and economic develop-
ment publications) that broadly cover our customer’s related domains of interest, and every question is
served over its own corpus. Its questions are generated following the Context-1 recipe (Bashir et al. 2026).
For each corpus, seed phrases taken mostly from document titles, supplemented by a hand-written list,
are issued to the search tools, facts are extracted from the retrieved passages, and a question is generated
for each fact, with that fact as its answer. Each fact is then located at further positions in the corpus, and
new facts from the surrounding text give follow-up questions in the same way. Chaining an initial question
with its follow-ups gives a multi-hop task in which answering hop k requires the answer to hop k − 1. A
hop is kept only if a search for its clue together with the previous answer finds the target document and
a search for the clue alone does not, so that each hop depends on the one before. Each corpus also
declares document-level filter columns, and the search tools accept a filter on them, so that every retrieved
document satisfies it. The collection holds training and test questions over all nine corpora, and none of
the training questions entered the SFT pipeline.

Randomisation per Episode A deployed retrieval application fixes its tools, its prompt and its index,
and these choices differ between applications. To keep the model independent of any one setup, each
episode draws its own configuration. It serves one or more of the search tools and a single document
access tool over one of the available index bundles of the corpus. Its prompt preamble comes from a
pre-configured set and fixes the answer format, the answer contents, whether tool use is required and the
step budget. Each session also samples a name, parameter names and a result format for every search
tool, and the name of the document-id parameter (document_id, doc_id or ref). The document access
tool is always read_lines when grep is served, and search results carry whichever address the access
tool takes: a document id, a filename and page, a character position or a line number.

Infrastructure The retrieval tools in production applications rely on a set of services: a tool-calling server,
an embedding-model server, a relational database, a vector-search engine and a lexical search index.

                                                                                      176 /189

Table 57. Corpus and task sources of the five Industry RAG benchmarks, in-house proxies for deployed customer assistants.

The environment mimics the production scenario by offering the policy the same tools, but simplifies
their implementation to reduce the burden on the training infrastructure. A tool handler inside the rollout
process serves the tools of Table 56, with the filter tools only where the corpus declares filter columns,
and dispatches each tool call as a direct function call on a retrieval engine embedded within the process.
Therefore, the only traffic leaving the process is generation requests to the policy’s inference endpoint.

For vector search, we use a sentence-transformers model that runs on the CPU inside the environ-
ment process as the query encoder. An asynchronous batcher coalesces the queries that parallel rollouts
send at the same time into batched encodes on a small pool of search-worker threads. We use small
embedding models (fewer than 1B parameters) which make CPU-based deployments feasible at sufficient
throughput. Lexical search uses a memory-mapped bm25s index over the same chunks as vector search.

Evaluation on Industry RAG Benchmarks To check that agentic RAG performance generalises beyond
MuSiQue and Honeypot, we built the five Industry RAG benchmarks of Table 57. They are in-house
benchmarks, not publicly available, and each is a proxy for a deployed customer assistant in a public
or industrial use case; their results are the Industry RAG rows of Table 29. The benchmarks share no
documents or questions with the SFT and RL data, although some Honeypot corpora come from the same
domains. Gains on these benchmarks therefore reflect how well the training and the randomisation of
tools, prompts and index bundles transfer to out-of-distribution real-world corpora and questions. Their
scores over the training runs of September 2026 are shown in Figure 44.

                                                            177 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier                                                               Aleph Alpha

I.2 Harness Tools

Some of the coding harnesses that are used have dependencies that might need to be installed at runtime.
The Terminus 2 harness of Terminal-Bench (Merrill et al. 2026) drives the agent through a tmux session
inside the sandbox, and most task images do not ship tmux. Installing it at rollout time would need network
access in every sandbox and would repeat the installation thousands of times per optimiser step. The
executor therefore adds a statically linked tmux binary as a read-only bottom layer of every sandbox’s
OverlayFS union (Section 3.2.3.5), so every sandbox sees tmux without any change to its image. The layer
places its files at the paths where images keep their own, so an image that ships tmux shadows the layer’s
copy and the layer only fills gaps. Static linking keeps the binary independent of the task image’s C library.

I.3 Per-Token Logit-Gradient Norms

By the chain rule, the derivative of the policy-gradient loss term of Equation (16) splits as

                                                            ˜PG   ∂ℓ˜PG     ∂z
                                                       ∇θℓt =
                                                                      t

                                                                       ∂z ∂θ

i.e., into the loss gradient with respect to the training policy logits ∂z ∂ℓ˜Pt G and the model’s Jacobian ∂z/∂θ. All
other quantities do not depend on θ or are non-differentiable.

I.3.1 Infinity-Norm of the Logit Gradient

Since applying the B-TV mask truncates the gradient, assume that the sampled token is not masked, i.e., it

has  kt  =  1.  Then,  Equation  (16)  simplifies  to  ℓ˜PG    =  −At  rt.  This  policy-gradient  term  is  linear  in  the  training

                                                        t

policy probability of the sampled token through the importance ratio, allowing us to determine the logit

gradient norm in closed form.

Log-Softmax Jacobian We define p = softmax(z) = πθ(· | st) as the training probabilities with entries

pv over indices v of the token vocabulary. Let w be a different vocabulary index, and yt denote the sampled

token, i.e., pθ,t = pyt . Splitting the logit gradient further around the normalised log-probabilities log p, yields

a partial derivative of the loss with respect to the log-probabilities, and a Jacobian of the log-softmax that

maps the unnormalised logits to the log-probabilities. The derivative of the log-softmax can be expressed

as
                                                       ∂ log pv = δvw − pw,

where δvw is the Kronecker delta. Since the loss depends on the logits only through the log-probability

log pθ,t of the sampled token, we only need the corresponding row ∇z log pθ,t of the Jacobian. This row is

I.3.3 Pushforward Analysis

The scaling ct of Section 3.2.1.2 bounds the gradient on the logits as a means to limit the change in training
policy probability. We next show this relationship by analysing a direct gradient step on the logits, as if they
were free parameters, and the resulting effect on the probabilities. In training, the step reaches the logits
through the model’s Jacobian ∂z/∂θ and sums over all tokens of the batch, so this analysis describes the
contribution of a single token.

To first order, a gradient step δz = −η ∇zℓ˜Pt G with step size η on the logits changes the probabilities through
the Jacobian of the softmax,

                                   δp = J δz,               J := ∂p = diag(p) − pp⊤.
                                                                   ∂z

                                                                179 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier  Aleph Alpha

Inserting ∇zℓ˜Pt G = −At rt gt and p⊤gt = pθ,t − ∥p∥22, the probability of the sampled token changes by
                                δpθ,t = η At rt pθ,t(︁1 − 2pθ,t + ∥p∥22)︁ = η At rt pθ,t ∥gt∥22.

With the 2-norm bounds (1 − pθ,t)2 ≤ ∥gt∥22 ≤ 2 (1 − pθ,t)2, this change satisfies

                                η |At| Gt pθ,t(1 − pθ,t) ≤ |δpθ,t| ≤ 2 η |At| Gt pθ,t(1 − pθ,t)

and is thus proportional to Gt up to a factor of at most 2. The scaling ct clips Gt at Gmax, and since
pθ,t(1 − pθ,t) ≤ 14 , a single token changes its own probability to first order by at most

                                 |ct δpθ,t| ≤ 2 η |At| Gmax pθ,t(1 − pθ,t) ≤ η |At| Gmax/2.

I.4 Training Recipe Details

I.4.1 FP8 Quantisation-Aware Training

InfiXAI Team et al. (2025) find that the training–inference mismatch comes from the quantisation itself
rather than from the FP8 kernels. Following this finding, the trainer of Section 3.2.1.3 applies the same
FP8 rounding as the inference engine but computes in BF16, without FP8 kernels. Even so, the median
training–inference mismatch |log rt| is roughly three times that of BF16 serving.

The inference engine stores weights in FP8 (E4M3) in blocks of 128 × 128, each with an FP32 scale, quan-
tises activations dynamically in groups of 1 × 128, and keeps the KV cache in FP8. The embeddings, the
normalisation layers and the router gates remain in BF16, and both policies keep the LM head’s logits in
FP32, which MiniMax (2025) find reduces the training–inference mismatch. In the forward pass, the trainer
rounds the weights and input activations of all other linear layers and of the routed experts to FP8 with
the same block geometry and scales, and rounds the queries, keys and values to FP8 with unit scales,
as the inference engine does. RMS normalisation (B. Zhang and Sennrich 2019) bounds the norm of its
output by its largest gain times the square root of its dimension, for any input. The QK normalisation
(Henry et al. 2020) therefore bounds every query and key element, and RoPE (Jianlin Su et al. 2024), where
applied, preserves this bound as a rotation. The normalisation before the attention block bounds the
values together with the value weights. For Kolibri, these bounds lie below the E4M3 maximum of 448
(Micikevicius et al. 2022), so no input can overflow. The backward pass uses the straight-through estimator
(Bengio, Léonard, and Courville 2013).

I.4.2 Staleness at Scale

The loop of rollout generation and optimiser steps (Section 3.2.1.5) runs at the speed of its slowest part, the
trainer, inference or saturated environment resources such as sandboxes, and is balanced when inference
produces groups as fast as the trainer consumes them. Staleness is counted in optimiser steps, but rollouts
take wall-clock time. The staleness of a group is therefore roughly its rollout duration divided by the step
time of the trainer, so any speedup of the trainer at a fixed generation latency makes groups staler. Suppose
a balanced loop takes 2 min per optimiser step and a code rollout takes 60 min, so code groups arrive with

                                                                                      180 /189
Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier            Aleph Alpha

a staleness of 30. Doubling both the trainer and the inference GPUs at the same batch size halves the step
time to about 1 min and doubles inference throughput, so the loop stays balanced. The decode speed per
request is unchanged, however, so a code rollout still takes 60 min and now arrives with a staleness of
60. In some of our runs, code groups exceeded the staleness limit in this way after we scaled up the run.
Scaling the batch size with the GPU count also keeps the step time, and hence the staleness, constant.

which the group-relative objective drops for low reward variance and whose highest environment reward is

of the turn the student sampled, rescored under the hinted context and the bare context. The hint down-weights a token with ∆ < 0 and

below 0.2, and Table 60 lists its failure classes. Counts come from the rollout traces, where all 24 detectors
score every assistant turn of every training sequence of the 8 OPSD environments. The trainer logs only
the first matching detector on at most five turns per OPSD group, so its logs serve only for the shares of
trained groups and tokens, the teacher agreement and the entropy. Early and late values span the first and
the last 50 steps on the traces, and, to compensate for staleness, steps 25 to 75 and the last 50 steps in
the logs.

Detected Failures Table 61 shows how detections per training sequence fall in 6 of the 8 environments,
with the average decreasing from 4.21 to 2.56. The left of Figure 69 shows that detections per rollout step
also fall, by 31 %, though not monotonically. This can be further improved by better hinting and distillation
strategies that target the remaining hard-to-solve errors. The right of Figure 69 shows how, over the
course of training, errors related to invented tools and references rapidly decrease and eventually are
trained away. In contrast, output or time limits rise, making up 59 % of the late detections. Nearly all of
them are output_truncated, which fires when the harness cuts a long command output, and they
rise in the software-engineering environments, where the curriculum keeps drawing long tasks on large
repositories. Table 61 shows how OPSD-annotated sequences consistently receive lower environment
rewards on average, suggesting that the error detection successfully finds sequences where the model
struggles. Figure 68 visualises the differences between teacher and policy token probabilities for two
exemplary flagged turns, finding that the teacher focuses on the turn’s error in both cases.

in the OPSD environments that become OPSD groups falls from 13.0 % to 7.2 % during training, and how
only 0.10 % to 0.034 % of tokens are trained with the distillation signal. The right of Figure 70 overlays the
